Page 8 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) I. STRATEGIC CONTEXT A. Regional Context 1. The proposed Program provides a comprehensive framework (and not a business as usual approach) to intervene at both the national and regional levels. The Food Systems Resilience Program (FSRP)—referred to in this document as “the MPA,” or “the Program”— aims to both tackle the underlying structural challenges of food insecurity and reduce beneficiaries’ sensitivity to unpredictable climate, crisis, and conflict events. It aims to achieve this by building resilient food production capacity, promoting the sustainable use of natural resources, enhancing food marketing, fostering resilience-focused public policies, and improving regional coordination. In that respect, while the Program can, by design, engage in short-term emergency response activities, it is to a large extent designed to complement emergency response activities and focus on medium- term investments that will help break the cycle of crisis and response. 2. The food systems of Eastern and Southern Africa (AFE) are some of the most vulnerable in the world. AFE is home to over 656 million people, many of whom are extremely poor and face significant challenges accessing adequate, safe, and nutritious food every day. The region’s food systems are generally beset by low levels of agricultural productivity, a severely degraded and stressed natural resource base, pronounced gender inequities in food and resource access, and relatively low levels of food trade and regional market integration. AFE is also among the region’s most affected by fragility, conflict, and violence (FCV) as well as the effects of climate change. Food system shocks, including the ones precipitated by extreme weather, pest and disease outbreaks, political and market instability, and conflict, are generally becoming more frequent and severe, putting more people at risk of being affected by both chronic and acute forms of food insecurity. Shocks to global food and energy systems have the potential to affect food systems in the entire Africa region. Together, the increasing weight of food system shocks in the region and the interdependent nature of the abovementioned challenges underscore the need to enhance regional food systems’ resilience to recover from setbacks. 3. In recent years, the vulnerability of AFE’s food systems has translated into a deteriorating food security situation. Over 40 percent of AFE’s population live on less than US$1.90 purchasing power parity per day, and as of 2019, nearly two-thirds were affected by moderate-to-severe food insecurity, 27 percent of the population severely so.1 In South Sudan, 85 percent of the population was food insecure in 2019 and 82 percent was in Malawi. As a result, with just 8.5 percent of the world population, AFE accounted for one-fifth of all severely food insecure people in the world in 2019 (18 percent of those affected by moderate-to-severe food insecurity), and the situation has been deteriorating. Between the periods 2014–2016 and 2018–2020, the number of severely food insecure people in AFE grew by roughly 22 percent.2 Since 2019, extreme weather, conflict, and the coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic have pushed nearly 50 million additional people into acute food insecurity across Sub-Saharan Africa, with 120 million now affected compared to 73 million in 2019. In AFE specifically, the share of undernourished people was projected to increase from 21 percent in 2019 to 29 percent in 2030 (Figure 1 and Figure 2)—and as of 2020, it had already increased to over 25 percent. In 2018–2020, undernourishment affected over 131 million people in AFE—an estimate that 1 World Bank (World Development Index 2022) poverty data are available for only 17 of 26 AFE countries, and the latest data year ranges from 2015 to 2019. For those countries, the weighted average poverty rate was nearly 43 percent. The 2019 food insecurity data were available for 20 of 26 AFE countries. 2 The State of Food Security and Nutrition in the World (SOFI) 2021. Page 9 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) excludes multiple countries for which data were not available.3 In 2020, the share of stunted children under five ranged from over 23.0 percent in Southern Africa to 32.6 percent in Eastern Africa and 36.8 percent in Central Africa (which includes three AFE countries: the Democratic Republic of Congo, Angola, and São Tomé and Príncipe) 4. Although food insecurity is becoming increasingly widespread, there are clear hot spots in the region where the problem is deepening at a faster rate. An estimated 66.4 million people in AFE are projected to experience food stress or find themselves in a food crisis, emergency, or famine (IPC5 Phase 2+) by July 2022. In the Horn of Africa (HoA) alone, the United Nations (UN) expects some 25 million people to face “high acute food insecurity” by the middle of 2022. Countries like Ethiopia, South Sudan, and Somalia are in IPC Phase 4, denoting an “emergency” situation. 3 The estimate is based on State of Food Security and Nutrition in the World 2020 data from 18 AFE countries. https://www.fao.org/3/cb4474en/cb4474en.pdf. 4 The State of Food Security and Nutrition in the World 2021. 5 IPC = Integrated Food Security Phase Classification. Page 10 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Figure 3. Food Production per Capita in AFE, 2000–2019 6 5 4 3 2 1 0 2000 2001 2002 2003 2004 2005 2006 2007 2008 2009 2010 2011 2012 2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 -1 AFE Trend Growth AFE Growth Volatility Source: Based on FAO and WFP and Global Network against Food Crises data. 5. A major long-term contributor to food insecurity in AFE is the changing climate. Climate change has already increased the frequency and severity of extreme weather events across Sub-Saharan Africa and accelerated the cycle of food production shocks. Across Sub-Saharan Africa, drought- and flood-related shocks to the food system that occurred once every 12.5 years on average between 1982–2006 occurred every 2.5 years between 2007–2016. Whereas there were two years in which per capita food production dropped by more than 2.5 percent in that first quarter century-long period (1983 and 1992, both El Niño-induced drought years), there were four such years in the roughly decade-long period that followed. Agriculture in AFE is particularly vulnerable to drought considering that about 90 percent of crop production is rainfed.6 Climate change also affects pest and disease vectors with the potential to harm crops and animals, and major locust outbreaks have plagued the HoA in recent years. In 2020, more than 90 million ha of crops were destroyed, costing HoA countries an estimated US$9 billion in economic losses. The increased frequency of weather- induced shocks such as these is making it even more challenging to sustain adequate long-term growth in per capita food production. 6. In this context, high returns are expected on investments in climate adaptation. Studies show that investing in climate change adaptation can save more than it costs, factoring in the costs of climate-induced crises, disaster relief, and recovery. Across Sub-Saharan Africa as a whole, spending of US$15 billion on agricultural and food system adaptation could save an estimated US$201 billion annually. Meanwhile, the latest projections indicate that the world is not on track to meet the Paris Climate Agreement target of limiting warming to 1.5 degrees over preindustrial temperatures. A 3-degree trajectory could catastrophically disrupt African food systems within the next 30 years, greatly narrowing the potential for adaptation. 7. Violent conflict has surged since 2010, food and water insecurity being both a consequence and a cause of conflict. AFE harbors some of Africa’s most protracted conflicts, and these have left a number of countries in a fragile state. Increased internal competition for land and water, particularly between pastoralists and crop producers, is a significant source of conflict, such as one evidenced in South Sudan. In turn, physical insecurity dissuades investment and disrupts the distribution systems that move food from surplus to deficit areas, while open conflict destroys infrastructure and other capital stocks essential for income growth. As violence intensifies, accompanying uncertainties cause food production shifts from commercial orientation 6 World Bank. 2020. The African Continental Free Trade Area: Economic and Distributional Effects. Washington, DC: World Bank. Page 11 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) toward more of a subsistence one, due to restrictions in accessing farmland and markets, while hindering regional integration and trade. 8. AFE’s food systems can be affected by shocks to the global food and energy systems. International food, oil, and fertilizer markets have significant influence over African food systems, and the disruption of these commodity markets can affect African food supply and prices to varying degrees, as it did in 2007–2008. Having delivered a major shock to global food, fuel, and fertilizer markets, the war in Ukraine is expected to significantly affect food and nutritional security. The Russian Federation and Ukrainian exports together account for 25–30 percent of the global wheat market, and Russia (and Belarus) are also major exporters of fertilizer. In 2021, Russia was the largest exporter of natural gas in the world and the second-largest exporter of crude oil and condensates. The commodity market and food system impacts of the conflict and its ramifications (sanctions, supply disruptions, and others) were felt immediately. As of March 2022, oil prices were 90 percent higher than they were a year earlier, and wheat prices were 80 percent higher. In fact, wheat prices increased by 50 percent just between early February 2022 and March 2022, and in April they continued climbing, reaching an all-time high. 9. Further trade and supply disruptions could compound existing agricultural commodity price inflation, leading African economies to see the cost of their food and agricultural inputs rise. African countries imported US$4 billion worth of agricultural products from Russia in 2020. About 90 percent of those imports were wheat and 6 percent sunflower oil. In AFE, eight countries get more than 10 percent of their national calories from wheat imports. It is also worth noting that substitution effects (for example, the substitution of palm oil for sunflower oil when the latter’s price rises) may lead more commodities to be affected by the disruptions than those specifically exported by Ukraine and Russia. The timing of these global market disruptions magnifies market volatility and food security concerns since African countries are still recovering from the last season’s historically tight supply and multiyear-record-high grain and oilseed prices. Recent data show that the majority of AFE economies experienced double-digit year-on-year food price inflation (Figure 4). Fertilizer prices, already rising, face increased volatility due to the disruption of supplies and increases in oil prices. Figure 4. Page 12 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 10. Globally, food insecurity affects more women than men, and this gender difference has been exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic. For example, In Rwanda, women were more likely than men to indicate that their household’s food production did not meet their food needs. Across the region, female farmers lacked access to productive assets, input and output markets, and information even before the pandemic began. And in a rapid gender assessment carried out after the pandemic started in 2020, 7 more than two-thirds of women in Malawi indicated facing a decrease in food availability, an increase in food prices, and a decrease in the supply of agriculture inputs. In AFE, the effects of food insecurity on women are dire and frequently come on top of inequalities in access to health care, markets, and income. Meanwhile, the impacts of climate change on food and agriculture have been shown to worsen gender-based violence (GBV). Although women and girls have grown more vulnerable and food insecure over the past years, there have been some notable signs of progress. In South Africa and elsewhere, social assistance targeting women directly has enhanced economic stability and improved their nutritional outcomes by enhancing their agency and control over resources. Experience generally shows that investment in women’s access to markets; their better integration into value chains; and the removal of institutional, legal, and regulatory barriers to their productive activities can all greatly improve women’s capacity to absorb shocks. B. Sectoral and Institutional Context 11. Agricultural productivity in AFE remains low by international standards and has not been the primary driver of sector growth. Cereal yields in Sub-Saharan Africa rose by 38 percent in the 38 years from 1980 to 2018 or roughly half the rate observed in South and Southeast Asia. Over the past several decades, agricultural growth in Sub-Saharan Africa has been owed more to agriculture’s expansion than to its intensification, with studies suggesting that only about one-quarter of growth in crop output has been attributable to yield growth. 12. Agriculture’s expansion has been damaging to the region’s forests, water resources, soil, and biodiversity. In fact, the agricultural sector has been the leading driver of soil degradation, land use change, and forest and biodiversity loss in AFE and the wider region. In recent decades, the rate of deforestation in AFE has largely exceeded the global average. Between 1990 and 2006, while the world lost an average of 0.1 percent of its forests each year, AFE lost an average of 0.3 percent per year. Poor agricultural land management practices have also been harmful to ecosystem services, leading, among other things, to a decline in soil fertility, carbon sequestration, and groundwater recharge and to the degradation of watersheds. Desertification and soil degradation affect about 29 percent of the land area in the Nile Valley and the HoA. Across Sub-Saharan Africa, the native vegetation carbon stocks suppressed by current uses of pasture are equivalent to 113 gigatons of carbon dioxide (GtCO2), more than twice the global emissions of all greenhouse gases (GHGs).8 13. In turn, the conversion of forests and grasslands into cropland and pasture, the degradation of soil and water resources, and the loss of ecosystem services have put downward pressure on agricultural productivity. For example, large volumes of sediment have progressively silted up irrigation systems, lakes, reservoirs, and pastoral watering points, affecting communities’ livelihoods and the productivity of cropland and pasture. In the 10 AFE countries shown in Figure 5, land degradation costs an average of US$108 per person per year or 9 percent of gross domestic product (GDP) in land productivity losses related to a combination of human-induced soil erosion, acidification, nutrient leaching, and compaction. Small-scale farmers suffer the 7 UN Women, United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA), and partners (2020) CATI COVID-19 rapid gender assessments (unpublished data) in Impact of COVID-19 on Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment in East and Southern Africa. Nairobi: UN Women and UNFPA, East and Southern Africa Regional Offices. 8 Based on vegetative cover, this estimate (31 GtCO ) by Hayek et al. (2021) in Nature Research does not account for the loss of soil 2 carbon stores. Page 13 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) most because poor soil conditions, climate and weather variability, land tenure insecurity, and limited access to markets pressure them to make short-term trade-offs that compromise long-term gains. Land degradation reduces options to meet both food demands and environmental needs sustainably. Overall, agriculture has driven the region’s natural resources endowment to decline on a per capita basis, further stressing its food production capacity in a context of rapid demographic growth and climate change. In fact, the interaction of land degradation with climate change represents one of Africa’s biggest and most urgent challenges. Yet these interactions have not been a central focus of adaptation planning. Figure 5. Annual Cost of Land Degradation in Selected AFE Countries (percentage of GDP) Source: Global Mechanism of the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification. 2018. Country Profiles. Investing in Land Degradation Neutrality: Making the Case. An Overview of Indicators and Assessments. Bonn, Germany. 14. Despite these challenging circumstances, there are several reasons to be optimistic about the potential for agriculture-led growth to build up food systems resilience in the region. Together with the complexity of the challenge, these and other emerging opportunities point to the need for an integrated and regional approach. 15. First, the agricultural and food sector remains a significant source of economic growth and job creation in AFE. Agriculture accounted for nearly 15 percent of AFE’s GDP in 2020, and the sector has been growing relatively fast by global standards. During the 2010s, agricultural value added grew by an average of 3.1 percent per year in AFE, compared to 3 percent in East Asia and the Pacific and 2.8 percent in Latin America and the Caribbean. AFE’s agricultural growth rate was only outdone by that of Western and Central Africa, where the sector grew by an average of 3.8 percent annually over the decade. As much as 59 percent of AFE’s population was employed in agriculture as of 2019, and in some countries, even larger shares were: 86 percent in Burundi, 80 percent in Somalia, 76 percent in Malawi, 70 percent in Mozambique, and 66 percent in Ethiopia and Zimbabwe. 16. Second, ongoing shifts in consumption are expected to create agrifood business opportunities. Demand for agrifood products is expected to increase significantly over the coming decades as incomes rise and lifestyles urbanize. By mid-century, food demand in Sub-Saharan Africa at large is expected to increase by 60 percent over 2005–2007 levels, with the population expected to grow by 1 billion and the average economy Page 14 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) expected to grow at 4–5 percent per year. Presently, the food and beverages industry accounts for 38 percent of GDP, and 60 percent of the consumed foods are now processed, packaged, or perishable. By 2050, the industry is expected to see an 800 percent increase in the value of food marketed through rural-to-urban value chains. In addition, trade in processed foods could increase by up to 90 percent.9 17. Third, there is significant potential in AFE to enhance agricultural productivity and climate resilience in the context through modern innovation and related service delivery systems. In AFE and the wider region, there is enormous potential for productivity to increase—by up to two to three times. That potential lies in the adoption of better farm inputs and production technologies, but also the more efficient use of water and soil resources, and the restoration of natural capital and ecosystem services. For example, digital tools for monitoring climate risks can identify the onset of climatic shocks before they happen and facilitate responses for building resilience. Automated irrigation systems, soil sensors, and drones can boost efficiency in production. However, while the returns to research and development (R&D) are consistently found to be very high (40–50 percent), funding for R&D has been limited over the past decades. Furthermore, the ability of farmers to adopt transformative technologies is influenced by their availability and costs, limited advisory services, and incentive structure from policy distortions. 18. Investments in knowledge solutions and the diffusion of climate-smart technologies are needed to close existing productivity gaps in environmentally sustainable and risk-ready ways. Important opportunities exist within AFE to strengthen agricultural innovation systems, reorient agricultural R&D to meet emerging climate challenges, modernize agricultural extension services, and build innovation capacity throughout the food economy. The high level of uncertainty implied by climate change points particularly to the need to build up the capacity, within different parts of the food system, to innovate. At the same time, there is a need to ensure that research and technology development be responsive to farmers’ and other end users’ circumstances and needs, have a path to commercialization and diffusion, and can ultimately reach farmers and be put into use effectively. In that respect, acting at both the national and regional levels can help increase the relevance and reach of innovation. 19. There is significant scope for improving the management of natural capital in the region, thereby strengthening the very foundations of resilient food production and rural livelihoods. Improved soil and water management is critical for food systems resilience. More efficient irrigation can help support agricultural productivity and diversification under a changing climate. Soil is widely and significantly degraded, yet is of critical importance to agricultural productivity, water use efficiency, water quality management, and carbon storage. A multitude of agroecological farming practices involving managed grazing, reduced tillage, cover cropping, permaculture, polyculture, landscape features, and more can contribute to promoting soil health and building soil organic matter and carbon storage capacity. The International Water Management Institute estimates that 29 percent more irrigated land will be required by 2025 to sustain food production and reduce poverty on the African continent. Studies generally point to lower rates of food insecurity in irrigator households compared to non-irrigator ones. Scaled-up investments in irrigation and water storage infrastructure, and watershed or landscape management interventions that promote “natural storage,” are needed to unlock the region’s full agricultural and economic potential. Importantly, the needs of pastoralists also need to be considered in agricultural and water management planning, given their importance to food security and 9It is important to note that a shift toward the consumption of more ultraprocessed food has the potential to create value-added opportunities as well as negatively affect public health in ways that need to be anticipated and managed, especially since the profitability of such foods can contribute to entrenching them in the economy and in consumers’ eating food habits. Page 15 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) livelihoods in dryland areas. Improving the management of natural resources can also help diffuse conflict driven by competition over land and water resources. 20. Efforts to foster a more favorable enabling environment can help boost the region’s small but budding agribusiness sector, contributing to efficiencies in food production and supply, more and better jobs, and a stronger economic footing from which to weather shocks. A vibrant business sector can help develop things like digital platforms and technologies, agricultural advisory services, professional farming and logistics services, farm input and equipment supply, aggregation and coordination services, agrifintech, and more. 21. While trade can also help expand agrifood business opportunities, it can also help stabilize food availability and access across the region’s markets in a context of increasingly frequent and intense climate and other shocks. In that respect, the removal of physical and regulatory barriers to (intraregional) agricultural trade holds particular promise for ensuring food security and food systems resilience, especially under a changing climate. Indeed, trade can help food move from surplus to deficit areas in response to localized harvest failures and other supply disruptions in the short run, and in response to climate-induced shifts in the geography of food production in the longer run. Trade can also lead to a more efficient allocation of resources in agricultural production.10 AFE and the wider region are characterized by relatively low levels of intraregional food trade by international standards, with agricultural trade among African countries accounting for less than 20 percent of the region’s agricultural trade. From that starting point, the further integration of agricultural markets, removal of technical barriers to trade, harmonization of trade modalities (including sanitary and phytosanitary [SPS] standards and controls), and coordination of responses to shocks, under the African Continental Free Trade Agreement (AfCFTA) or other initiatives like trade facilitation platforms11, could all contribute to building food systems resilience. The implementation of the AfCFTA is expected to increase intracontinental trade in agricultural goods by 49 percent and trade in processed food by 90 percent. It is also expected to increase trade between Africa and the rest of the world by 10 percent. If, in the process, it fosters the movement of food to deficit areas in AFE, it could help improve regional food security and resilience. 22. Although many countries in the region face substantial public resource constraints, significant opportunities exist to use public resources more effectively and better align them with food system objectives. In several countries, bloated expenditures on unproductive investments, such as poorly targeted input subsidies that actually undermine resilience, are crowding out more productive investments in agricultural R&D and extension, climate and market information, and other services. Moreover, restrictions on trade in agricultural inputs and outputs are pervasive across the region, and they are often tightened when crisis strikes. The resilience of food systems can be strengthened by repurposing public resources, reforming fiscal and other policies, and enhancing food systems governance and institutions. 23. More generally, the food systems resilience agenda calls for strengthening food systems governance and policy at the national, subnational, and regional levels. Strong public policies and institutions are needed to use and leverage public and private resources more effectively. In that respect, efforts are needed to align resources with evidence-based priorities; coordinate efforts across sectors, agencies, levels of government, and polities; enable knowledge-sharing and learning; and identify and pursue spending synergies. Building food systems resilience at every level, from rural communities to urban agglomerations across AFE, will ultimately 10 Trade also has the potential to help optimize agricultural production and resource allocation from an environmental perspective, provided that special measures are taken to allow it to do this. 11 Ones that help connect, inform, and service buyers and sellers and other stakeholders. Page 16 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) rest on strengthening food systems governance, working with regional organizations, national and subnational ministries, cities, and civil societies. 24. Regional and cross-regional partnerships, coalitions, and investments in public goods are another way of amplifying scarce public resources in support of food systems resilience in AFE. It has been increasingly recognized that some drivers of food insecurity are best addressed through regional approaches. That applies, for example, to the management of cross-boundary natural resources, fragility and conflict, pests, and zoonotic diseases. It is worth noting that in AFE, a large share of land and water degradation plays out in transboundary valleys and watersheds and cannot be effectively addressed without coordinated interventions by upstream and downstream riparian countries. Food crisis prevention and management are also best carried out at a regional level, the latter enabling greater risk diversification and transfer than a national approach. Furthermore, returns to R&D increase with scale.12 For instance, regional and cross-border collaboration in agricultural innovation systems and in the provision of hydrometeorological (“hydromet”) and early warning information to farmers can generate positive technology and knowledge spillovers as lower-capacity countries can learn from leaders to adopt new technologies and build effective forecasting capabilities (including for flood and drought). In sum, AFE needs more regional coordination and thinking to build food systems that support more reliable and sustainable food supply, better jobs, higher incomes, and greater food security. 25. Momentum to address food systems resilience is building at the regional level. In 2019, the African Food Security Leadership Dialogue (AFSLD) was convened in Kigali, bringing together African leaders and development partners and raising the ambition for joint action to solve the region’s food security challenges. Key priorities identified by the AFSLD include food systems’ adaptation to climate change, leveraging science and digital technology, and strengthened collaboration among development partners. It emphasized the need to implement existing agricultural and food security commitments including the African Union (AU) Agenda 2063 and the Malabo Declaration. C. Relevance to Higher-Level Objectives 26. The MPA aligns with key regional World Bank strategies. It supports thematic pillar 2 (“Promoting Trade and Market Integration”) and thematic pillar 4 (“Reinforcing Resilience”) of the 2020 Africa Regional Integration and Cooperation Assistance Strategy Update for FY21–FY23,13 and it directly contributes to the new FCV strategy’s pillars of engagement #1 prevention and #3 transition out of fragility and the World Bank Group (WBG) Climate Change Action Plan 2021–2025. Mainstreaming climate change and addressing climate resilience constitute key priorities in the World Bank’s 2025 climate change targets. The Program is also aligned with the WBG COVID-19 Crisis Response Approach Paper to mitigate the socioeconomic impacts of the COVID-19 crisis; the WBG Gender Strategy (FY16–FY23) on enhancing human development outcomes, improving economic opportunities, and removing barriers to asset ownership; and the Africa Region Gender Action Plan (FY19–FY22). The Climate Change Action Plan outlines a strong commitment to improving the planning and implementation of interventions to more robustly and systematically address climate-related risk through the World Bank’s Green, Resilient, and Inclusive Development approach. Under its Reinforcing Resilience thematic pillar, the World Bank’s Africa Regional Integration Strategy seeks to enhance resilience to shocks (including the drought- 12 World Bank. 2016. The pillars focus on agropastoralism, food security and climate change adaptation and mitigation, conflict and forced displacement, and transboundary waters and natural resource management (NRM). The HoA is among the FCV strategy’s priority regions. Investments in the resilience of the region’s food systems will also advance the World Bank’s commitment to the Next Generation Africa Climate Business Plan (ACBP) for 20 countries. The ACBP aims to implement climate-smart policies and programs designed to scale up integrated landscape approaches on 60 million ha and provide 150 million people with access to impact-based warnings to facilitate adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) by 28 million farmers. 27. The Program is also aligned with key client strategies and builds on extensive existing national-level analytical work by the World Bank and other partners. In particular, the Program is aligned with the AU Agenda 2063, the Malabo Declaration on African Agriculture, the Comprehensive Africa Agriculture Development Program (CAADP), and the Forum for Agricultural Research in Africa (FARA) strategic plans. The Program is developed under the umbrella of the Africa Food Security Leadership Dialogue (AU-WBG-FAO-ADB-IFAD, Kigali, 201914). It builds on strong analytical foundations, including CAADP National Agriculture Investment Plans (NAIPs), which set out a development strategy for countries’ agriculture sectors (all countries), CSA profiles, and investment plans that identify priority technologies and delivery channels to increase climate smartness in the sector. The Program also builds on other World Bank interventions such as the previous East Africa Agricultural Productivity Program (EAPP, P112688) and the Agricultural Productivity Program for Southern Africa (APPSA, P094183), including by scaling up regional collaboration in development of agricultural technologies, facilitating access to regional markets for food commodities and products, and leveraging institutions and experiences of these previous projects. It also builds on the West Africa Food Systems Resilience Project MPA (P172769); the Development Response to Displacement Impacts Project in the HoA (DRDIP, P152822) (Djibouti, Ethiopia, Uganda, and Intergovernmental Authority on Development [IGAD]); the Pastoralism and Stability in the Sahel and Horn of Africa Project (P153713); the Regional Pastoral Livelihoods Resilience Project (P129408); and the Nile Cooperation for Results Project (P130694). It complements the Accelerating Impacts of Consultative Group on International Agricultural Research (CGIAR). Climate Research for Africa (AICCRA) by linking the same set of regional and national actors to the international science frontier from international CGIAR centers. The Program also complements other regional programs seeking to build resilience in the targeted territories, such as the World Bank’s De-risking, Inclusion, and Value Enhancement of Pastoral Economies in the Horn of Africa (DRIVE, P176517) and HoA Regional Groundwater for Resilience Project (P174867) and the ADB’s Multinational Program to Build Resilience for Food and Nutrition Security in the Horn of Africa . 28. The Program plans to proactively convene national-, subregional-, regional-, and international-level stakeholders expected to play important roles in achieving Program objectives. The international partners comprise both humanitarian and development organizations, including the FAO, WFP, and member agencies of the CGIAR. Regional organizations addressing issues such as science and technology, extension, political economy, crisis response, and markets and trade are among the organizations that the Program will convene. The goal is to strengthen participating countries and regional organizations to proactively engage with the international system supporting food security and food systems resilience. At the continent level, the AU acts as the custodian of African countries’ commitments to sustainable, economic, and social development under Agenda 2063. Guided by the AU, the African Union Commission (AUC) elaborates, coordinates, and supports regional economic communities (RECs)15 in promoting economic integration among member states of the Sahel, HoA, Central Africa, West Africa, Eastern Africa, and Southern Africa regions. The MPA will leverage the 14 ADB = Asian Development Bank; IFAD = International Fund for Agricultural Development. 15 AU recognizes eight RECs. Page 18 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) convening power of the AU and its RECs to influence country- and subregion-scale policies and processes. The Program will directly partner with RECs such as the Southern Africa Development Community (SADC), the Common Market for Eastern and Southern Africa (COMESA), the East African Community (EAC), and IGAD; technical agencies such as the Centre for Coordination of Agricultural Research and Development for Southern Africa (CCARDESA), Association for Strengthening Agricultural Research in Eastern and Central Africa (ASARECA), and the Groundwater Management Institute (GMI); and international, regional, and subregional networks like FARA, the Alliance for Food Sovereignty in Africa (AFSA), and the Regional Universities Forum for Capacity Building in Agriculture (RUFORUM). While discussions are held with SADC, ASARECA, EAC, and other regional and technical organizations, Phase 1 will include IGAD and CCARDESA. D. Multiphase Programmatic Approach Rationale for Using a Regional MPA 29. The MPA offers a pragmatic and adaptive approach to addressing food systems resilience regionally. Although their circumstances are diverse, the countries of Eastern and Southern Africa are bound together by shared risk, culture, natural resources, and trade and their food systems exhibit strong interdependencies. Strong motives exist for adopting a regional approach to building food systems resilience across the countries of AFE. Food systems resilience calls for a broad, coherent, and holistic program that will facilitate joint action and cooperation across the region, bridging and coordinating the efforts of multiple sectors and countries. In that respect, the MPA is the approach of choice, one prime reason being that it allows multiple countries to join at different times. 30. By creating a platform for its participants to invest, plan, and learn together, the MPA will allow the region to act on a number of food system challenges and opportunities in more cooperative and effective ways. The MPA will create a framework in which participating countries can more readily take on joint investments in regional public goods like climate and CSA research, agrifood marketing infrastructure, information services, and the capacity of regional institutions involved in food systems governance. In this way, it will also facilitate cooperation on the management of shared and transboundary watersheds and resources, agrifood commerce, pests and pathologies, and food safety. It will encourage the joint planning of responses to shocks, including ones involving trade, data sharing, and food reserves. With its robust learning agenda, 16 the MPA is also set up to facilitate peer-to-peer learning and its “horizontal” nature builds in opportunities to improve the Program at each successive phase. The MPA is set up to learn fast and adapt fast. 31. The fact that these opportunities will only grow with increased Program participation is another reason the MPA is the most suitable approach. The MPA is flexible enough to accommodate the differing needs, priorities, and timelines of a diverse region and thus attract the critical mass of countries needed to address regional aspects of food insecurity and food system vulnerability. By giving participants flexibility to join on their own schedule and terms while establishing a shared purpose and responsive investment framework, not offered by regular investment projects, the MPA is expected to attract strong participation and partnership. 32. The MPA is better suited to the task because it can minimize response times while maintaining a strategic vision and a coherent approach. Striking this balance is critical in light of the region’s circumstances and aspirations. On the one hand, AFE faces a deteriorating and rapidly evolving food security situation, and with several countries in the region classified as “high risk FCV countries,” rapid response situations can and 16 The MPA learning agenda requires the ex-ante identification of learning needs at the regional and country levels. Page 19 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) should be anticipated. On the other hand, countries in the region have an opportunity to break the cycle of shock, response, and fragility by also taking a longer-run perspective and investing in the building blocks of food systems resilience. The MPA is well suited to strike that balance as it establishes a mechanism for rapid response while also providing a longer-run vision and investment framework. Countries that join the MPA are expected to achieve better processing efficiencies than ones initiating new, stand-alone projects. At the same time, the common design elements of the MPA create a cohesive structure for the pursuit of regional food systems resilience. The MPA provides a shared Program Development Objective (PrDO) that serves as a shared space for all participants to approach project design. It defines the goals and priorities that will guide all national and regional investments. And the Program pillars offer an inclusive menu of investment options that are based on the latest thinking on food security and resilient food systems. The “horizontal” nature of the MPA also lends itself to reassessing the balance between rapid response and more structural spending as the situation in the region evolves. 33. The choice of the MPA creates opportunities for enhancing aid effectiveness. The MPA provides a platform for coordinating the efforts of countries, regional organizations such as the AUC, IGAD, and SADC, and global research partnerships including those under the CGIAR. In this way, the MPA is designed to help avoid a fragmented approach to country assistance, avoid the duplication of efforts, and identify potential complementarities and synergies. The choice of the MPA modality also sends a strong signal to other development partners of the World Bank’s commitment to financing food systems resilience in the region and can help mobilize, focus the efforts of other development partners and help attract co-financing. One specific characteristic of the MPA is that, while the financing envelope approved by the Board guarantees the financing only for the first phase of the Program, it indicates a commitment to finance future phases, even if financing for future phases remains subject to the availability of country allocations, IBRD/IDA terms, and countries’ borrowing limits. Figure 6. Map of the AFE FSRP 39. The sequencing of phases under this horizontal MPA will reflect borrowers’ needs, requests, and readiness. As shown in Table 2, Phase 1 of the MPA will cover Ethiopia and Madagascar, two countries that are experiencing food security crises, are keen to improve the resilience of their food systems, and have demonstrated readiness for rapid project preparation as well as the capacity for implementation. They will be joined in the first phase by two regional organizations: IGAD, and CCARDESA. IGAD is an eight-country trade bloc that includes governments from the HoA, the Nile Valley, and the African Great Lakes region. Through its Agriculture and Environment Division (AED), IGAD cochairs with the FAO, the Food Security and Nutrition Working Group (FSNWG), a regional platform that provides early warning information on the food security and nutrition situation and a forum to build consensus on how to address food security and nutrition issues. CCARDESA is a subsidiary organization of SADC mandated with coordinating all matters of agricultural R&D in the SADC region. The two regional organizations will support various aspects of the MPA’s implementation, Page 23 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) including coordination and learning activities, and may see the scope of their involvement and Program funding expand as more countries join the Program. 40. Countries that join the Program will demonstrate their commitment to the PrDO. Participating borrowers will show how the set of activities they select from the program-level “menu of options” contributes to the Program’s overall food systems resilience objective at the national and/or regional levels and to the relevant Program indicators. At the regional level, there are several knowledge gaps that, if filled, would support increased regional integration through policy harmonization, peer-to-peer learning, and improved research coordination. Recognizing the opportunity, the Program will finance learning activities that include assessing (a) regional agrifood trade policies and areas of potential reform that would support food security in times of crisis; (b) climate-related and other emerging threats to food security and how they can be addressed; (c) the quality and relevance of agrometeorological and other data and services and opportunities to enhance regional climate information and early warning systems; (d) the readiness and effectiveness of regional crisis response mechanisms based on common regional criteria on food reserves monitoring, planning, or supply chains; (e) transboundary watershed management arrangements and interventions that would maximize positive spillovers; and (f) opportunities to strengthen regionally strategic food value chains. Findings generated by these and related studies will enable the Program to engage in course correction as needed and maximize returns on its investments. II. PROGRAM DESCRIPTION A. Program Approach 42. The MPA’s goal is to increase the resilience of the region’s food systems, thereby putting all people in the region, including the most vulnerable, on a path to having reliable access to adequate, safe, and nutritious food, while contributing to enhancing rural livelihoods and healthy ecosystems. While priority is given to medium-term investments, the Program also offers short-term support in case of deteriorating food security situation. To this end, the Program is built on five pillars: (a) Responding to a Deteriorating Food Security Situation, (b) (Re-)Building Resilient Agricultural Production Capacity, (c) Supporting the Sustainable Development of Natural Resources for Resilient Agricultural Landscapes, (d) Getting to Market, and (e) Promoting a Greater Focus on Food Systems Resilience in National and Regional Policymaking. The following are some key features of the Program:  It offers a flexible menu of activities for countries to choose from based on their food system characteristics, vulnerabilities, aspirations, and needs in ways that can complement existing operations—at both the national and subnational levels (community, watershed, or local level).  The selected activities will need to contribute to the overall PrDO and should be within the scope of the Program’s results chain.  Participating countries will be encouraged to use a holistic and people-centric approach to project design and implementation. To that end, participating countries can use either existing or new consultation and planning processes, provided they integrate the perspectives of various sectors and agencies (planning, agriculture, environment, water, and so forth) and stakeholders (at national and community levels). Participants are also encouraged to leverage the opportunities for peer-to-peer learning provided by the MPA. Page 25 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566)  All country-level projects under the Program will include an assessment of gender gaps and develop an action plan to address them.  The Program will offer borrowers both IPFs and PforRs (subject to the requirements of these instruments).  The Program will support third-party implementation in conflict areas. B. Program Pillars and Components 43. The MPA is built around five pillars. It also includes contingent emergency response and project management components. Pillar 1: Responding to a Deteriorating Food Security Situation 44. This pillar will provide short-term support to farmers and households to restore basic productive capacity following climate-related shocks. It will also provide support to participating countries in mitigating the impact of crises and price spikes in food, feed, and fertilizers on the most vulnerable populations. The pillar will provide a range of support which the World Bank has considerable experience with. This support may include procuring and distributing agricultural inputs including seeds and fertilizer, vouchers for the purchase of inputs from local markets if available, animal feed, and livestock; clearing and restoring affected areas or on- farm facilities; and facilitating access to fertilizers as short-cycle or rapid food production solutions. Financing for the labor-intensive restoration of infrastructure, engaging crisis-affected populations, will also be made available to borrowers/recipients. The Program will also provide technical assistance for the deployment of this support. Finally, participating countries will be able to either trigger a contingent emergency response, restructure their existing project to include short-term activities under this pillar, or seek additional financing. 45. Pillar 1 will give priority to emergency support for activities that can speed up the recovery of food production. In case of an urgent need for immediate food security measures due to a significant disruption in the supply, the provision of food may be also necessary, either in the context of managing strategic reserves or as a social protection adaptation measure. Such support would be on a short-term basis, jointly with medium- term measures to enhance the resilience of the food systems, ensuring access and affordability to the most vulnerable population and in close coordination with the country’s partners to address the full scope of the demand. Financing of Import of food would require participating countries to also put in place adequate measures to address fiduciary/corruption risk and optimize efficiency including, where necessary, the use of third party monitoring. To the extent that participating countries requesting Bank support for food imports also have food export restrictions in place, there should be a timetable for phasing these out. In case of food imports for strategic reserve purposes, the Program would not expect to exceed a country’s historic stock-to-use ratio and would avoid supporting imports beyond 6-9 months of national consumption. Likewise, any engagement relating to the provision of food assistance would have to comply with the Articles of Agreement of the Bank and the Association and their applicable policies, directives and procedures. Retroactive financing for food expenditures would not be financed under the Program. Phase 1 of this MPA does not include the provision of food. Page 26 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Pillar 2: (Re-)Building Resilient Agricultural Production Capacity 46. Pillar 2 aims to strengthen the resilience of food supply to climate change and other shocks and stressors with a focus on agricultural production and related supporting services.21 Taking a longer-term perspective than Pillar 1, it will build climate resilience and support agricultural producers’ access to quality inputs, technology, and know-how and a suite of upstream and downstream agricultural services. It will also support agricultural research and innovation systems, extension and advisory services, agricultural information systems, the provision and financing of high-quality inputs (such as seed, fertilizer, and equipment) and risk management tools, gender-smart agriculture, quality assurance systems for farm inputs and outputs, productive infrastructure (such as storage facilities, sheds, and barns), and other publicly and privately provided goods and services with a particular focus on resilience. The pillar will support both the development of these services (including through public-private partnerships [PPPs]) as well as farmers’ ability to benefit from them—working not just with farmers but with the full “ecosystem” of stakeholders involved in primary food production. It will give priority to activities with significant potential climate co-benefits such as the development, multiplication, and adoption of climate-adapted crop varieties and animal breeds or the dissemination and adoption of climate- smart agricultural practices. 47. Pillar 2 is organized around two sub-pillars. The first focuses on the development of national and regional information systems, and the second, on developing agricultural technologies and services and delivering them to farmers and other relevant stakeholders. To these ends, the Program will finance the procurement of goods and equipment, training, technical assistance, capacity building, and works. It will also provide grants to pilot or scale up public-private approaches such as fee-based services. Sub-pillar 2.1: Developing National or Regional Agricultural Information Systems 48. Sub-pillar 2.1 will support the development of national and regional agricultural information systems for adaptation planning and resilience building. Well-functioning and widely adopted information systems can play an important role in helping equip agricultural producers, supply chain actors, and service providers for effective climate risk management. The agricultural information systems will enhance producers’ and other decision-makers’ preparedness by equipping them to adapt not only to seasonal variability in weather but also to longer-term trends in climate change. Information systems also allow policymakers to monitor climate trends and assess the current state of rural areas and farming, livelihood sources, where people are vulnerable to or have been affected by different kinds of shocks and provide support accordingly. These circumstances change over time along with shifting weather patterns, market and economic dynamics, or migration patterns and need to be monitored continuously for authorities to target responses swiftly. 49. The agricultural information systems supported by this sub-pillar include ones that provide, often in real time, food price and market data; animal and plant health data (such as pest and animal disease outbreaks and management), soil data, hydromet data (based on real-time weather, water, early warning, and climate information), and agricultural production and food supply data. This sub-pillar may work through regional organizations to support countries develop digital climate advisory services (DCAS) that help farmers adapt and build resilience to weather variability and climate change. Through DCAS, farmers can receive timely and site- specific recommendations on crop cultivars, soil preparation, optimal sowing, fertilization, and harvest time and 21Shocks are understood to be transitory adverse events such as natural disasters like floods, hurricanes, landslides, and acute drought events; crop, livestock, and human disease outbreaks; (armed) conflict; and significant market disruptions. Stressors are understood to be persistent adverse trends, for example, long-term droughts, desertification, and protracted conflict dynamics. Page 27 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) other information that will enable them to better manage agricultural risks and reduce uncertainties that often constrain decision-making. DCAS can also help farmers and agricultural value chain actors match inputs and activities to climate trends, thereby minimizing climatic impacts. Information systems supported by the Program may also relate to more downstream aspects of food systems resilience, such as social protection systems and shock-responsive safety nets for climate adaptation. Sub-pillar 2.2: Developing and Delivering Resilience-Enhancing Technologies and Services 50. Sub-pillar 2.2 will support the development and delivery of climate-smart technologies and services to farmers. Climate-smart technologies will help farmers increase productivity, enhance resilience, and reduce GHG emissions from agricultural practices. Specifically, it will support (a) climate-adapted agricultural technologies, developed and diffused using an innovation systems approach that considers research, commercialization, farming, and other needs and incentives (covering climate-resilient agricultural22 inputs and equipment or machinery as well as digital technologies); 23 (b) farming practices and services that support soil health and water conservation for emissions reduction and carbon sequestration; (c) the modernization of agricultural research and services for soil restoration; (d) public and private plant and animal health and breeding services (multiplication, certification, registration, veterinary services, animal nutrition, and so forth); (e) national and regional institutional capacities to prevent and respond to crop and livestock disease outbreaks; (f) innovative extension and advisory services and delivery models (focusing mainly on climate-friendly agricultural practices, farmer-to-farmer knowledge exchange, women-targeted services designed for inclusion, innovative agricultural media, and digital services); and (g) agricultural risk management tools including, but not limited to, agricultural insurance. To maximize the ownership and adoption of improved agricultural management practices, the Program will promote the development of a farmer field school (FFS) curriculum in which training sessions will be conducted “on farm,” following an experiential learning model based on the demonstration, practice, and application of learning material on farmers’ own plots. 51. Sub-pillar 2.2 will also create a platform modeled on the “One Health” approach, a climate adaptation approach that aims for the simultaneous protection of humans, animals, and the environment from climate change impacts. The One Health approach to climate change adaptation is more cost-effective compared to public and animal health sectors working separately and may significantly contribute to food security with emphasis on animal source foods, extensive livestock systems, environmental sanitation, and steps toward regional and global integrated syndromic surveillance and response systems. In relation to its work with plant and animal health and breeding services , the Program will facilitate the creation of a platform that will convene and coordinate animal, human, and environmental health services; facilitate regional and interagency coordination; and support the delivery and dissemination of knowledge products. Those could include the initial diagnoses and mapping of gaps and resources, and the establishment of a cadre of health system professionals and paraprofessionals that can be mobilized according to regional and country needs. This platform will play a critical role in supporting other activities related to plant and animal health extension services and the rehabilitation of infrastructure and in fostering resource- and knowledge-sharing and synergies across sectors. 22 In addition to crops, this includes livestock, aquaculture, and forestry. 23 The latter may include technologies that take advantage of the region solar resources. The pillar will adopt a watershed or landscape approach to enhance the sustainable and resilient use of natural resources for food systems and livelihoods within priority areas, consistent with the spatial, ecological, and socioeconomic contexts of the participating countries and responding to changing climatic conditions. 53. Pillar 3 is structured around two sub-pillars. The first finances the planning of activities at the local or watershed level including knowledge, information, and institutional capacity building pertinent to the use of natural resources. The second finances investments in sustainable land, water, ecosystem services, and biodiversity management and related knowledge. The pillar will finance technical assistance, analytical and advisory work, consultancies, goods, civil works, operating costs, training, community-led grants and revolving fund schemes, and policy and institutional reforms. Sub-pillar 3.1: Identification and Validation of Interventions at the Local or Watershed Level 54. Sub-pillar 3.1 will support the identification or validation of holistic interventions at the local or watershed level. Using a multisectoral, multiscale, and spatial approach, it will work with communities to identify (a) resilient livelihood opportunities that make sustainable use of natural resources and integrate investments for multiple uses of water and land and (b) “no-regrets” investments to support these opportunities. The identification of livelihood opportunities and related investments will be guided by climate considerations to ensure that they will lead to climate adaptation and mitigation benefits. Depending on the status of existing planning and consultative processes in each country, the Program will either develop such a process or strengthen existing ones to ensure that investments are screened for enhanced resilience to shocks. At both the national and regional levels, the sub-pillar may also support a knowledge base for coordinated action, the prioritization of intervention areas and themes, and the development of specific territorial investment plans. It may also work on the improvement and expansion of hydromet monitoring, weather advisory services, and spatial analysis tools to improve the timely availability and quality of data for decision- making. Support will be available to select targeted areas, strengthen decision support systems and advisory services, and prioritize investments in a chosen location. Page 29 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Sub-pillar 3.2: Investments in Sustainable Natural Resources Management 55. Sub-pillar 3.2 will finance investments that promote the resilient development of water and land resources, including investments identified under Sub-pillar 3.1.24 The sub-pillar will do so with due attention to complementarity with activities under Pillars 1 and 3 or the cross-cutting policy activities supported under Pillar 5. Sub-pillar 3.2 will support (a) regenerative agricultural practices and soil and water conservation measures consistent with sustainable, productive, and CSA (including the promotion and adoption of low and zero tillage or system of rice intensification and other practices to improve drought tolerance and soil water retention capacity or reduce excessive evapotranspiration); (b) stream and groundwater control, including small-scale storage; (c) irrigation development, rehabilitation, and/or improvement; (d) the protection and restoration of natural catchments, riverbanks, and groundwater recharge including through forest restoration, agroforestry, and silvopastoral systems; (e) local-level infrastructure such as multipurpose reservoirs for livestock and drinking water culverts and minor flood protection works; (f) livestock management, including the development of water points including for pastoralists; (g) sustainable natural resource-based livelihoods identified by community groups, such as nontimber forest products, beekeeping, green charcoal, nature based tourism, and so forth; (h) the restoration of ecosystem services including those of wetlands, forest reserves, and oases; (i) activities to improve carbon sequestration, restore land or prevent land degradation, and reduce surface runoffs and soil erosion; (j) investments in sustainable rural energy, including biomass, solar water lifting devices, or other measures to reduce energy use in agricultural production; and (k) conservation of landscape biodiversity or otherwise promotion of CSA. The sub-pillar will include related organization and implementation support for beneficiaries, for the operation and maintenance (O&M) of infrastructure of landscape restoration activities, including through the use of FFS and village committees for managing community revolving funds. 56. The sub-pillar will capture both the adaptation and mitigation opportunities leading to significant climate co-benefits. Opportunities for adaptation lie in improving irrigation services to anticipate changing climate variability and optimize water use, constructing new or rehabilitating existing irrigation infrastructure to enhance resilience to climate change impacts, improving O&M practices to enhance the resilience of water infrastructure to climate change impacts, and restoring ecosystem services through nature-based solutions. Mitigation opportunities lie in improving or restoring watershed functions through activities such as afforestation and protected area management that also restore soil carbon pools; developing guidelines on watershed management and erosion control; developing a long-term watershed conservation and restoration plan that results in sustainable soil aggregation, land restoration, and reforestation in target areas; and investing in water conservation systems that reduce methane emissions. Funding will also be made available for climate co-benefits to be measured through a simple and effective georeferenced monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system designed and implemented to assess the impacts of decisions taken and actions carried out at the landscape level. 57. Pillar 3 will privilege community-led consultation and implementation and “devolved delivery” mechanisms to foster buy-in, inclusion, and social resilience. Examples of such mechanisms include community environment conservation funding—an approach promoted by the International Union for Conservation of Nature and adopted in various East African countries—and local government and community action planning focused on both production and conservation. To improve the quality of services, participating countries may 24In case of natural disasters, the Program will also be able to provide financing short-term response by rehabilitating of damaged water/irrigation infrastructure. Watershed interventions, clearing of canals, riverbank protections, and other small infrastructural works can also support communities in case of natural disasters by generating short-term employment opportunities for unskilled labor, including opportunities specifically dedicated to female unskilled laborers (for example, gabion weaving). Page 30 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) adopt mechanisms such as a field-level leadership approach, a value-driven change management approach that mobilizes staff to become leaders in the ranks of public agencies, and farmer-led approaches. Pillar activities will be deliberate about integrating women’s representation and voices in community-led decision-making about infrastructure and natural resources, applying methods dedicated to applying this principle. 58. Pillar 3 will seek out opportunities to help countries benefit from innovative climate and environmental finance. For example, it may provide readiness and preparatory support to countries interested in accessing dedicated climate funds such as the Climate Investment Funds and the Green Climate Fund and other funds with climate objectives such as the Global Environment Facility, the Global Agriculture and Food Security Program, and various private sources. The Program will attempt to leverage and blend such funds to, for example, pilot or scale up climate information systems, soil carbon management schemes, measures to enhance carbon stores on pasture, and various other CSA innovations and “natural climate solutions” that relate to food production, processing, and marketing. Innovative and results-based initiatives may be utilized for both financing as well as support systems, for instance, using private sector models for farmer-led irrigation development (FLID) or payment-for-watershed services including use of climate and impact funding.
