This pillar aims to improve physical and economic access to sufficient, safe, and nutritious food by improving agrifood producers’ access to domestic and international markets and enhancing marketing infrastructure. The pillar will help agricultural producers and agro-entrepreneurs create and capture more value from their agricultural products by supporting (a) producers’ capacity to participate in domestic and international markets, including by helping them organize and meet market standards, and (b) the development of well-functioning distribution, logistics, other marketing, and quality infrastructure. The latter will include activities to help mitigate the high costs of intraregional food supply as well as losses in food supply, which can variably affect incomes, food availability, and the environmental footprint of food. In these ways, Pillar 4 will Page 31 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) help increase and diversify the sources of farming households’ income, while enhancing domestic and regional food availability. These effects will, in turn, improve farming households’ access to adequate, safe, and nutritious food; help them smooth their consumption; preserve their assets when shocks occur; and accumulate assets. Taking a private sector-led approach, the pillar will develop market opportunities to which farmers and their organizations, especially ones involving women and youth, wish to respond. 60. Value chains to be supported by the Program will be selected by participating countries based on, among other factors, their importance to regional food security and nutrition, the livelihoods of target groups, and their potential to serve domestic markets or promote regional integration. Consideration will be given to potential impacts on export earnings, poverty reduction, and job creation. Priority will also be given to value chains that demonstrate potential to leverage the new AfCFTA as well as preferential trade agreements with higher-income regions. Due attention will also be paid to fostering value chains that are not only competitive but also environmentally sustainable, socially responsible, and resilient to climate-related and other shocks. The pillar will also strongly focus on quality, which is seen as an important means of (a) meeting market requirements in terms of food safety and quality standards, (b) reducing the carbon and environmental footprint of food, and (c) delivering safe and nutritious food to consumers. 61. Pillar 4 is structured around two sub-pillars. The first involves strengthening agrifood value chains in ways that will open new opportunities for rural producers, expand their access to domestic and international markets, and increase food supply in domestic economic and regional markets. The second involves establishing or upgrading agrifood marketing infrastructure in ways that will increase value addition and market connectivity. To support these sub-pillars, the Program will finance analytical and advisory work, technical assistance, capacity building, matching grants, construction and rehabilitation work, and the crowding-in of private investments. Sub-pillar 4.1: Strengthening Agrifood Value Chains 62. The activities under this sub-pillar aim to professionalize value chain stakeholders and build their capacity to serve domestic and international markets (including regional ones). They will contribute to improving physical and economic access to sufficient, safe, and nutritious food. For investments in agricultural value chain development to have a sustainable impact on poverty and food security, climate resilience must be a priority outcome of the Program. Hence, the project will ensure that actors involved in agricultural value chains are aware of climate risks and empowered to manage these risks. To build climate-resilient value chains, the Program will also focus on climate risk assessment, adaptive management of value chain activities, and value chain actors’ capacity to respond to shocks. In addition, the project will support (a) market opportunity assessments and related action planning; (b) value chain-specific policy reforms; (c) the capacity of value chain organizations and collaborative models involving producers, processors, and service providers; (d) producers’ ability to meet domestic and international market requirements; and (e) value chain actors’ access to financial services, paying attention to women’s inclusion. Some of these efforts will contribute to reducing the informality of value chains. 63. To support the assessment of domestic and international market opportunities (including regional ones) and the development of action plans, the sub-pillar will also support a range of analytic efforts. These include (a) the gathering of domestic and international market intelligence that indicates the country’s current position on domestic and international markets and the prospects it faces for growing its market share, (b) in- depth value chain assessments and recommendations for public policies and investments, and (c) market information systems. These analytics will draw on broad stakeholder consultations to identify constraints and Page 32 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) bottlenecks to capturing domestic and international market opportunities and feed into joint action planning by private and public sector actors to capture greater market share. 64. In relation to value chain-specific policy reforms, the Program will finance work on policy reforms relating to price controls, input and output subsidies, marketing boards, and trade facilitation and export promotion programs. The Program will also address fiscal systems’ exposure to climate change-related risks by supporting the development of dedicated policies and guidelines, including ones that establish financial incentives for public investment in projects that strengthen climate resilience. These policy reforms are expected to crowd in private investments in competitive and sustainable value chains by addressing related market and policy failures. Broader cross-cutting policy reforms with systemic or economywide impacts will be supported under Pillar 5. 65. With respect to the capacity of value chain organizations, the sub-pillar will support (a) the establishment of new value chain organizations and collaborative models and the strengthening of existing ones, including, but not limited to, aggregation centers, productive alliances, interprofessional associations, cooperatives, and interest or advocacy groups; (b) improved coordination among value chain actors (including producers, buyers, and service providers); (c) the preparation of climate-smart investments including through business proposal or business plan preparation, activity supervision, or M&E; and (d) investments (through the provision of matching grants). 66. This sub-pillar will work with producers to meet a variety of domestic and international market requirements, including, but not limited to, mandatory and voluntary standards. Examples of the latter include SPS and product quality standards, product traceability requirements, good agricultural practice standards, and organic, environmental, fair trade, and geographic indicator labeling standards. To help producers meet applicable market requirements, the Program will support (a) the analysis of standards and controls applied in domestic and international markets, (b) the adoption of relevant practices, (c) the development of traceability systems including ones that leverage digital technologies, and (d) quality infrastructure (standards and metrology, accreditation capacity, and more). 67. The sub-pillar will facilitate agrifood value chain actors’ access to credit, insurance, and other financial services to enhance resilience. To do this, it will support (a) capacity building and training for agrifood producers to prepare viable business and investment plans; (b) the implementation of those plans; (c) training for financial institutions to increase their involvement in the agrifood sector; (d) the development of value chain-specific (movable) collateral; (e) community-based risk mitigation mechanisms that minimize the need for collateral in lending and enhance women's access to credit; (f) the development of value chain-specific credit information; (g) the development of agricultural insurance products to mitigate against price and climate risk; (h) the development of financial services used in agrifood trade; and (i) the development and deployment of digital financial services to reduce information asymmetry along the value chain and increase financial inclusion. 68. Data being an important enabler of climate-smart approaches to agriculture and innovation, the development of effective data services is an important means of boosting resilience. Many types of data can serve these purposes. Examples of data streams that the MPA will support include the collection and dissemination of data on climate and weather variability, on farms (including their location, soils, and demographics), on the timing and choice of crop plantings, crop health, the price of agricultural inputs and outputs, market-specific supply and demand, weather patterns, pests and pathologies, and water levels. Challenges exist with respect to both the collection of such data as well as its wide dissemination and use in decision-making, the latter resting on the wide availability of decision support tools or advisory services that can Page 33 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) help people take informed action. Disaggregating data by gender, or analyzing through the lens of gender issues, can sometimes reveal and help address the specific needs of rural women. Box 1. Supporting Financial Inclusion of Rural Women Pillar 4 will help identify, design, pilot, and scale up innovative financial solutions that promote women’s equitable participation in food systems along with their resilience to climate change. 25 The opportunity to leverage digital financial services is particularly relevant to women, who often lack access to formal financial services provided through banks and microfinance institutions (MFIs). New technologies such as mobile payments and alternative credit scoring can overcome some traditional barriers to women’s access to finance, while the entry of new players like e-commerce and last-mile distribution companies boasting large agent networks can facilitate the reach of financial services and inputs into underserved rural communities. To this end, Pillar 4 will support financial institutions, e-money providers, and fintech and other emerging players such as e-commerce companies and last-mile distributors to expand women’s access to (a) financing for inputs (such as labor, seeds, fertilizer, pesticides, and tools), (b) insurance for pest and weather risk, and (c) payment products that improve access to input and output markets. The sub-pillar will specifically support women’s adaptation to climate change by helping financial service providers develop and scale financial products and services that enable women to adopt things like CSA practices, more climate-resilient crops, drought-resistant seed and other improved inputs, and risk mitigation solutions like insurance and post-harvest storage solutions. In general, Pillar 4 will strive to identify and support service providers and business models that are able to reach, serve, and employ rural women. “Assisted” e-commerce businesses such as Copia and Kasha are examples of such businesses.26 They offer customers a range of agricultural and household goods, services, and information and employ agents to represent and explain their products, take customer orders, and then aggregate and deliver them, even in remote, rural areas. They employ tens of thousands of agents, reach millions of customers, and are exploring how to embed financial services for their agents and customers alike, leveraging the vast amounts of data they collect. These businesses also present a unique opportunity for learning, innovation, and scale as they operate in multiple AFE countries and have ambitious plans to expand in the region and beyond, with designs on markets in Nigeria, South Asia, and Middle East and North Africa. Sub-pillar 4.2: Upgrading Agrifood Marketing Infrastructure 69. The activities under this sub-pillar aim to establish or strengthen existing marketing infrastructure, including quality infrastructure. The latter are expected to facilitate value creation while lowering the cost of supplying food to consumer markets. This sub-pillar will support (a) the assessment and planning of marketing infrastructure, (b) the construction and rehabilitation of marketing infrastructure, (c) the same of public laboratories and equipment, and (d) the planning and construction of connective infrastructure. In the food supply chain, food storage logistics, infrastructure, and good practices can drastically reduce losses. Examples include the correct use of metal drums and hermetic bags, drying equipment (for grains, fish, or vegetables), and cold storage for fresh produce. Commercial storage facilities associated with formal warehouse receipt systems can offer viable food storage solutions for farmers who cannot afford to build their own facilities. 70. In assessing and planning marketing infrastructure, the sub-pillar will support (a) the preparation of feasibility studies on the construction and rehabilitation of agricultural infrastructure and common services that support value addition, ensure food safety, and reduce food losses (for example, storage, cold chain, logistics, 25 Tailored financial programs for rural women will in part be accompanied by the Women and Rural Livelihoods Program of the Consultative Group to Assist the Poor (CGAP). 26 Copia: https://copia.co.ke/. Kasha: https://kasha.co/ Page 34 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) processing, market infrastructure, and laboratory services);27 (b) the technical design and environmental and social impact assessment of agricultural infrastructure investments incorporating climate considerations; and (c) the analysis of options for efficient asset management and operational modalities, especially in the case of existing public marketing infrastructure leveraging the private sector as much as possible. 71. For the construction and rehabilitation of marketing infrastructure, this sub-pillar will incorporate a mix of public financing and private capital mobilization, following a PPP process, when feasible. This sub-pillar will seek the involvement of the International Finance Corporation (IFC). 72. The sub-pillar will also finance the rehabilitation, construction, and equipping of public laboratories to perform their roles in quality and SPS standard controls, epidemio-surveillance, trade border controls, seed certification, and fertilizer and pesticides quality controls, among others . The sub-pillar will integrate design standards and building codes that account for climate resilience. Other key climate adaptation and mitigation measures will include the adoption of building design and materials with lower embedded GHG emissions, integrating water recycling infrastructure to building designs, thermal protection through green roofs and shading, use of energy efficient mechanical and electrical equipment, and installation of renewable energy sources. The construction or rehabilitation will be based on either a performance assessment and gap analysis of the laboratory with respect to predetermined standards (for example, OIE28 veterinary service analysis) or a call for proposals seeking improvements in laboratory performance with respect to specific standards. 73. In regard to the planning and construction of connective infrastructure, the sub-pillar will finance the building or rehabilitation of infrastructure—notably, feeder roads29 and digital technologies—that facilitates the transportation of agricultural products and the linking of rural producers to market opportunities. With respect to feeder roads, priority will be given to the rehabilitation of roads that small-scale producers, especially rural women, depend on to access agricultural input and output markets. In the case of digital technologies, the sub- pillar will seek to promote the scaling of digital solutions such as marketplace platforms that facilitate small- scale producers’ access to market information, inputs, buyers, storage, and transportation. Pillar 5 aims to promote a greater focus on food systems resilience in policymaking. By elevating and “mainstreaming” this orientation within national governments and regional organizations, Pillar 5 aims to enhance public sector organizations’ overarching capacity to anticipate and respond to various shocks and stressors more effectively. To do this, Pillar 5 will work closely with national government agencies and regional organizations to support high-level policies, initiatives, institutional arrangements, and even budgeting decisions that have cross-cutting relevance to food systems resilience. It is in this high-level and cross-cutting focus that Pillar 5 distinguishes itself from the more thematically or sector-focused policy efforts of Pillars 1, 2, and 3. In Phase 1, the Program will specifically build the capacity of IGAD and CCARDESA, but other organizations such as the AUC and SADC are expected to be involved in future phases. 75. Through coordinated multisectoral approach pertaining to agriculture, water, environment, trade, and other domains, national policies and regional development frameworks will be enhanced with a stronger focus on food systems resilience. Developed at an appropriately high level and within a strategic, holistic, and coherent framework that includes both a short-term and a long-term perspective, this pillar will support policymaking, policy harmonization, and policy implementation efforts. 76. Pillar 5 comprises three sub-pillars. The first involves bringing a food systems resilience focus to public institutions, policy, and spending at the national and regional levels; the second involves building the capacity of national governments to implement such policies; and the third is about supporting regional institutions to lead or facilitate relevant country and transnational initiatives. Pillar 5 will generally engage in these activities by funding analytical and advisory activities, technical assistance, consultative and multistakeholder collaboration processes, knowledge exchange, capacity building, and the procurement of goods and services. Sub-pillar 5.1: Making Food Systems Resilience a Priority in Public Policies and Spending 77. This sub-pillar will support efforts to elevate food systems resilience considerations in public institutions, policies, and spending. Such efforts will involve bringing a food systems resilience focus to development plans, strategies, legal and regulatory frameworks, institutional arrangements, programs, budgets, and other initiatives of national governments and regional organizations. It will also support the development or updating of participating countries’ NAIPs under their CAADP commitment, if required. The sub-pillar may support the development of new, resilience-focused policies, their insertion into existing ones, and the Page 36 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) alignment of public resources with resilience-informed policy priorities. It will specifically support (a) the mainstreaming of food resilience objectives in a country’s strategic vision and priorities; (b) the development of relevant strategies and action plans and other policy documents; (c) efforts to align price and policy incentives in agriculture, natural resources management, trade, and other domains with food resilience objectives (defined at the national or regional level30); (d) the management food stocks or reserves with a focus on building innovative systems for procuring, importing, storing, and monitoring those stocks, while facilitating interagency coordination; (e) cross-sectoral national policy and institutional coordination, including through the development of collaborative multistakeholder platforms and processes; (f) the “insertion” of a resilience focus in M&E frameworks as well as the systematic inclusion of gender-disaggregated data relating to agricultural development activities; (g) the systematic inclusion of gender in strategies and policy documents; and (h) reflect resilience dimensions within information systems developed under Pillar 2 and generally enhance evidence- based policymaking. Efforts to better align public expenditures with resilience-related policy objectives (as per activity c) may involve funding public expenditure reviews (PERs); public expenditure tracking surveys; and other studies that are sector-specific, multisectoral, or sub-sectoral in scope. These can help point to directions of reform by revealing how existing spending patterns may be out of alignment with desired policy objectives— by, for example, disincentivizing the adoption of resilient and climate-friendly technologies and practices. Sub-pillar 5.2: Building Institutional Capacity to Implement Resilience-Focused Policies 78. This sub-pillar will build the capacity of national agencies and institutions to implement resilience- focused policies. It will strengthen human and material resources in agencies that are responsible for formulating and overseeing food systems resilience-focused policies. Given the multisectoral nature of resilience-related policies, this sub-pillar will leverage platforms that enable more effective cross-sectoral and interagency collaboration. It will also be mindful of the incentives, skills, and resources that shape the functioning of public agencies and underpin interagency collaboration. Specific activities could include (a) technical assistance for policy analysis and harmonization through analytical work, needs assessments, and policy dialogue or policy harmonization activities in key areas that affect R&D at the national and regional levels; (b) analytical work on seed production systems, intellectual property rights, operationalization of harmonized seed regulatory system, implementation of biosafety regulations, and similar topics; (c) capacity building for improved institutional administration and performance management systems; and (d) resilience-focused analytical studies, specialized technical assistance, training of national and regional experts, communication, and knowledge management. Sub-pillar 5.3: Supporting Regional Organizations to Build Food Systems Resilience Transnationally 79. This sub-pillar will support the overall capacity and specific efforts of regional organizations to build food systems resilience transnationally. It will provide resources to regional organizations that provide Program-relevant services to countries within AFE. This sub-pillar will (a) strengthen regional R&D and technology dissemination, (b) improve the functionality of agricultural R&D, and (c) support policy efforts relating to regional agrifood trade. 80. Regional R&D and technology dissemination will be strengthened with a focus on climate-smart, gender-informed, and environmentally sustainable solutions. The race to adapt food systems to climate change requires continuous surveillance, investigation, trial, and deployment, and follow-up as the shifting environment for crops and livestock will require new traits to be harnessed and deployed quickly. To the extent 30 For example, as defined by the Malabo/Maputo targets. Page 37 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) possible, the sub-pillar will try to stimulate the development of disruptive agricultural technologies (DATs) useful in food production and marketing and sustainable landscape management. 31 81. In parallel, the sub-pillar will work on improving the functionality of agricultural R&D . Numerous stakeholders work on this challenge at the national, regional, and international levels and need to be involved. Efforts under this sub-pillar may include (a) improving national research systems and the dissemination of improved varieties and technologies across participating countries; (b) promoting regional cooperation around DATs and improved data exchange on agriculture, food security, climate vulnerability, women in food systems, and related topics; and (c) promoting green mechanization, data infrastructure, remote sensing and mapping technologies, precision agriculture, and capacity building of a cadre of national and regional experts to effectively deploy and disseminate this technology. 82. With regard to trade policy, the sub-pillar will support efforts at trade policy harmonization and coordination and other transnational (policy) efforts relating to agrifood trade. The sub-pillar may, for example, provide related analytical support to counterparts and facilitate forums for deliberation and agreement among nations on matters relating to agrifood trade. The sub-pillar will concern itself with policy and regulatory reforms that have the potential to facilitate intraregional trade in raw and processed foods, agricultural inputs, and technologies, and more. When relevant, activities and agreements will be analyzed using a One Health lens, with the aim of increasing trade while reducing the movement of pests, pathogens, and invasive species. Table 7. In addition to financing activities under the five pillars, phases will also provide financing under a Continent Emergency Response Component and expenditures related to program management: Contingent Emergency Response Component (CERC) 84. This Program will finance eligible expenditures in the event of an emergency precipitated by a disaster. Activation of CERC allows funds to be disbursed rapidly to reduce damage to infrastructure, ensure business continuity, and recover more rapidly from a disaster. Following a major disaster, the affected participating country may request that the World Bank channel resources from other MPA pillars into the CERC. As a condition for disbursement, an emergency response manual will be developed for each country, stipulating the fiduciary, safeguards, monitoring, and reporting requirements related to invoking the CERC as well as other coordination and implementation arrangements. Program Management 85. This Program component will finance all aspects of program management. They include equipment and materials, training, compliance with fiduciary, procurement, and Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) requirements, M&E and impact assessment, knowledge management, and communications. At the national level, these activities will be performed by the Project Implementation Units (PIUs). Expected Allocation among Program Pillars and Components 86.
