Program Beneficiaries 87. Direct beneficiaries include farmers (with attention to women and youth), small-scale producers and processors, and agricultural (M)SMEs.32 The Program aims for at least 40 percent of its direct beneficiaries to be women. Additional beneficiaries will include other food system actors such as government line ministries, regional organizations (IGAD, CCARDESA, and others), and other public and private institutions. The results framework presents a breakdown of beneficiaries in each participating Phase 1 country. The Program also aims to reach a large number of indirect beneficiaries spanning the range of food system actors, from producers to consumers. D. Rationale for Bank Involvement and Role of Partners 88. The WBG has unique expertise and experience to support the development and implementation of integrated, multisectoral approaches and tackle food insecurity and malnutrition across the region’s wide variety of contexts. It has extensive experience in agricultural development, sustainable natural resources management, climate change mitigation and adaptation, social protection, innovation and trade policy, and private sector development—including in violence- and conflict-affected countries. For example, past operations in the region and beyond demonstrate the World Bank’s expertise in national and transboundary watershed and landscape management33 and regional agricultural research and productivity enhancement.34 In addition, the World Bank’s various practice groups (including those focused on agriculture and food, environment and natural resources, water and disaster risk management (DRM), finance, competitiveness, and innovation) are already extensively engaged in relevant sector work across the region. Thus, the WBG is well placed to leverage the lessons, experience, relationships, and presence to build this regional program. 89. The WBG is also well placed to make the most of available development resources to build food systems resilience in the region. In particular, it can leverage its broad portfolio of projects as well as its power to convene stakeholders in the region to identify investment synergies that maximize development impact, including ones involving other development partners. It can also draw on the IFC to leverage private sector support. More broadly, the WBG is committed to advancing the Mobilizing Finance for Development (MFD) agenda in several ways. The approach includes targeted public investments in infrastructure and the enabling policy environment necessary to leverage private sector investment by agribusinesses in priority value chains, the leveraging of private sector and trust-funded carbon financing, and the leveraging of IFC and the Multilateral 32 (M)SMEs = (Micro), small, and medium enterprises. 33 For instance, the Ethiopia Sustainable Land Management Project, Madagascar Agriculture Rural Growth and Land Management Project (CASEF), and Karnataka Watershed Development Project–I (KWDP-I). 34 West Africa Agricultural Productivity Program (WAAP, P094084), East Africa Agricultural Productivity Project (EAAPP, P112688), and Agricultural Productivity Program for Southern Africa (APPSA, P164486) projects. Page 40 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Investment Guarantee Agency expertise in public-private collaboration and guarantees to help de-risk prospective commercial investments. E. Lessons Learned 90. The MPA design incorporates several lessons from past and ongoing operations and programs, including the West Africa Food Systems Resilience Program. The Program builds on the recommendations of the 2019 report, “Financing for Food Security: Four Decades of World Bank Support to Respond to Crisis and Prevent Famine.” Specifically, the Program draws on the following insights. 91. First, food systems grow more resilient when public institutions develop the capacity to foresee, prepare for, and act effectively in response to crises. Institutions that have this capacity are better positioned to take early measures to prevent an impending crisis, manage an ongoing crisis, and rebuild to a “better starting point” post crisis. For institutional capacities to work effectively, coordination mechanisms need to be in place that can generate, decipher, and use data in near real time to inform decision processes. Across AFE, several agencies are engaged in seasonal forecasting and monitoring, but a regional coordination mechanism does not exist and the links among the agencies involved remain weak and ad hoc at best. Having multiple data collection efforts also creates duplication and inefficiencies across AFE countries. There is an urgent need to adopt regionally coordinated data collection services that use harmonized indices and are able to generate information based on single- and multi-country requests. Use of modern technologies including geospatial models, and harmonized management information systems (MISs), and digital platforms could better support communication and knowledge exchange among countries. The Program will support institutional coordination efforts to promote the adoption of effective food security information systems. 92. Second, the harmonization of emergency response policies across countries can help build resilience at the regional level by shortening emergency response time and preventing the adoption of counterproductive policies. For example, the regional coordination of policies relating to intraregional food trade can help improve the efficiency and integration of domestic and regional food markets and maintain food trade flows in times of crisis. While agricultural productivity has been a key focus of national policy across the AFE region, only limited efforts exist to coordinate agricultural research capacity at the regional level. Countries in the AFE region are at varying stages of developing laws and regulatory frameworks for generating and sharing agricultural research and technologies. Regional harmonization of these would require a coordinated effort to ensure the participation of relevant stakeholders including private sector ones. 93. Third, increasing agricultural yields and productivity is not sufficient for achieving food systems resilience. As reflected in the regional programs in West, Eastern, and Central Africa, integrating digital extension tools and promoting the adoption of modern technologies are important for supporting access to agronomic, climate, and market information by a greater number of farmers. The Program will promote data analytics and “agricultural intelligence” solutions backed by data infrastructure, remote sensing and mapping technologies, precision agriculture tools, and computing power to enable data-driven decision-making by policymakers, public agencies, and private service providers. 94. The Program’s resilience focus calls for an integrated, multisectoral approach leveraging intertwined perspectives on water, land, agriculture, environment, climate, energy, and technology. There is growing concern that traditional sectoral approaches are inadequate to meet global challenges such as land and water degradation, biodiversity conservation, and food production; achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs); and efficiently contribute to development in Africa. Approaches to date have consistently lacked an Page 41 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) integrated development vision that considers all components of local ecosystems. It is at the watershed level that farmers, foresters, agencies, nongovernmental organizations (NGOs), businesses, and civil society encounter concrete development demands and that land and water management systems have to balance their competing interests. As land and water resources are limited and need to accommodate multiple uses, the Program proposes to promote multifunctional, integrated, and collaborative management solutions. When possible, its processes will build on existing country planning and institutional frameworks. 95. There is a need to ensure strong links across multiple initiatives. Having a positive and durable impact on food security and food systems calls for coordinated interventions across sectors and subsectors. Yet, often even in single-sector projects, coordinated implementation across the various components is not easy to achieve as many stakeholders and at times different implementing agencies (IAs) are involved. Projects on food security and resilient food systems, which necessarily bring together various interrelated initiatives, need to be designed from the outset to systematically build synergies across initiatives to ensure that components complement and build upon one another. 96. The adoption of innovative technologies and practices by farmers is of central importance to increasing agricultural productivity. As agricultural research capacity has improved in the Eastern Africa subregion over the years, a large stock of technologies and innovations has become available to support a potential increase in agricultural production and processing. It is estimated that more innovations are now available in the subregion than were available in India at the start of the Green Revolution. The main challenges now lie in supporting the regional capacity for adaptive research and accelerating the adoption of technologies and innovations by sector stakeholders, especially through an increase in private sector participation and investment, with support for the emergence of SMEs. The Program will work to strengthen regional research capacity and increase adoption, including by facilitating private sector participation. 97. Lessons from the region show that working with both cooperatives of smallholders and private enterprises is key for the development of agricultural value chains. For instance, the First and Second Agricultural Growth Projects (AGP I, P113032 and AGP II, P148591) in Ethiopia supported the establishment of farmers’ associations as part of its commercialization agenda. It did not, however, work with private enterprises and hence was not able to effectively strengthen key agricultural value chains. The experience suggests that a more holistic approach to enterprise and agricultural value chain development is necessary. The Program will continue to support cooperatives and introduce support to small, private commercial businesses. It also recognizes that access to finance is a key bottleneck and will provide technical assistance to help financial institutions provide a greater mix of services to rural enterprises. 98. Investments in agricultural services, input supply systems, and rural infrastructure development should be coupled with support to regulatory services and appropriate use and governance of natural resources. Many projects on irrigation development today are showing good results in terms of increased production and adaptation to climate change. However, much of the increased use of water resources that allows this irrigation development is unregulated, with a real risk, at least in some water scarce contexts, of water depletion. Clearly, infrastructural development needs to be complemented by appropriate evaluations of the cumulative impact of the new and rehabilitated infrastructure on the water resources and by the development of governance systems and the building of related capacity. Page 42 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) III. IMPLEMENTATION ARRANGEMENTS A. Institutional and Implementation Arrangements Country-Level Implementation 99. At the country level, project implementation will be the responsibility of the respective borrowers and conducted through either newly established PIUs or ones already in place. In Ethiopia, for example, project implementation will be handled by the PIU of the Food Systems Resilience Project (P176167) while in Madagascar an independent national PIU will be established. Where needed, country-based implementation structures will be strengthened through the recruitment of additional staff and consultants who will be made responsible for Program management tasks including administration, M&E, communication, procurement, financial management (FM), and safeguards. The latter will include ones relating to GBV and sexual exploitation and abuse and sexual harassment (SEA/SH). In each PIU, experts from the different relevant technical disciplines (such as agronomy, integrated landscape management [ILM], water management, irrigation, and hydrometeorology) will be contracted as needed. 100. Each country will establish a national Steering Committee (SC) to provide policy and project implementation guidance. The committee will meet at least twice a year to, among other actions, review and approve the draft annual work plan and budget (AWPB) and review the annual report and the status of implementation progress. This SC will include representatives of the ministries of agriculture, water, and environment. Each country will also prepare a detailed Project Implementation Manual (PIM) that will incorporate all operational details at the national level including a description of technical and M&E activities as well as administrative, ESF, and fiduciary procedures. 101. For investments at the local level, communities will be involved in the selection of priority activities and validation and implementation of the activities. Investments will be identified, prioritized, and selected through joint-agency (multi-ministerial) visits in the communities. Seeing the different state actors aligned and presenting a unified interface with the communities is also an important element of restoring communities’ trust in the government agencies. To support these efforts, NGOs or facilitators working with local organizations (or both) will be hired, depending on the country context. 102. Given the broad geographic coverage of the proposed Program, the implementation arrangements used by participating countries will necessarily vary. Participating countries facing FCV or natural disaster conditions may need to take advantage of alternative implementation arrangements, particularly if they first need to stabilize the food security situation to enable the longer-term work on food systems resilience. In such cases, participating countries can choose to contract with a third-party implementation agency (TPIA) (for example, a UN agency) to implement their project in part or in whole. Standard output agreements already negotiated between the World Bank and several UN organizations could enable participating governments to sole source contract an agency, which would then report to the PIU as it carries out the contract. In the event of extreme insecurity or fragility in a participating country, full third-party implementation may be explored. In rare cases, the Program will accommodate such an arrangement to the extent that it can help stabilize the system and enable the country to move to a resilience agenda. Page 43 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Regional-Level Implementation 103. The regional activities will be implemented by various regional organizations including, but not limited to, IGAD, CCARDESA, the AUC, ASARECA, and SADC’s Food, Agriculture, and Natural Resources (FANR). Each participating regional organization will have its own Financing Agreement with clear accountability and monitor and coordinate the implementation of its respective part of the Program. These organizations have managed World Bank-funded projects in the past; have the capacity to deal with fiduciary arrangements, including procurement and FM; and have experience with the World Bank’s ESF. These organizations will support cross-country learning, M&E, and analytical work. The World Bank’s Coordinating Role 104. At the World Bank level, the MPA will be monitored by a World Bank team comprising a task team for the overall MPA and country task team leaders. The World Bank team will facilitate coordination among World Bank country task teams and focal points in operational and technical units as needed. It will also monitor the implementation of individual projects and keep World Bank management and the Board of Executive Directors informed. In doing so, the World Bank team will operate as a unified cross-country team to address key issues. It may, for example, help align the implementation of different country-level operations, monitor Program achievements and its use of key indicators, facilitate knowledge exchange and communication, develop harmonized reporting mechanisms and identify gaps in monitoring, report to and coordinate with different partners and stakeholders, and develop adaptive strategies. B. Results Monitoring and Evaluation Arrangements 105. To provide timely and reliable information to facilitate informed decision-making in program management, an M&E framework for the Program has been developed. At the country level, results frameworks will be developed and include both Program- and country-level indicators. The latter will be based on the project activities that participating countries decide to pursue.35 The data will be disaggregated by gender wherever possible. In addition to being an important management tool, the M&E system will be a valuable source of learning and a knowledge management mechanism. 106. The regional partners and national PIUs will be responsible for the internal monitoring of Program outcome and output indicators as defined. Each M&E unit, as well as all key implementing entities, will produce semiannual progress reports along with notes synthesizing information on risks, resilience, and food security at the level of Program beneficiaries. Within each country, the results-based M&E activities will be built on its existing M&E system and arrangements of implementing ministry including knowledge management guided by Program knowledge management strategy and comprehensive M&E manual. The main Program M&E activities include the following: (a) Where applicable, a Program-specific MIS will be established using the client country’s existing online M&E MIS platform which will be linked with the IA’s broader database system. (b) The Program will use the Geo-Enabling Initiative for Monitoring and Supervision (GEMS) KOBO Toolbox to collect real-time and geotagged data for the Program investment mapping and monitoring. GEMS is also useful for establishing a baseline for carbon accounting and understanding changes in carbon sequestration in soils and on land. In conflict-affected areas, 35 Results frameworks have already been developed by Phase 1 countries. Page 44 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) third-party monitoring may be required.36 That information can contribute to assessments of the potential to mobilize climate finance to sustain funding beyond the life of the Program. (c) In each country’s PIM, detailed M&E requirements will be included based on the PrDO and intermediate results indicators to guide the overall M&E system implementation. (d) A baseline survey will be conducted during the onset of program implementation, and additional surveys will be held at the midterm review (MTR) stage and Program completion. External consultants will be recruited to conduct these surveys and evaluations in every participating country and regional organization. MPA participants will be encouraged to share their M&E insights and experience to ensure continuous learning at the Program level. 107. Regional knowledge sharing and networking events will be organized among the Phase 1 countries and regional organizations. These events may involve countries that plan to join subsequent phases. The objectives of regional knowledge and networking events will be to (a) take stock of Program implementation progress and share lessons, (b) disseminate country-specific knowledge and experiences, and (c) promote cross- sectoral and peer-learning. 108. The World Bank will explore additional technical assistance to carry out rigorous IE at the country or Program level. The team will work closely with West Africa MPA colleagues to develop a consistent methodology for measuring food insecurity and explore collaboration with regional or global research partners. Potential focus areas include the food security and resilience impacts of sustainable land and water management practices, climate-smart technology adoption, and regional trade facilitation. This activity will involve setting up a data system to track key market, productivity, and food security indicators at a high frequency to capture responses to climate shocks and evaluate the extent to which Program interventions support the capacities of target communities to absorb these shocks and recover. C. Sustainability Technical and Financial Sustainability 109. All Program interventions will strive to have a lasting impact. Under Pillar 2, for example, the Program seeks to involve private providers in the delivery of technical services through self-sufficient business models. Moreover, research systems will be consolidated to operate under a model that is not dependent on project funding. Under Pillar 3, community engagement in meaningful participatory planning will reinforce community ownership of Program activities. Under Pillar 4, the Program will maximize private sector leadership by following whenever possible a competitive PPP process and involving private partners in collaboration with the IFC, and relying on existing commercial structures (for example, financial institutions) to deliver support. Under Pillar 5, the regional research systems will be consolidated to operate under a model that is not dependent on project funding. Institutional Sustainability 110. The Program aims to work with and build on the current institutional ecosystem already working to build food systems resilience. At the regional level, activities dedicated to capacity building will further enhance the Program’s sustainability by strengthening human and material resources in agencies that are responsible 36 See Annex 1 for details about the use of third-party monitoring in the Tigray region. Page 45 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) for formulating and overseeing food policies with a resilience focus. At the subnational level, the strong engagement of the different sectoral institutions (agriculture, water, and environment) in the preparation and implementation of specific investments will further strengthen Program ownership and, in turn, institutional sustainability of the food-systems-based approach. 111. The medium-term horizon of the Program will help generate buy-in for structural approaches and build institutional capacity and capital. Studies consistently indicate that regional institutions play a key role in regional food systems. To play that role well and meet the challenges that lie ahead, regional institutions— specifically RECs and other regional technical organizations—must enhance their business models, make strategic capacity-building investments (for instance, in digitalization), and transition toward financial sustainability. Earlier project-centric approaches of development partners led to fluctuating institutional capacity and limited institutional capital accumulation. The Program provides the continuity required to build sustained regional capacity. IV. PROGRAM APPRAISAL SUMMARY A. Technical, Economic, and Financial Analysis 112. Economic analysis results and the justification for public provisioning and financing are strong. The economic analysis, which was carried out for Phase 1 of the MPA, considers the incremental benefits of (a) improvements in input supply, postharvest handling services, and agribusiness services; (b) the accelerated adoption of improved agricultural technologies; (c) increased agricultural productivity, sales, and income of producers and processors; (d) enhanced food and nutritional security; (e) increased job creation and youth employment; (f) benefits from green, grey, and marketing infrastructure; (g) enhanced climate resilience across landscapes using ILM practices and technologies; (h) the broad use of CSA practices; and (i) the economic integration of local and regional agricultural markets. Based on total economic values costing US$873.07 million37 and a social discount rate of 6 percent, Phase 1 is expected to have a net present value (NPV) of US$1,131.85 million and an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 25.8 percent. These figures do not reflect GHG sequestration benefits or the shadow price of carbon (SPC). 113. Sensitivity analysis demonstrates that Phase 1 can absorb substantial negative impacts , relating, for example, to delays in the start of project benefits, increases in costs, decreases in benefits, or an increase in the discount rate, and still generate an EIRR above the 6 percent discount rate. Benefits or costs would have to double and operating costs and maintenance increase fourfold for Phase 1 to register a negative return. Adding the SPC to the analysis increases the EIRR to 27.9, 40.4, 72.9 and 52.1 percent, using the World Bank’s market, low, and high and average SPCs, respectively, while the contribution of carbon sequestration varies from 6.8 to 31.5 to 47.8 percent of overall benefits, respectively. 114. With respect to development impact and poverty reduction, Phase 1 of the MPA makes significant efforts to address the issues of food and nutritional security and the resilience of food systems. The analysis is based on an assessment of how Program investments will improve agricultural productivity and value addition, improve natural resources management, develop more resilient livelihoods activities, reduce food insecurity, and achieve reductions in poverty. The Program’s expected results are driven by investments in agricultural landscapes, watersheds, and specific agrifood value chains, the latter being expected to lead, among other things, to increased agricultural productivity and sales, higher incomes for agricultural producers and 37 Economic values exclude taxes. Page 46 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) processors, improved market access, greater capacity of institutions and value chain actors, the generation and dissemination of climate-resilient technology, improved food access, and lower food prices on local and regional markets. B. Rationale for Public Sector Provisioning/Financing 115. The Program’s core role lies in providing public goods across national borders and addressing market failures. At the same time, it will seek to leverage private investments from farm households, agribusinesses, and climate finance for securing results-based investment beyond the Program duration. (a) Reduction of environmental externalities in transboundary watersheds. The Program targets transboundary watersheds where downstream users are considerably affected by upstream activities (for example, land degradation and water pollution). The low probability of investments in the long-term provision of environmental services under market conditions justifies public investments. In addition, climate mitigation benefits constitute a clear public good. (b) Climate data and early warning services. These are widely considered to be public goods. Public provision thereof would create opportunities for the private sector to deliver a wide range of value-added information services for enhancing end users’ climate resilience. (c) Strengthening regional integration and collaboration. By harmonizing the regional policy and regulatory framework, the Program will facilitate value chain development. (d) Investments in rural infrastructure. They are undersupplied due to both coordination and market failures. While the initial investments are typically justified as publicly financed, special attention will be paid to the O&M costs and efforts. C. Fiduciary (i) Financial Management 116. An FM assessment of the national and regional implementing entities has been conducted following the World Bank’s IPF directive. The objective of the assessment was to determine whether the FM arrangements of the respective implementing entities are adequate to ensure that (a) Program funds will be used for intended purposes efficiently and economically; (b) Program financial reports will be prepared accurately, reliably, and on time; (c) Program assets will be safeguarded; and (d) the Program is subjected to a satisfactory auditing process. 117. The FM arrangements of the project build on existing arrangements and are able to meet project- specific requirements. The fiduciary responsibilities of the project will be managed by the national and regional entities which are already established38 and have experience in managing World Bank-financed operations. The project will have an AWPB which will be approved by the World Bank and country/institutional systems. Accounting policies and procedures will be guided by existing procedures for institutions and projects and will have systems in place to produce timely and reliable financial reports. The project will have quarterly interim financial reports (IFRs) for national and regional implementers to be submitted to the World Bank within deadlines set out in each project participating recipient’s DFIL and audited financial statements within six months of the year end. Funds will flow into Designated Accounts to be opened, from which transfer to local 38 Ethiopia - the PIUs of AGP II; Madagascar - the PIU of the PADAP (P154698); at the regional level - IGAD and CCARDESA. Page 47 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) currency accounts will be made for decentralized operations. Detailed disbursement arrangements will be provided in the Disbursement and Financial Information Letters (DFILs). Detailed FM arrangements for Phase 1 are discussed in Annex 7. 118. FM assessments reviewed the critical FM arrangements to identify risks and mitigating measures. The project will inherit the various strengths of country’s/institution’s public financial management systems and World Bank-financed projects. However, given the decentralized nature of the project involving institutions at subnational level, delay in preparation of the AWPB which in turn affects disbursement, turnover of finance staff, internal control gaps, and delay in submission of quarterly IFRs and annual audit reports are risks identified. Several measures need to be taken to mitigate fiduciary risk to the extent possible. Specific FM action plans will be prepared for participating countries and institutions but in general, the proposed mitigation measures for FM risks identified include (a) updating the FM manuals of procedures and PIMs to reflect the specificities of this new program; (b) agreeing upon the IFR format for the program’s quarterly and semiannual reports; (c) submitting for World Bank no-objection the terms of reference (TOR) for, and subsequently recruit and maintain, external auditors; (d) strengthening FM arrangements by recruiting finance specialists and internal auditors as identified in FM assessments; and (e) allocating adequate budget for capacity building and internal audit reviews. (ii) Procurement 119. Procurement under the proposed Program will be carried out in accordance with the following World Bank procedures: The World Bank Procurement Regulations for IPF Borrowers, fourth edition, dated November 2020; “Guidelines on Preventing and Combating Fraud and Corruption in Projects Financed by IBRD Loans and IDA Credits and Grants,” dated October 15, 2006 and revised in January, 2011 and as of July 1, 2016; and other provisions stipulated in the Financing Agreements, using the Standard Procurement Documents (SPDs) accompanying the Procurement Regulations. 120. All procuring entities as well as bidders and service providers (namely, suppliers, contractors, and consultants) shall observe the highest standard of ethics during the procurement and execution of contracts financed under the Program. They will do so in accordance with paragraph 3.32 and Annex 13 of the Procurement Regulations. When procurement is done in the national market, as agreed in the Procurement Plan, the country’s own procurement procedures may be used with the requirements set forth or referred to in paragraphs 5.3 to 5.6 of the National Procurement Procedures. The bidding documents for all works procurement from the national market shall be prepared based on the model SPD, requests for bids, small works, and One Envelope bidding process, March 2021, as customized to the client’s context. 121. Project Procurement Strategies for Development and Procurement Plans. All FSRP IAs of Phase 1 prepared Project Procurement Strategies for Development (PPSDs) which were reviewed and cleared by the World Bank. The PPSDs set out the procurement arrangement and market approach options both for high- value/high-risk and low-value/low-risk project procurement activities. The PPSDs incorporated Procurement Plan for the first 18 months of the Program and shall be updated, at least annually, or as required, to reflect changes in the procurement arrangement, procurement activities, market conditions, and procurement environment. 122. Procurement capacity assessments. The World Bank team in the respective countries carried out procurement capacity assessment in accordance with the World Bank Procurement Risk Assessment and Management System (PRAMS). Based on the assessment, the client’s capacity in each country is determined to Page 48 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) be adequate to implement procurement in the project with overall procurement risk of “Substantial” with due consideration of risks and risk mitigation measures provided in Annex 8 of the PAD. 123. Systematic Tracking of Exchanges in Procurement. The World Bank’s Systematic Tracking of Exchanges in Procurement (STEP) system will be used to prepare, clear, track, and update procurement plans and conduct all procurement transactions for all IAs of the Program. Procurement staff of the IAs who are not familiar with STEP will be trained by the World Bank STEP champions. . D. Legal Operational Policies . Triggered? Projects on International Waterways OP 7.50 Yes Projects in Disputed Areas OP 7.60 No . E. Environmental and Social 124. The overall environment and social risk of the MPA is rated High. The MPA will have significant positive environmental and social outcomes as it will finance improvements in water resource management, soil conservation, sustainable livelihoods, improved market access, and various forms of resilience. However, there are also various environmental, social, health, and safety (ESHS) risks that need to be properly managed. 125. The environmental risk of the Program is rated Substantial. Activities to be financed under Pillars 1 and 2 could lead to an increased demand for agrochemicals and will require management of wastes that may be generated from animal healthcare services. The Program will also finance the rehabilitation and construction of small-scale irrigation (SSI) schemes (Pillar 3) and the construction and rehabilitation of market infrastructure (Pillar 4) for value addition, food safety, and reduced food loss and waste (for example, storage, cold chain, processing, and marketing). The latter can result in various environmental, health, and safety risks and impacts such as (a) the inappropriate use and disposal of agrochemicals including pesticides as well as agricultural research laboratory chemicals; (b) health and safety risks and impacts during construction works and agricultural research laboratory activities; (c) the inappropriate use of water resources through overuse and agrochemical contamination, which can affect water quantity and quality in neighboring communities and downstream; (d) the physical and chemical degradation of soils due to unsuitable land management techniques; (e) impacts on biodiversity and ecosystems of agricultural activities including agricultural water usage and pollution and the introduction of invasive species; and (f) air, construction, waste, noise, water, and other environmental pollution linked to the construction of small-scale infrastructure such as storage facilities and SSI schemes, although environmental pollution is expected to be site specific and small in scale in the context of the Program. 126. Agricultural activities produce GHG emissions including methane, nitrous oxide, and carbon dioxide at different stages in the production cycle. However, the activities to be financed by the Program are expected to be community-driven development (CDD) type activities and hence no significant emissions are expected. The small irrigation schemes to be financed by the Program will meet the World Bank’s requirements for small dams. The Program will be implemented in existing agricultural lands and hence will not lead to conversion of natural Page 49 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) habitats. Considering the nature of the activities to be financed by the Program, the environmental risk of the Program is rated Substantial at this stage. 127. The Program’s social risk level is considered High. The intended beneficiaries of the proposed Program are expected to benefit through increased food systems resilience and ability to respond to shocks. The MPA will also result in the creation of job opportunities (including for youth and women), increased agricultural yields, increased access to diverse food and nutrition, improved livelihoods, improved access to markets, improved access to finance, and an improved skill and knowledge base. The social risks of the Program are considered high due to the nature and scope of the proposed activities as well as the range of contextual risks that exist across AFE (those risks including conflict, social tensions around rights to land and natural resources, and the risk of exclusion). 128. Pillars 1, 2, 3, and 4 include various types of civil work that could lead to land acquisition and possibly involuntary resettlement. Under those pillars, the Program may invest in agricultural infrastructure, SSI schemes, small dams, water points, market infrastructure, and roads. Social risks related to involuntary resettlement include physical resettlement, the loss of land or access to land, the loss of access to natural resources, economic displacement, and the loss of other assets (such as crops, structures, and water points) at the individual, household, or community levels. Affected people are also at risk of exclusion (especially vulnerable groups such as women, people living with disabilities, and the elderly), inadequate consultation and engagement, a lack of compensation at replacement cost, and a lack of access to grievance redress mechanisms (GRMs). Investments may also be implemented in areas where Indigenous Peoples/Sub-Saharan African Historically Underserved Traditional Local Communities (IP/SSAHUTLC) are present. Members of these communities may reside in or have collective attachment to land in Program areas. These realities will need to be considered in the planning and implementation of investments, and free prior and informed consent may be needed for activities on such land, which may be complicated to achieve. Program activities may also create or exacerbate existing conflicts or tensions between groups over the use and rights to land (such as pasture and cropland), natural resources (water points, dams), and access to project benefits, which will need to be considered in project planning and implementation through engagement strategies and, if needed, security management planning. The livelihood strategies of IP/SSAHUTLC as well as other disadvantaged and vulnerable groups will need to be considered when designing project activities to ensure their inclusion in decision-making and access to benefits. 129. Other social risks are associated with not only civil work but also other Program activities. For example, social risks may be associated with the provision of extension services and the capacity building of farmers and other beneficiaries. These risks include (a) insufficient community and other stakeholder engagement (including the potential exclusion of vulnerable individuals or groups); (b) elite capture of project benefits; (c) the exclusion of vulnerable groups or individuals from project benefits due to poorly designed, disseminated, or nontransparent beneficiary selection processes or eligibility criteria; (d) social tension and conflict induced by competition over agricultural resources including irrigation water and by ongoing contextual security risks in conflict-affected areas; (e) labor influx and associated risks including community health and safety and transmission of diseases; (f) SEA/SH and other forms of GBV as a result of either labor influx or changes in power dynamics in homes among men and women; (g) failure to comply with labor standards, including due to the use of child labor (a known risk in the agricultural sector); (h) operational concerns due to remoteness and insecurity, namely in relation to the monitoring and supervision of social risks and grievance management; and (i) weak implementation capacity, especially at the grassroots level characterized by limited functional structures and Page 50 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) trained manpower. The propagation of COVID-19 during the implementation of Program activities has also been identified as a cross-cutting risk. 130. Technical assistance activities at the country level are classified as “Type 2 supporting the formulation of policies, programs, plans, strategies or legal frameworks” and “Type 3 capacity building activities” as defined in the Operations Environmental and Social Review Committee’s Advisory Note Technical Assistance and the Environmental and Social Framework. Given the likely nature of the technical assistance, significant environmental and social risks and impacts are not anticipated. However, technical assistance will need to be undertaken in line with the requirements of the ESF including all TORs and outputs. 131. To identify and manage the potential ESHS risks, the relevant national Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) (or implementing agency) will prepare the required environmental and social instruments. Environmental and social instruments to be prepared include (a) a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) and (b) an Environmental and Social Commitment Plan (ESCP). Additional instruments will also be prepared as required depending on the proposed activities. Those may include, among others, (a) an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) to screen project activities and guide the development of site-specific instruments; (b) an Integrated Pest Management Plan (IPMP) to mitigate potential risks and impacts associated with the application of pesticides; (c) a resettlement policy framework (RPF) to guide the development of site-specific resettlement action plans (RAPs); (d) an IP/SSAHUTLC framework or plan; (e) Labor Management Procedures (LMP); (g) SEA/SH action plans as part of the ESMF; and (h) Security Management Plans included in the ESMF. These plans will include any client capacity building requirements needed to enable the management of identified environmental and social risks. The full set of ESF instruments were disclosed: (i) for Ethiopia, by the World Bank on April 24, 2022 and in country on April 26, 2022 (ii) for IGAD by the World Bank on April 24, 2022 and in country on April 28, 2022; and (iii) for CCARDESA by the World Bank on April 24, 2022 and in country on April 28, 2002. For Madagascar, the SEP and ESCP were disclosed on April 24, 2022 by the World Bank and April 25, 2022 in country, and the preparation, adoption and disclosure of the remaining documents is a condition of disbursement as stated in the ESCP. 132. Furthermore, site-specific risk management instruments and plans will be prepared during project implementation, following the requirements of the framework documents. Those instruments and plans will include Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs), Environmental and Social Impact Assessments (ESIAs), Social Development Plans (SDPs), and RAPs, depending on the nature and scope of project activities selected by the participating countries. Associated facilities, if any, will be clearly identified during project preparation at the national level and the ESMF will also cover the potential risks and mitigation measures of the associated facilities. 133. Regional entities such as IGAD and CCARDESA will implement technical assistance activities associated with environmental and social risks. The aim is to build resilience through technical assistance, capacity building, and institutional strengthening activities that will help enhance the ability of selected entities and communities to prepare for, respond to, and develop food systems resilience. Direct environmental and social risks in a defined physical footprint are not expected. Induced impacts may occur, although, given the nature of the proposed technical assistance, these are not expected to be significant. The Program will ensure that consultancies, studies, capacity building, training, and any other technical assistance activities are carried out in accordance with the relevant requirements of the ESF. Any outputs from technical assistance activities shall also be consistent with the ESF. Risks associated with the implementation of technical assistance by participating regional entities relate to inclusion, ensuring the requirements of the ESF are fully reflected in technical assistance activities, access to information, consideration of vulnerable groups, contextual issues (for example, drought, climate change, and Page 51 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) overexploitation of natural resources), and cumulative impacts. To address these risks, the regional entities will prepare (a) SEPs and (b) ESCPs. 134. In Ethiopia, the project triggers the World Bank Policy on “Projects on International Waterways” (OP/BP 7.50) because some of the proposed investments will take place in the Tekeze, Abay, Baro-Akobo, Omo- Ghibe, Genale-Dawa, Wabi-Shebelle, Awash, and the Rift Valley Lakes basins, which are shared by Djibouti, Egypt, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia, South Sudan, and Sudan and hence are international waterways as defined by the policy. All riparian countries were notified on August 21, 2021, and no objections were received. The requirements of OP/BP 7.50 have therefore been met. The process was approved by the Regional Vice President on March 7, 2022. F. Gender 135. Each phase of the Program will include an analysis of entrenched gender gaps in the food system, at both the country and regional levels. All Program pillars include, in their menu of activities, specific options for addressing the many relevant gender gaps that have been identified. Three specific gender gaps are singled out as ones the Program commits to addressing: (a) access to finance, (b) access to productive resources and tools, and (c) representation at the communal level and within national agricultural policies. Barriers to women’s participation as well as more specific issues that may occur at different levels of the food systems, and in particular subsectors have been outlined in a separate technical annex that is available on file. These analyses will be incorporated in each participant’s Project Implementation Manual. The Program’s success in closing gender gaps will be measured both by a dedicated indicator in the Results Framework and by the gender disaggregation of other indicators. Projects will systematically report on the specific activities they are undertaking to address identified gender gaps. G. Citizen Engagement 136. Designed to be beneficiary oriented, the Program will use as many as three citizen engagement (CE) approaches. The first CE approach involves consultation, encompassing all beneficiaries during the Program life cycle (preparation, ESF, implementation, and closing). Consultation activities would be based on countries’ specific objectives, and it would be regularly conveyed to beneficiaries how the feedback was considered. The second CE approach is grievance redress. The Program will establish effective and efficient GRMs at the project level with the capacity to receive and respond on time to grievances at various levels. The third CE approach considered involves collecting, recording, and reporting on inputs received from beneficiaries through beneficiary satisfaction surveys. In addition, the Program would explore a “thick CE approach” that provides citizens and communities with resources and decision-making powers with respect to the O&M of water and other community infrastructure, equipment, or landscape restoration by involving water users’ associations (WUAs) and farmer-based organizations (FBOs). The envisioned beneficiary feedback indicators for the results framework would be “beneficiaries satisfied with the Program’s interventions” and “percentage of GRM claims addressed among those received.” The SEP will elaborate in detail on the proposed CE approaches. H. Mobilizing Finance for Development 137. The MPA design recognizes the key role of the private sector in achieving food systems resilience as well at its potential to leverage finance, expertise, and innovative solutions to support sustainable growth. The Program foresees a spectrum of activities that will enhance the policy and regulatory environment for private investment. From the MFD perspective, important enabling activities to be undertaken by the Program will Page 52 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) include (a) developing regulations for SPS services, harmonizing phytosanitary data management, and forecasting systems along with certified laboratories to improve regional trade; (b) creating incentives and providing matching grants that encourage the private sector to provide some of the services whose provision is currently dominated by the public sector; (c) strengthening regional research capacity and increasing the adoption of innovative technologies with more private sector participation; (d) facilitating public-private dialogue and studies that help catalyze policy reforms and foster a business-friendly climate; (e) working with commercial banks and MFIs to expand access to credit, while also supporting the preparation of business and investment plans; and (f) constructing and rehabilitating market infrastructure that supports value addition, food safety, and reductions in food loss and waste (including investments in storage, cold chain, processing, and marketing). In these and other ways, the Program is expected to mobilize private sector investment and participation in the region’s agrifood value chains. V. GRIEVANCE REDRESS SERVICES 138. Communities and individuals who believe that they are adversely affected by a World Bank-supported project may submit complaints to existing project-level GRMs or the World Bank’s Grievance Redress Service (GRS). The GRS ensures that complaints received are promptly reviewed in order to address project-related concerns. Project-affected communities and individuals may submit their complaint to the World Bank’s independent Inspection Panel which determines whether harm occurred, or could occur, as a result of WB non- compliance with its policies and procedures. Complaints may be submitted at any time after concerns have been brought directly to the World Bank’s attention, and Bank Management has been given an opportunity to respond. For information on how to submit complaints to the World Bank’s corporate GRS, please visit http://www.worldbank.org/en/projects-operations/products-and-services/grievance-redress-service. For information on how to submit complaints to the World Bank Inspection Panel, please visit www.inspectionpanel.org. VI. KEY RISKS Key Program Risks 139. Overall program risk is rated Substantial after mitigation measures. The Program risk reflects the aggregation of project risks in AFE, which is one of Africa’s most fragile subregions where 22.5 million people currently face severe food insecurity and 10.8 million people experience forced displacement and armed conflict. To manage the residual risk the Program will, among others, (a) provide technical assistance and a standard technical design with quality assurance elements to help country teams in project preparation and implementation; (b) use emergency procedures, such as direct contracting, expedited contract approvals, and use of specialized UN agencies for better procurement when needed; and (c) manage a learning agenda to raise the quality of implementation across all participating countries. 140. Political, governance, and security risks are High. Phase 1 countries, Ethiopia and Madagascar, and the preliminary set of countries (Angola, Eswatini, Kenya, Sudan, South Sudan, Somalia, Tanzania, Zambia, and others) that may participate in the later phases, are at different stages of development, ranging from fragile and conflict-affected states to low- and middle-income countries, all with different political and governance-related challenges. Program implementation will take place in some areas that are characterized by FCV, where security risks are high. Activities that require field visits and work may be affected in some countries by the rapidly changing security situation. To mitigate these risks, and with a view to systematically enhancing real-time access Page 53 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) to the project’s implementation areas, the team will use and make available to borrowers the tools and technologies enabled by GEMS used in FCV-affected areas. As a risk mitigation strategy, the World Bank team and borrowers will work closely with local partners that have regular and safe access to the project implementation areas. The team is mindful about the general election calendar for the participating countries. In 2021, elections took place in Ethiopia, and they will take place in Somalia in 2022. The team will work with the current government officials to ensure continuity of efforts with the new administrations. 141. Risk related to Sector Strategies and Policies is rated Substantial. The sectoral strategies and policies for agriculture and trade in participating countries are broadly aligned with the Program’s objectives, yet critical reforms should be initiated in some countries to ensure the sustainability of program activities, including the approval of NAIPs/NAFSIPs. Through its national and regional implementation arrangements (particularly the establishment of governance bodies with inclusive representation of key stakeholders, such as the Regional Coordination Committee), the Program will serve as a coordination platform that is expected to consolidate regional cooperation in agriculture and food security. Furthermore, the Program will directly intervene in both national and regional sectoral strategies and institutional capacity building related to agriculture, natural resources management, and food system resilience, and will support the generation of knowledge and data to further develop and consolidate evidenced-based strategies. Despite these mitigation measures, the current global food security crisis and potential future shocks may influence participating countries’ sector strategies and policies, and risk therefore remains substantial. 142. Macroeconomic risks are Substantial. The COVID-19 pandemic has affected economies in the region, and the economic outlook remains uncertain and contingent on the course of the pandemic. If the pandemic continues, the consequent macroeconomic effects may undermine the achievement of the PrDO, especially if they lead to a further tightening of fiscal space and reduced availability of counterpart funds or an increase in the price of crucial imported inputs. As a mitigating measure for these risks, price contingencies were accounted for in the Program’s costing. At the same time, the region’s fragility and exposure to climate impacts pose risks to the countries’ macroeconomic situation, which the Program will help mitigate by strengthening the capacity of key institutions at all levels during rollout. 143. Fiduciary Risk is High. Differences in capacity for procurement, fiduciary management, and project management among participating countries could lead to uneven progress in implementing activities and achieving targets. Fiduciary assessments have been conducted in each country and provisions have been made to strengthen the procurement and FM capacity for both the newly recruited and existing staff. Implementation will be decentralized for countries where capacity limitation, staff turnover, and limited oversight capacity could affect fiduciary compliance. Based on the procurement capacity assessments carried out, the procurement risks are related to lack of adequate procurement capacity (structure and staffing) to accommodate the workload associated with the new project and lack of experience in the procurement of some of the envisaged activities like the contracting and management of third-party implementer by the MoA (Ethiopia). Absence of adequate skill in preparing bidding documents, evaluating bids, and contract management and inaccessibility of the complaint management process and associated delays affect the integrity of the procurement system. Implementation will be centralized in national PIUs for countries where local capacity limitation, staff turnover, and limited oversight capacity could affect fiduciary compliance, despite the provisions made to strengthen the capacity of both the newly recruited and existing staff. In some of the countries, the shortage of basic construction inputs and price hike are affecting market responsiveness and successful completion of contracts. In addition, the restrictions associated with COVID and security and fragility situations affect the interest of potential contractors/consultants (local and international) to participate in bidding opportunities. Page 54 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 144. The Program’s environmental and social risks have been classified as High. The environmental risk rating is Substantial as the direct environmental risks of the Program are expected to be predictable, reversible, and site specific and are not likely to be highly significant. The social risk rating is High given the contextual risks including the security situation in many countries of AFE, the risk of conflict which can be unpredictable, and social tensions that exist in many countries between groups and factors such as challenges in access to land, implementation of mitigation measures associated with the presence of IP/SSAHUTLC, and inclusion risks. Each project activity will have a specific location and salient physical, biological, and socioeconomic characteristics that will need to be understood as part of the environmental and social assessment. It is expected that the Program’s physical footprint will be limited to small-scale capital investments across participating countries. The Program will have positive environmental and social outcomes involving the management of water resources for resilient and sustainable water supply for productive food systems and water and soil moisture conservation as well as improved livelihoods and the ability to respond to shocks. However, there are also a number of inherent risks including the exclusion of vulnerable and marginalized groups, the potential for social conflict over rights to land and natural resources, physical and economic displacement, and elite capture by members of society leading to unequal distribution of assets and benefits. Risks associated with SEA/SH may occur as a result of Program activities, notably in association with the influx of labor, with even relatively small numbers potentially leading to increased risks. Female workers may be at risk of SEA/SH in the workplace. 145. Inclusive stakeholder engagement and the project-level GRMs will be central to risk mitigation efforts and help manage the grievances of communities or parties who feel, or will be, adversely affected by the Program. The project-level GRMs should serve as an avenue for communities to channel their concerns. Clients need support to establish an accessible, effective, and efficient GRM with the capacity to receive and respond to grievances in local languages on time. Grievances related to SEA/SH as well as other forms of GBV will need to be addressed with special attention, and protocols to enable survivor-centered responses will need to be put in place. 146. Stakeholder risk is Substantial. The large number of stakeholders and development partners active in the region may not have harmonized approaches to support the participating countries. When that is the case, there is a risk of inefficiency, duplication, and possible exclusion, especially of stakeholders belonging to vulnerable and disadvantaged groups. A strong communication strategy and capacity-building plan will be developed at the beginning of the program to mitigate this risk. In addition, in Ethiopia this risk is being mitigated with the establishment of a Multi-Donor Trust Fund which is expected to maximize coordination and harmonization of interventions. The World Bank will also regularly organize consultations with the various development partners to ensure coordination. .
