Page 70 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) ANNEX 1: Ethiopia Food Systems Resilience Project I. STRATEGIC CONTEXT A. Country Context 1. Ethiopia is Africa’s second-most populous country with a population of more than 115 million. Ethiopia has made important development gains over the past two decades. However, they are getting eroded by the ongoing armed conflict in the north. Continued limited access to external financing sources coupled with lack of reprofiling of external debt service obligations poses significant risks to macroeconomic stability. Inflation increased significantly in 2021, mainly driven by high food prices, worsening supply conditions in food markets due to the slowdown in production, disruptions in logistics and supply chains, and the persistent foreign exchange shortages. 2. Precipitated by armed conflict, population displacement, climate change, drought, harvest failures, and the loss of livelihoods, Ethiopia is experiencing one of the worst food security emergencies globally. In total, more than 9.4 million people are estimated to need food and humanitarian assistance across Afar, Amhara (6.8 million people in this region alone), and Tigray according to the United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs.39 The conflict has contributed to the displacement of 2.1 million people, disrupting agricultural and income-generating activities.40 The ongoing drought, particularly severe in the southern parts of the country, has led to extensive crop and livestock losses in the context of a sector that is highly dependent on rainfall. 3. High levels of malnutrition remain a major concern in Ethiopia, particularly for women and children. An estimated 44 percent of children in Ethiopia are stunted, and in the Amhara region, over 55 percent are affected.41 According to a United States Agency for International Development (USAID) report, an estimated 3.8 million children under five years of age and pregnant and lactating women in Tigray, Amhara, and Afar are candidates for nutrition interventions (malnutrition prevention and treatment).42 4. Climate change is widely recognized as a “threat multiplier” due to its role in exacerbating food and nutritional insecurity. Climate change is increasing competition for already scarce natural resources as it brings about greater weather variability, higher temperatures, changes in precipitation, and a higher frequency and intensity of climate-related hazards like flooding and drought. Ethiopia has experienced eight major droughts in the past 15 years. At the same time, Ethiopia’s vulnerability to climate change is compounded by internal and external stress factors. Chronic stressors include high rates of poverty, especially in rural areas where 26 percent of the population falls below the national poverty line, while acute ones include active conflict, the worsening food insecurity situation (which was already critical in the wake of locust infestations and flooding), and the economic impacts of COVID-19. Ethiopia has the fifth-highest number of COVID-19 cases in Sub-Saharan Africa. 5. The project introduces many new and innovative elements. They include support for: (a) the implementation of agricultural and rural development policy reforms and improvements in the enabling environment for agriculture, (b) the private sector provision of agricultural services and private investment in 39 https://reports.unocha.org/en/country/ethiopia/ 40 https://reliefweb.int/report/ethiopia/ethiopia-northern-ethiopia-crisis-fact-sheet-5-fiscal-year-fy-2022 41 Government of the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia (2013) National Nutrition Program. 42https://reliefweb.int/report/ethiopia/ethiopia-northern-ethiopia-crisis-fact-sheet-1-fiscal-year-fy- 2022#_ga=2.225968538.1684441589.1637978294-879527520.1637978294 Page 71 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) supply chain infrastructure, (c) private agribusiness development and related job creation (including support for individual enterprises and groups thereof), (d) the application of digital technologies in agriculture to reduce the digital divide, (e) urban and peri urban agriculture as a source of both food and income for poor urban populations, (f) the agricultural technical vocational education and training (ATVET) system, which trains extension workers, (g) the MoA’s M&E systems, and (h) agricultural lending (through technical assistance to both lenders and borrowers). The Ethiopia FSRP will strive to take a people-centric approach to activity design and implementation. The Government has also agreed to adopt third-party implementation arrangements in Tigray. B. Sectoral and Institutional Context 6. The performance of the Ethiopian economy is highly correlated with that of the agricultural sector. The sector is a direct source of employment and income and an important source of livelihoods and resilience for much of its population. 7. Ethiopia’s agricultural sector grew by an average of 7.6 percent per year during the 2010s and significantly contributed to poverty alleviation. The sector is credited with having reduced poverty by an average of 0.9 percent per year over that period. In 2004–2014, agricultural growth was driven by rapid increases in yields (averaging 7 percent per year) as well as a similarly rapid spatial expansion (averaging 2.7 percent year). Significant increases in the adoption of improved seeds and fertilizer and considerable public investment in the sector, including in agricultural extension, rural roads, and irrigation, played a major role in promoting higher yields. 8. While agricultural growth has been impressive, there are concerns about the sustainability of that growth. Reasons why the sector may struggle to sustain such high growth rates, especially as it moves beyond its “low starting point,” include the expected impacts of climate change, the underdeveloped state of the country’s irrigation infrastructure, and the continued use of environmentally unsustainable agricultural practices. In fact, sector growth rates have already begun to decline. Whereas Ethiopia’s agricultural GDP grew by an average of 6.3 percent per year during the first phase of its Growth and Transformation Plan (GTP I) spanning 2010–2015, that rate fell to 4.1 percent in the plan’s second period (GTP II) spanning 2015–2020. Crop production during the main harvest season grew by only 2 percent in 2021 compared to 6.2 percent in 2020.43 This slowdown highlights the need to rethink Ethiopia’s strategy for accelerating or even sustaining agricultural growth. 9. The Ethiopia Food Systems Resilience Project (Ethiopia FSRP) will address multiple related challenges, including the following ones: (a) Climate change, especially as manifest in severe droughts and floods and erratic weather patterns. Long-term climate change predictions are varied but converge in expecting droughts and floods as well as increasing weather variability, to remain major stress factors for the national food system in the coming decades. Biotic and abiotic stressors increase the vulnerability of the agricultural sector. In 2020, Ethiopia witnessed the worst locust invasions of the past quarter century, an event partly attributable to the changing climate. Investments in CSA are needed to help the agricultural sector adapt while contributing to climate change mitigation efforts. (b) Agricultural water availability and control (limited access to irrigation and weak management of irrigation water resources). While the irrigated area expanded faster in Ethiopia than it did anywhere else on the continent—growing by 52 percent between 2002 and 2014—less than 8 43According to the MoA, 41 million quintals of crop (about 12 percent of 2021 meher grain production) is estimated to have been damaged in the Amhara region alone due to the conflict. Production of sesame—a major export commodity—is the most affected as 70 percent of production comes from areas affected by the conflict. Page 72 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) percent (1.2 million ha) of Ethiopia’s cultivated land is currently irrigated and 92 percent of crop production is rain fed. To reach its full agricultural potential going forward, Ethiopia will need to build out and improve its irrigation and water storage infrastructure. This could involve investing in small dams, ponds and tanks, and other water harvesting structures at the community and household levels as well as improving landscapes and watersheds to facilitate “natural storage” solutions such as aquifer storage. Overall, a more strategic approach to the management of water resources will help build the resilience of Ethiopia’s agricultural production capacity in the context of a changing climate. (c) Land degradation and the loss of topsoil. A significant share of Ethiopia’s agricultural soils is not as productive as they could be due to a suboptimal pH level. According to Ethiopian Soil Information System data, 28 percent of soils are acidic while a significant proportion are alkaline. Furthermore, the extensive loss of soil organic matter is diminishing the productive capacity of Ethiopia’s agricultural land and reducing soils’ capacity to retain moisture, leaving rainfed farms more vulnerable to drought. Efforts to restore degraded landscapes and enhance soil fertility are needed to sustain productivity growth, especially in smallholder farming systems that operate with relatively fewer technical skills. (d) Yield gaps, especially in the livestock and horticulture subsectors. Although Ethiopia has seen remarkable increases in yields over the past decade, significant yield gaps remain. Some of these could be addressed by reorienting and diversifying agricultural services to address the causes of yield gaps in these subsectors and help them realize their full potential. (e) Post-harvest losses are in the double digits for many commodities. These losses are driven in part by fragmentation of supply chains and lack of vertical coordination between upstream and downstream segments. Poorly coordinated value chains are less competitive in domestic and export markets. (f) Gender disparities.44 Ethiopia’s agrifood system is marked by various gender-related issues that deserve careful consideration in the context of building a resilient food system. Decision-making and control over economic assets are generally dominated by males, leaving women farmers with fewer and lower-quality resources for production and post-harvest management. A 2019 study found that female farm managers produced 36 percent less per hectare than their male counterparts,45 and malnutrition is more pronounced among women and children than it is among men. The voices and agency of women, girls, and youth need to be strengthened to increase their participation in decision-making in all spheres of life. (g) Weak private sector investment in agriculture (beyond smallholder farmers and their cooperatives). Private sector engagement in agriculture can drive commercialization, promote market-led development, strengthen the links of the sector to manufacturing and exports, and create jobs. However, distortive policies, the state’s overbearing presence in the business sector, and macroeconomic imbalances keep private investments at bay in Ethiopia. Private participation in the sector may be enhanced by (i) a supportive enabling environment that ensures a level playing field; (ii) improved access to agricultural finance, which is currently limited; (iii) a more vibrant 44 In this project, gender also includes persons living with disabilities who should be included and duly targeted by specific project activities. 45 World Bank (2019), Ethiopian Gender Diagnostic Report – Priorities for Equity. Page 73 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) business ecosystem involving MSMEs; and (iv) greater institutional capacity within the business sector. 10. From a national policy perspective, the Ethiopia FSRP is informed and guided by three key policy documents. They are the country’s Vision 2030 food system strategy, the MoA’s forthcoming revision of the National Agriculture and Rural Development Policy (ARDP), and the 10-Year Agriculture Sector Perspective Plan (2020–2030). Following the successful two phases of the AGP (AGP I, P113032; and AGP II, P148591), the proposed Ethiopia FSRP is expected to become the next flagship project of the MoA. 11. Issued in 2021, “Vision 2030: Transforming Ethiopian Food System” of the Government of Ethiopia (GoE) identifies five central goals for achieving a “food system transformation,” as called for by the United Nation’s 2021 Global Food Systems Summit. They are to (a) diversify food production and increase the supply of healthy foods; (b) strengthen supply chains and develop innovative strategies and mechanisms to improve food management and handling systems; (c) promote food production practices that conserve soil health and the environment and improve access to agricultural inputs, technologies, and financial services; (d) develop an agrifood processing sector consistent with more equitable food-related livelihoods, improved food safety, and the mitigation of post-harvest losses; and (e) build resilience to vulnerabilities and shocks. 12. The MoA is currently revising the ARDP. The reform foresees six key changes: (a) an increased focus on labor productivity in agricultural production, together with the longer-standing focus on yield enhancement; (b) a renewed focus on strategic value chains from the perspectives of export diversification and import substitution; (c) a greater emphasis on land and water development; (d) a greater focus on rural development; (e) increased attention to capital flows to the agricultural sector; and (f) a shift in the public sector’s approach to agricultural development, characterized by move away from public sector-led development, and an embrace of more pluralistic systems. At the time of writing, the revised policy was under review by the government and expected to be approved in due course. Alongside the revised policy, the MoA’s 10-Year Agriculture Sector Perspective Plan (2020–2030) sets ambitious targets relating to productivity, value addition, and the competitiveness of smallholder and pastoral agriculture. 13. From a regional perspective, increasing productivity in Ethiopia to promote exports, reduce imports, and improve nutrition can have positive spillover effects on regional food security. The majority of Ethiopia’s neighboring countries—notably Djibouti, Somalia, Eritrea, and Sudan—are lowlands with scarce rainfall that rely heavily on food imports, including from Ethiopia. Today, agriculture accounts for about 60 percent of Ethiopia's exports and 13 percent of its total exports are to other African countries. In that respect, the benefits of investments in key agrifood value chains within Ethiopia could have positive effects on food security beyond Ethiopia’s borders. 14. These regional benefits cannot be achieved by activities in isolation. The overall framework of the Ethiopia FSRP contributes to improving regional food security and food system resilience. The CAADP of 2012 identifies four key pillars for food security improvement and agricultural investment: (a) sustainable land and water management, (b) market access, (c) food supply and hunger, and (d) agricultural research. By addressing these pillars in a holistic way through its four components, the Ethiopia FSRP will be able to improve the national and regional availability of food with significant benefits for regional food security. Even activities seemingly national in focus, like irrigation investments, are needed to diversify production and have the potential to contribute to dietary diversification and nutritional security at the regional level. 15. Since Ethiopia is part of Phase 1 of the MPA, its experience will generate lessons learned for other countries participating in the MPA. Knowledge will be generated, for instance, on digital and other technologies Page 74 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) promoted under Component 1, the implementation of small- and micro-scale irrigation promoted under Component 2 (including the innovative “irrigation plus” model described below), and ways of improving enabling conditions for national and regional agrifood trade. C. Relevance to Higher-Level Objective 16. The project supports the GoE’s vision of a structurally transforming national economy and fully aligns with the World Bank Country Partnership Framework (CPF) for Ethiopia for FY18–FY22 (Report No. 115135-ET) discussed by the Board on May 22, 2017 The Ethiopia FSRP is specifically aligned with two pillars of the CPF— Promoting Structural and Economic Transformation through Increased Productivity and Building Resilience and Inclusiveness—and will contribute to several CPF results framework targets, including enhanced resilience and inclusiveness, increased agricultural yields, job creation, natural resources management, gender equity, and climate co-benefits. In addition, the project will address the WBG’s twin goals of alleviating poverty and boosting shared prosperity. The project will also support the four strategic objectives of the World Bank Gender Strategy for 2016–2023: (a) improving human endowments, (b) removing constraints to more and better jobs, (c) removing barriers to women’s ownership and control of assets, and (d) enhancing women’s voice and agency and engaging men and boys in activities that support gender equality. The FSRP is also aligned with the GoE’s Agricultural Sector Perspective Plan (2020–2030) and the revised ARDP. The National Adaptation Plan and the Climate Resilient Green Economy strategies strongly emphasize that CSA has an important role to play in meeting national climate change mitigation and adaptation goals, which the project fully supports. The FSRP will also contribute to the goals outlined by Ethiopia’s Vision 2030: Transforming Ethiopian Food System, the World Bank’s ACBP, and Ethiopia’s Nationally Determined Contribution. The financing aligns with Ethiopia’s commitment to the CAADP and the commitment of The Africa Food Security Leadership Dialogue to invest in science and promote CSA research (under Climate Change in Africa). II. PROJECT DESCRIPTION A. Project Development Objective 17. The PDO of the Ethiopia FSRP is to increase the resilience of food systems and preparedness for food insecurity in project areas. Progress toward the PDO will be measured using five PDO indicators and 20 intermediate indicators. All relevant indicators will be disaggregated by gender (men and women) and age (youth and adult), as described in table A1.1. B. Project Results Indicators Table A1.1. Project Description 18. In addition to introducing many new and innovative elements as mentioned above, the project builds upon more than a decade of successful experience implementing AGP I and II. While the project will introduce many new elements, it will also seek to scale up many of the successful interventions and approaches of AGP I and II. The Ethiopia FSRP will expand its reach to additional woredas and specifically scale up or continue the push for (a) an integrated “one-wash” agenda using an “irrigation plus” approach; (b) the mainstreaming of nutrition-, gender-, and climate-smart agriculture; (c) an integrated research and extension system that is responsive to farmers; (d) soil and water conservation measures in upstream catchment areas that ensure the sustainability of SSI and other activities related to micro-scale irrigation technologies (MSITs); and (e) improved agricultural service delivery through and the transition to a pluralistic agricultural extension system. Component 1: Transforming Agricultural Services and Innovation Systems (US$242.47 million, of which US$215.88 million IDA equivalent and US$26.59 million Multi-Donor Trust Fund [MDTF]) 19. Aligned with Pillar 2 of the MPA, Component 1 of the Ethiopia FSRP will promote the adoption of CSA Page 77 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) practices at the national level to strengthen regional food security. This component will focus on the entire ecosystem of agricultural services to enable the adoption of improved technologies and sustainable agricultural practices, both on-farm and in post-harvest stages of production. When appropriate, it will be farmer led. Subcomponent 1.1: Promoting the Efficiency, Efficacy, and Scale-Up of Agricultural Services 20. This subcomponent aims to improve the quality, relevance, and coverage of agricultural services including agricultural extension, plant protection, and animal health ones. Proposed interventions include (a) upgrading services through the development of physical and human resources; (b) promoting differentiated services that are responsive to diverse circumstances (including differences in agroecology, soil type, and household resources, and vulnerability); (c) introducing new delivery models, including ones that leverage digital technologies; and (d) promoting greater pluralism in service delivery. With respect to extension services, the project will specifically promote specialized services relating to mechanization, nutrition-sensitive agriculture, 46 post-harvest management activities, urban and peri urban agriculture, and irrigated agriculture. This subcomponent will specifically finance four complementary activities: (a) Strengthening agricultural extension services. This subcomponent will both strengthen the public extension system and promote private sector engagement in extension service delivery by piloting and/or scaling up public-private arrangements. It will promote advisory and other specialized services that encourage the adoption and scale-up of CSA for increased production, enhanced resilience, and reduced GHG emissions through training, capacity building, and information dissemination. Services will be tailored to women, including female household heads (FHHs) and others who are members of male-headed households. The subcomponent will also support large- scale, climate-smart demonstration activities—lessons learned from which will also benefit other countries joining the MPA—and the upgrading of farmer training centers. In addition, the subcomponent will develop and roll out public-private modalities for extension service delivery that improve the financial sustainability of public extension services. Gender-sensitive technologies will be selected based on the priorities identified by AGP II.47 Lastly, the subcomponent will create structures that enhance “connectivity” of farmers, such as farmer training and knowledge centers that give smallholders access to information, technologies, and the resources they need to adapt to climate change and access new markets. The subcomponent will also help farmers adopt agronomic practices that help reduce the carbon intensity of agricultural land, including by increasing carbon stocks and carbon sequestration (through improved natural resources and soil fertility management and measures to restore vegetation cover). These efforts will contribute to reducing GHG emissions and mitigating the effects of climate change. (b) Supporting agricultural training services. Reforming the ATVET system and improving the in-service and preservice training of development agents (DAs) and subject matter specialists are priorities of the Ethiopia FSRP. This subcomponent will support the development of various skill sets relating, for example, to climate adaptation and mitigation planning, CSA at the farm and landscape levels, 46 NSA interventions contribute to improved nutrition through three pathways: (a) food production, (b) agricultural income, and (c) women’s empowerment. NSA activities include strengthening capacity of agriculture extension workers to promote dietary diversity; scaling up selected nutrition-sensitive technologies to increase production and consumption of diverse foods at the household level and improving post-harvest handling, food storage, preservation, and processing; and increasing women’s role in decision-making over use of income for household nutrition. 47 Gender-sensitive technologies identified within AGP II include (a) small ruminants (shoats) production package; (b) agricultural mechanization: multi-crop threshers, power tillers, harvesters, and hand maize sheller; (c) fully packaged poultry production; (d) fully packaged modern beekeeping (through queen rearing); (e) manually operated milk churner; and (f) modern stove molder. Page 78 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) agroprocessing to reduce food loss and waste and associated GHG emissions, household-level food processing and preservation (for enhanced nutrition), rural business development, and other nonagricultural activities. Curriculums for agricultural training services will integrate content on climate change and its mitigation, such as causes and impacts of climate change and activities that capture, sequester, or reduce GHG emissions. Curriculums will also include content on climate change adaptation, such as the local impacts of climate change, drought management in agriculture, flood response, and water conservation, with the aim of shaping a next generation of agricultural extension workers who are expert in CSA. The project will take measures to attract women to the ATVET system (for example, scholarships, rewards, and flexible hours of attendance) and assess and strengthen the gender sensitivity of ATVET’s curriculum. It will also adopt dedicated indicators to assess development agents’ effective promotion of gender equality in their field of expertise and their acquisition of relevant skills and knowledge. (c) Supporting plant protection and animal health services. This subcomponent will broadly seek to strengthen the management of plant and animal pests and diseases. To prevent pest-related crop losses and improve animal health, it will promote (i) climate and pest monitoring services that help predict and enable rapid responses to new, emerging, and existing pests and (ii) integrated pest management approaches that emphasize preventive measures including ones that anticipate changes in pest dynamics under changing climatic conditions. The subcomponent will invest in strengthening of animal health posts, animal health clinics, cattle crush, livestock quarantine stations, livestock genetic improvement centers, and so on. Livestock veterinary services will be supported to control/prevent/ major livestock diseases. This will lead to more efficient production and/or better-quality animal products that can contribute to food security as well as climate change adaptation and mitigation. In the process, the project will promote and incentivize the private and community-based provision of animal health and plant protection services. The latter may involve the surveillance, monitoring, and diagnosis of plant pests and pathologies, plant quarantine and management services, and the organization and licensing of spraying service providers. (d) Supporting public agricultural regulatory services. This subcomponent will strengthen inspection, quality assurance, testing, certification, and other regulatory services that can help improve sector competitiveness and boost trade, while also enhancing climate change readiness. Relevant regulatory services relate, for example, to monitoring and regulating agro-input suppliers and building climate literacy to increase climate-smart pest management knowledge; implementing effective regulatory procedures to register and control available agro-inputs and monitor the quality of products on the market; and developing public policies and regulatory instruments that incentivize and reward farmers who adopt climate-smart pest management practices. The subcomponent will also build institutional capacity for both public sector regulation and self- regulation by market actors and associations. Subcomponent 1.2: Transforming Agricultural Technology Generation and Research System 21. This subcomponent will focus on generating agricultural technologies that improve crop and livestock productivity as well as the management of natural resources, while enhancing resilience and reducing agricultural GHG emissions. In this endeavor, the project will build on the rich experience of AGP I and II. By strengthening systems for commercializing agricultural technology and innovation, the project will promote a more market-oriented R&D system. Among other things, the subcomponent will support agricultural research activities within both public and private institutions (bringing in regional expertise and expanding on national areas of excellence), the development of strong PPPs, participatory research projects, and “pre-extension” Page 79 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) demonstrations that enhance links among farmers, extension services, and research. The subcomponent will conduct participatory appraisals of the current agricultural extension system to identify its structural weaknesses, understand the roles and links of different stakeholders, and identify (and prioritize) ways in which extension services can reduce farmers’ vulnerability, thus increasing the system’s capacity to support farmer resilience. It will also support the ongoing elaboration of a CSA research agenda. 22. Research and commercialization efforts will focus heavily on climate-smart technology. The selection of supported technologies will be guided in part by activities from the approved list in Annex A.C.1 of the 2020 Joint Report on Multilateral Development Banks’ Climate Finance.48 Mitigation technologies promoted by the project will conform to those listed in Table 5 of Common Principles for Climate Mitigation Finance Tracking for Multilateral Development Banks.49 Families of technology supported by the project will include (a) irrigated agriculture management technologies, (b) acid soil and vertisol management technologies, (c) integrated soil nutrient and crop management technologies, (d) soil and water conservation technologies, (e) agroforestry technologies, (f) “source technology” (the production of breeder seeds, breeds, feed seeds, prototypes, tissue cultures, microorganisms, and so forth), and (g) agricultural machinery. Many of these and other technologies will help farmers adapt to climate change, reduce their GHG emissions, and increase their sequestration of carbon. Specifically, technologies supporting soil and water conversation and agroforestry will enhance carbon sequestration. Technologies around irrigated agriculture, acid soil, and soil nutrient management will ensure judicious fertilizer use and help reduce the emission intensity of agricultural fields, while mechanization technologies will focus on energy efficiency and reduce overall emissions. Investing in the deployment of such technologies—from research into use—is considered a centerpiece of climate change adaptation and mitigation. Subcomponent 1.3 Digital Agricultural Services 23. To help achieve the government’s vision of digitizing agricultural systems and support the “last-mile delivery” of digital agricultural services to farmers (men, women, and youth), this subcomponent will stimulate the development of a vibrant digital agricultural innovation ecosystem in Ethiopia. Subcomponent 1.3 will support (a) the delivery of digital agricultural extension and advisory services (relating, for example, to agrometeorology, climate risks, soil health, livestock health, and more); (b) the implementation of the national roadmap on digital agricultural extension and advisory services that is currently being developed, which will also provide inputs to a larger regional strategy on digitalization supported by IGAD under the MPA; (c) upgrading of the MoA’s data systems and infrastructure; and (d) a platform that facilitates private sector start-ups’ delivery of digital services to project beneficiaries. Digital technologies can help support agrifood systems that are more productive, adapted to climate change, gender inclusive, environmentally friendly, and resilient. Digital agriculture and big data enable analyses that can inform landscape-level adaptation planning to achieve higher productivity while minimizing GHG and other emissions. Combining climate information with site-specific data on factors such as soil fertility and erosion risk enables decision-making on the sustainable productive potential of land in the near and long term. In terms of mitigation, digital agriculture enables more efficient fertilizer and other input use. Under this subcomponent, support for soil health advisory services will improve soil fertility management, encourage more judicious fertilizer use, mitigate related emissions, and contribute to carbon sequestration, thus helping to reduce the carbon footprint of agriculture while enhancing food security. Moreover, the delivery of agricultural services will reduce the need for extension agents for more face-to-face interaction, thereby reducing field travel and fossil fuel emission. 48 ERBD et al. (2021), 2020 Joint Report on Multilateral Development Banks’ Climate Finance. 49 https://www.eib.org/attachments/documents/mdb_idfc_mitigation_common_principles_en.pdf Page 80 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Component 2: Resilient Small-Scale Irrigation Development and Management (US$217.26 million, of which US$192.09 million IDA equivalent, US$18.55 million MDTF, and US$6.62 million from beneficiary contribution) 24. Rural populations in Ethiopia are quite vulnerable to climatic risk and this is particularly true of smallholder farmers who are reliant on rainfed agriculture. To address their vulnerability and build their resilience together with that of the broader food system, Component 2 of the Ethiopia FSRP will—in keeping with Pillar 3 of the MPA—support the development and management of resilient SSI. It will support the development of irrigation with a focus on water- and energy-efficient SSI schemes50 and MSITs,51 the choice depending on water resources suitability, farmer segmentation, and agricultural value chains. This component is critical for regional food security as it will help boost productivity, in turn, reducing food imports and boosting exports. Irrigation infrastructure and management capacity will enhance farmers’ ability to manage climate-induced water scarcity. At the same time, introducing irrigation will allow the introduction of horticulture in some suitable areas, thus contributing to higher incomes and better nutrition. Subcomponent 2.1: Small-Scale and Household-Level Irrigation Development 25. This subcomponent is expected to expand irrigated areas in project woredas to intensify production and increase farmers’ resilience to climate change-induced weather variability. It will develop irrigation infrastructure through the rehabilitation of existing SSI schemes and the construction of new gravity flow irrigation schemes, thus rendering the use of diesel pumps that contribute to carbon emissions unnecessary. The latter may include water storage infrastructure such as small dams, ponds, and tanks and other relevant community- and household-level water harvesting structures. The project will also rehabilitate irrigation systems to improve energy efficiency and reduce GHG emissions, for instance, through the installation of solar pumping in micro- household-level schemes. Rehabilitated irrigation infrastructure such as canals will help reduce leakages and improve water availability. Opportunities may exist to reduce time spent on household work and support gender- and nutrition-sensitive technologies. Under this subcomponent, a number of household-based gender-related activities that were initiated under AGP II to transform social and cultural norms will be assessed for scale-up. It will also promote and demonstrate simple household- or community-level irrigation technologies like FLID and MSIT, along with water saving irrigation methods such as drip and micro-sprinkler irrigation. Overall, the program plans to develop 35,000 ha of irrigated farmland, of which 12,000 ha will be rehabilitated or upgraded, 7,000 ha will involve new SSI schemes, and 16,000 ha will use MSITs. The irrigation infrastructure developed by the project will have a minimal carbon footprint and is expected to mitigate GHG emissions for three reasons. First, a majority of the irrigation schemes supported by the project will be gravity flow schemes not requiring any energy for water lifting and distribution. Second, access to irrigation will significantly boost productivity, thereby reducing emission intensity per ha of land and agricultural output. Third, afforestation and other soil and water conservation measures upstream of irrigation works will help reduce siltation and help sequester carbon through afforestation measures. 26. SSI schemes will be implemented using a watershed approach that considers upstream and downstream uses of water. This approach is expected to help avoid or minimize conflict among upstream and downstream water users while ensuring a sustainable use of water resources. In certain “hotspot” areas, the approach may lead to parallel investments in upstream watershed management works that help prevent 50 SSI schemes, in the Ethiopian context, refer to individual or community managed schemes up to 200 ha using surface or groundwater resources. 51 MSITs in Ethiopia refer to small water harvesting structure (such as farm ponds) and manual or motorized pumps in combination with application technologies (for example, sprinklers and drip) often used by individual households (less than 5 ha) or in small groups (less than 20 ha). Page 81 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) sedimentation in irrigation infrastructure, enhance flood protection, and improve groundwater recharge. The development of irrigated agriculture is viewed as a means of combatting food insecurity under changing climatic conditions. Improved water management based on the introduction of new irrigation techniques and climate- resilient agricultural practices can help improve the living conditions, food security, and nutritional status of farming families. Through the promotion of FLID and MSITs, the project will incentivize farmers to invest in on- farm water resource development, provide farmers with related services (such as drilling, input supply, and the repair of water lifting equipment), and introduce climate-smart technologies and best practices (such as solar pumping, through trainings and financial schemes) that help increase water use efficiency and water availability while saving energy and reducing emissions. A demand-driven “irrigation plus” approach, 52 which was tested with encouraging results under AGP II, will also be adopted, and special attention will be given to nutrition-sensitive irrigation. Subcomponent 2.2: Water Management and Infrastructure Governance 27. This subcomponent will support the establishment of IWUAs and targeted capacity building aimed at making them more effective and inclusive organizations. It will contribute to improving users’ resilience by providing climate change-related trainings, enhancing community institutions, enhancing IWUAs, and establishing or strengthening farmers’ associations. The latter will be encouraged to seek greater participation of women and youth and supported to develop the capacity to manage and maintain the irrigation systems they oversee. Activities may cover trainings on farm-level irrigation water management—including on climate change—and general management skills relating to topics like planning, organizational management, women and youth assertiveness and leadership, infrastructure O&M, O&M costing and water pricing, accounting, and bookkeeping. Component 3: Food Market Development (US$135.09 million of which US$110.43 million IDA equivalent, US$13.38 million MDTF, and US$11.28 million beneficiary contribution) 28. Component 3 will strengthen agrifood value chains in ways that improve market efficiency and market access. Whereas Components 1 and 2 focus on improving the efficiency of production, helping subsistence farmers generate a surplus that can be sold on markets and diversify into higher-value products, Component 3, in keeping with Pillar 4 of the MPA, will support well-functioning agricultural markets (input, output, and financial markets) and farmers’ access to them. In this way, Component 3 will help producers—including women, men, and youth—add value to and obtain higher prices for their outputs and increase domestic and regional food availability, thus enhancing resilience to shocks. Improving smallholders’ access to markets can, on the one hand, help increase the availability of a diversity of micronutrient-rich foods on domestic and regional markets and, on the other hand, support the food security of rural households by helping to diversify and enhance those farmers’ livelihoods. Higher and more diversified sources of income increase farming households’ resilience by improving their access to adequate, safe, and nutritious food and helping them smooth consumption and preserve assets when shocks occur. The project’s support for agrifood marketing will be informed by its potential to contribute to food security and improved nutrition at the household level. Moreover, by focusing on regionally strategic value chains, it will help boost regional trade. 52 Irrigation plus is a multiuse system (MUS) entry point, under which irrigation systems are redesigned and developed such that non- irrigation uses—both productive and domestic—can be accommodated. The premise behind the MUS is that people’s livelihoods require water for a variety of purposes. In addition to irrigated agriculture, rural households typically use at least some water for livestock, home gardens, and other small-scale productive uses as well as domestic uses such as drinking, washing, and cooking. Page 82 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Subcomponent 3.1: Improved Market Services 29. This subcomponent will enhance resilience and adaptive capacity by strengthening a variety of market services. It will develop or upgrade certification, traceability and quality standards, market promotion, financial services, weather-resistant footbridges, and climate-resilient market infrastructure, helping to build the resilience of targeted communities. Footbridges can improve connectivity to markets, particularly during flooding and heavy rainfall events, during which communities residing in highlands can get cut off from the outside world. Improved commercialization (though climate-resilient market infrastructure development) is a core adaptation strategy. Targeted services will include ones that facilitate value chain transactions and innovation, ensure food safety and traceability, engage in trade facilitation and export promotion, and enable the effective regulation of agribusiness. Through investments in market infrastructure, the subcomponent also aims to mitigate post-harvest losses and associated GHG emissions. It will also support the inclusive provision of value chain information and finance, notably by building the capacity of financial institutions and exploring credit facilitation mechanisms. Privileging PPPs where appropriate, the project will take steps to build a resilient and inclusive financial system that involves the private sector in financing agricultural marketing and value chain infrastructure. At the same time, it will invest in relevant public infrastructure, including physical markets and “last-mile” connective infrastructure such as footbridges. Investments in public infrastructure and markets will systematically consider climate risk and strive for resilient design. 30. The subcomponent will also promote access to financial services with a particular focus on female farmers and agro-entrepreneurs. It will contribute to resilience through interventions that enhance farmers’ access to tailored and demand-driven financial services, build the capacity of financial institutions to manage exposure to specific agricultural risks, and support financial institutions in reducing transaction costs. It will help establish and enhance access to financial mechanisms including microcredit that increases farmers’ capacity to invest in farm- or landscape-level changes in production systems. In terms of promoting women’s access to finance, the project may work to ease the bureaucratic and collateral requirements faced by women’s savings and credit associations. By offering technical and managerial training and business development and marketing services, the subcomponent will also support producer cooperatives and unions in taking on a more commercial orientation. Subcomponent 3.2: Enterprise Development 31. This subcomponent will support enterprise development as a means of strengthening agricultural markets, developing nonfarm rural employment, and driving down poverty. To build the resilience of households in the context of a changing climate, this subcomponent will attempt to reduce reliance on land-based livelihoods and support rural off-farm employment by working with individual enterprises as well as groups thereof. This subcomponent will specifically support climate-informed business development and incubation services and skills training, working either with individual enterprises (like sole proprietorships or partnerships), or collective ones (like common interest groups [CIGs] or cooperatives). The subcomponent will be deliberate about considering specific gender-related needs, constraints, and aspirations. Particular attention will also be paid to fostering productive partnerships between farmers (involved in production, primary processing, and other upstream agribusiness activities) and both input suppliers and agribusinesses (involved in trading, processing, food services, and other downstream activities). Subcomponent 3.3: Strengthening Smallholder Farmers’ Market Linkages 32. This subcomponent aims to strengthen smallholder farmers’ market linkages. It will build the capacity of farmers’ organizations and institutions involved in climate-smart value chain development. It will generally help Page 83 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) smallholders manage climate risks, reduce transaction costs, and realize economies of scale. Specifically, the subcomponent will pilot contract farming and outgrower schemes for climate-smart agricultural products, provide technical support to aggregation platforms, strengthen value chain alliances and agribusiness associations, and facilitate value chain finance arrangements, including by leveraging digital tools when appropriate. These activities are required to create a stable market system, a core requirement for building climate resilience. Criteria for technical support will be climate informed, for instance, addressing climate risks across the entire value chain, with specific channels for increasing resilience and reducing emissions from the agricultural sector. Building a robust and stable market system and connecting farmers directly with major buyers can contribute to significant reduction in post-harvest losses (by improving supply chain efficiency) and associated GHG emissions. Subcomponent 3.4: Strengthening Regionally Strategic Value Chains 33. This subcomponent will identify value chain bottlenecks and work to overcome those by making strategic investments and coordinating value chain stakeholders. It will focus on developing competitive value chains of strategic importance for regional food security. For investments in agricultural value chain development to have a sustainable impact on poverty and food security, climate resilience must be a priority outcome. To achieve this, the project will ensure that actors involved in agricultural value chains are aware of climate risks and empowered to manage these risks. To build climate-resilient value chains, the project will also focus on improving (a) climate risk assessment, (b) adaptive management of value chain activities, and (c) response to shocks by value chain actors. This subcomponent will invest in cold chain facilities, milk collection facilities, and post-harvest storage infrastructure and address other infrastructure bottlenecks so as to improve climate resilience and mitigation in priority value chains. The subcomponent will focus on both the upstream and downstream segments of those value chains to increase the supply and quality of agricultural products, add value to them in ways that support nutritional security, and have a tangible, positive impact on regional food security and trade. One way it will do this is by providing climate-informed competitive matching support to enterprises. The latter could be used, for example, to fund the construction of cooling facilities or warehouses. The subcomponent will strengthen producers’ links to markets and aggregation arrangements, prioritizing, when possible, value chains that are critical to women’s empowerment or offer nutritional benefits. Component 4: Improving the Enabling Environment for Food Systems Resilience and Project Management (US$90.68 million of which US$81.67 million IDA equivalent and US$9.07 million MDTF) 34. Component 4 will help finance, execute, and monitor the implementation of Ethiopia’s new ARDP and more broadly support the development and operationalization of policies that help scale up CSA and build food systems resilience. Aligned with Pillar 5 of the MPA, this component will also support project management and ensure that the project’s performance and impact are carefully tracked. Subcomponent 4.1: Support for National Agricultural and Rural Development Policy Reforms and Mainstreaming of cross cutting issues 35. This subcomponent will support the ARDP’s implementation. Agricultural policy interventions play a key role in increasing agricultural productivity and generating economic growth, both of which help reduce poverty, food insecurity, malnutrition, climate change vulnerability, and gender inequality. Over the past two years, the GoE has worked on revisions to the ARDP. The project will support the reform’s implementation in three ways . 36. First, it will help design and pilot new approaches and provide technical assistance to take forward new initiatives introduced by the ARDP. The ARDP foresees significant shifts in the way agricultural development is approached in Ethiopia—in directions where country experience is lacking. Innovation is also needed for climate Page 84 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) resilience and mitigation. In this context, the project could pilot (a) PPPs for the development of rural infrastructure, including tripartite arrangements involving private infrastructure developers, financial institutions, and the government; (b) the transformation of producer groups and farmers’ associations into commercial production companies; and (c) new agricultural finance products, including related processes and toolkits to enable access to finance for climate adaptation and mitigation. 37. Second, it will support the development of more detailed policy actions and instruments. The project will undertake analytical work to identify detailed policy actions and formulate or revise regulations with the potential to improve the enabling environment for agricultural development. It will also provide legal support for drafting relevant regulations. Activities that will be supported will include establishing financial incentives to promote public investment projects that strengthen agricultural climate resilience and mitigation; developing and implementing policies to incorporate climate adaptation and mitigation measures in agricultural planning processes; repurposing expenditures to support the adoption of CSA and sustainable land management; developing and implementing policies that enhance agricultural productivity and competitiveness by incorporating climate resilience and mitigation measures; supporting the mainstreaming of climate change adaptation and mitigation policies at the national level and strengthening agencies and institutions to monitor and address climate change risks; and introducing policies that result in more efficient fertilizer use and help mitigate fertilizer- and livestock-related GHG emissions. 38. Third, it will strengthen oversight mechanisms with a focus on sector-specific M&E systems . The project will provide technical assistance to the MoA to improve its existing MIS in ways that support its policy reform agenda (including as outlined by the Ten-Year Perspective Plan) while supporting its capacity to monitor and evaluate policies and make strategic use of those systems. 39. Fourth, on a number of topics that are critical to food systems resilience, this subcomponent will support knowledge and awareness among project and government staff. Those topics include nutrition, gender gaps, job creation, pandemic awareness (COVID-19 and HIV), and climate change. The subcomponent will develop, update, and quality-control training materials, brochures, and curricula and create opportunities for experience sharing. CSA, in addition to gender-sensitive and nutrition-sensitive agriculture, is among the project’s cross- cutting agendas. This subcomponent will build capacity of the IAs to mainstream CSA in the overall project and contribute to climate resilience. The capacity development focus on CSA as a cross-cutting agenda will help improve capabilities of IAs to promote climate change mitigation (through carbon sequestration, reducing emission intensity, and reducing post-harvest losses). Subcomponent 4.2: Project Management, Monitoring, Evaluation, and Learning 40. This subcomponent covers project management at the country level. It will handle the Ethiopia FSRP’s planning, implementation, and coordination as well as its communications at the national, regional, woreda, and kebele levels. It will also handle fiduciary matters and monitor environmental and social risk mitigation measures. These functions will be carried out by Project Coordination Units (PCUs) to be put in place at the federal, regional, and woreda levels. The project will finance project staff, consultants, equipment, and capacity-building activities. The project will assess staff and implementing partners’ learning needs and offer continuous training throughout the project cycle—with a focus on gender responsiveness. 41. The project will develop and implement a robust monitoring, evaluation, and learning (MEL) system to track and assess the project’s progress toward the PDO, generate information that can be used in project management, and document and share project learnings. The M&E system will disaggregate all relevant project data by gender, further distinguishing female participants by age (women and girls) and household type (female- Page 85 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) headed and male-headed), and it will analyze how gender gaps evolve over time. The project results MEL system will build on the MoA’s existing M&E system and will be guided by a project-level knowledge management strategy and MEL manual to be prepared at the start of implementation. A project-specific MIS will be established within the MoA’s existing, online M&E MIS platform. The GEMS/KOBO toolbox will be used to supplement the project MIS. Some aspects of M&E will be participatory, engaging the community in tracking project milestones and results and in identifying implementation problems and best practices. Component 5: Contingency Emergency Response Component (US$0) 42. Following an adverse natural event that results in a major natural disaster, the GoE may request the World Bank to reallocate project funds to support short-term response and reconstruction efforts. This subcomponent would draw resources from the unallocated expenditure category and/or allow the government to request the World Bank to recategorize and reallocate funds from other project components to partially cover emergency response expenditures. This component could also channel additional funds should they become available as a result of a qualifying emergency. Should this component be triggered, all expenditures would be made in accordance with the World Bank Policy on Investment Project Financing and reviewed and accepted by the World Bank before any disbursement is made. The bulk of the project interventions will likely be carried out in “high-potential” highland woredas located in nine regional states (Oromia, Amhara, Tigray, SNNPR, Sidama, Benishangul Gumuz, Gambella, South West and Harari) as well as in Dire Dawa City Administration and Addis Ababa (figure A1.1). However, the project may also intervene in less populated lowland areas, in ways that complement the existing portfolio of support measures under the Lowlands Livelihoods Resilience Project (LLRP) (see below). While it will primarily focus on rural areas, the project will also work in selected cities to promote urban agriculture and improve the efficiency of secondary and tertiary agricultural markets. In addition, the project will undertake some activities that are national in scope and will influence the agricultural sector at large, irrespective of geography. Figure A1.1. Map of the Ethiopia FSRP 44. The project has engaged with regional governments within Ethiopia to ensure their buy-in, reflect their unique context in project design, and ensure the Ethiopia FSRP’s coordination with other active projects. The Ethiopia FSRP is one of several World Bank-funded projects that supports agricultural and rural development. It is designed and will be implemented in the context of a comprehensive approach to rural development covering all Page 87 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) regions and the country’s four main agricultural production systems. The latter are (a) mixed crop-livestock agriculture in areas of high agricultural potential, (b) specialized agricultural production systems in urban and periurban areas, (c) more diversified systems in moisture-stressed areas, and (d) pastoral and larger-scale commercial agriculture in the arid and semiarid lowlands. The Ethiopia FSRP will focus on the first two types of production systems but will be implemented in close collaboration with the LLRP, which focuses on (d). 45. In addition to working on broader agricultural development issues in selected geographies, the project will specifically work on a number of strategic and regional value chains. The selection of these value chain will be based on criteria that include (a) potential contribution to the agricultural policy’s ambition of strengthening exports and import substitution; (b) relevance to diversification into high-value production, building on opportunities created by investments in irrigation; (c) relevance to women; (d) potential to generate significant GHG emission reductions; (e) potential to support diet diversification and better nutritional outcomes; and (f) potential to generate jobs. Based on these criteria and after consultation with the relevant regions, the MoA has suggested an initial list of 20 value chains for the project: (a) teff, (b) wheat, (c) sesame, (d) maize, (e) coffee, (f) avocado, (g) chickpeas, (h) soyabean, (i) dairy, (j) poultry, (k) honey, (l) fish, (m) tomato, (n) onion, (o) potato, (p) red meat (fattening), (q) Niger seed, (r) sunflower, (s) flax, and (t) haricot beans. 46. Already involved in project design, the IFC will also be involved in its implementation. The following measures have been put in place to involve the IFC. First, an investment and advisory team from IFC has been part of the core design team for the project. Second, consultations have been undertaken with the IFC Financial Institution Group (FIG) investment team to identify instruments that could be piloted to spur commercial agricultural lending. Third, consultations have been undertaken with the FIG advisory team to develop a joint technical assistance project for commercial banks and MFIs to facilitate agricultural finance. Fourth, consultations have been undertaken with the manufacturing, agribusiness, and services team to forge deeper collaboration (both investment and advisory) around contract farming operations and agribusiness support. 47. The Ethiopia FSRP being the new flagship project, multiple development partners have participated in its design and preparation. Those partners include (a) the European Commission (EC); (b) the Netherlands Ministry of Foreign affairs; (c) USAID; (d) Global Affairs Canada; (e) Spanish Agency for International Development Cooperation; (f) Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW), the German development bank; and (g) the Bill and Melinda Gates Foundation. A US$65.5 million stand-alone co-financing multi-donor trust fund (MDTF) is being established to complement IDA financing. The following four donors have confirmed their commitment to participate in the MDTF: (a) the EC, (b) the Netherlands Ministry of Foreign affairs, (c) USAID, and (d) KfW. 48. The project will be financed by an IDA credit with co-financing in the form of a grant from a new MDTF that is being established. All payments will be shared at the same ratio between the grant and credit across all components. As and when additional contributions are passed on to the Government through the Grant Agreement, this fixed ratio may be amended through revisions in the work plan agreed by the GoE and the World Bank. It is expected that grant resources, in an indicative total amount of US$65.5 million for the recipient- executed portion, will be secured latest by June 30, 2024. The Grant Agreement will be signed upon receipt of the first contributions into the MDTF. Additional grant resources will then be passed on to the Government as and when they are received without the need to process a restructuring or additional financing, but (an) amendment(s) to the Grant Agreement will be required. In the meantime, project implementation will proceed using available IDA resources. If co-financing does not materialize or is less than expected, additional resources may be sought through IDA or trust funds or the project will be scaled down. 49. The project is expected to have the following impact on poverty. The project addresses two critical Page 88 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) factors behind low household income growth in rural areas: low market participation and limited livelihood diversification due to limited off-farm opportunities. A taxonomy of the poor and near-poor men, women, and youth in Ethiopia shows that the majority of them (72 percent) reside in “high-potential” agricultural areas but have limited market participation and limited livelihood diversification. Only 11 percent have nonagricultural income. One pathway out of poverty for this group involves increased agricultural income and livelihood diversification. The project will target these outcomes through interventions that aim to promote rural enterprise and nonfarm job opportunities crucial to livelihood diversification, while also increasing agricultural incomes by increasing farmers’ participation in markets. The latter will be pursued by strengthening market linkages and producer groups (using a value chain approach), promoting agricultural technology adoption, and expanding access to irrigation. E. Project Beneficiaries 50. The expected primary beneficiaries of the project are 4,055,776 households, of which 1,709,617 will be direct project beneficiaries, and 2,346,159 will be indirect ones. The project will mainly target viable farmers (mostly smallholders) engaged in crop and livestock production. These beneficiaries will be from the 181 “high- potential” woredas targeted by the project. The target proportion of direct female beneficiaries (both female head of household and married female) is 40 percent. Farmers will be grouped in producer organizations that have the potential to be linked to markets, either directly or through various outgrower and contract farming schemes. Producer organizations to be supported include rural youth organization such as CIGs, primary cooperatives, and unions. 51. The project will also target a range of secondary beneficiaries, including enterprises along selected agricultural value chains. Secondary beneficiaries will include a variety of value chain actors including people involved in agricultural cooperatives, informal farmers’ organizations, agro-enterprises and SMEs (such as trading and agroprocessing companies), agricultural service providers (public and private), MFIs, and government line departments. In addition, the project will reach a significant number of indirect beneficiaries, including household members and farmers benefitting from improvements in public infrastructure and agricultural service offerings (such as agricultural extension and animal health services), farmers adopting new technologies because of the project, and farmers benefiting from improved input and output markets. III. IMPLEMENTATION ARRANGEMENTS A. Institutional and Implementation Arrangements 52. The project will be managed and coordinated by Ethiopia’s MoA and implemented by a range of decentralized actors. The project will be implemented by regional and decentralized agencies that include the Ethiopian Institute for Agriculture Research (EIAR) and different regional institutes, including the Agricultural Transformation Institute (ATI) (formerly the Agriculture Transformation Agency [ATA]), the Ethiopia Agriculture Authority, and the governments of the eight regional states and two city administrations. In this respect, the project’s implementation will resemble that of its predecessor project, AGP II. The project will establish federal-, regional-, and woreda-level PMUs. The coordination units will be guided by SCs and Technical Committees at each level. At the regional level, dedicated support units will be put in place to assist and oversee implementers on the ground on specific topics. In addition, Ethiopia will establish an MPA national SC to provide policy guidance. The SC will meet at least twice a year to undertake, among other tasks, the review and approval of the draft work plan and budget, approval of the annual report, and review of the status of project implementation progress. 53. To facilitate the implementation of project activities in conflict-affected areas, the MoA will deploy a Page 89 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) TPIA. The GoE has decided to adopt third-party implementation arrangements in all the selected woredas of Tigray. The TPIA will initially be executed for 18 months and will subsequently be reevaluated. The following criteria were deployed to identify activities for the TPIA: (a) immediate needs (activities could address short-term needs of the conflict-affected communities, (b) implementable in 18 months (activities can be completed within a short time frame of 18 months), and (c) independent activity (activities are not contingent on accomplishment of other activities such as prefeasibility study and detailed assessment. Accordingly activities support (a) demonstration of improved agricultural technologies, (b) farmer field days and experience sharing, (c) rehabilitation of farmer training centers, (d) rehabilitation of animal health clinics and post, (e) community-based seed producer groups, (f) fruit seedling producing groups, (g) organizing of poultry production groups, (h) rehabilitation of agricultural office facilities, and (i) provision of women-tailored extension service and promotion of nutrient-dense crops on farmer training centers and farmers’ backyards. For this third-party implementation arrangement, the Government has selected the FAO and its contracting will be a condition for the project effectiveness. The MoA will sign a standard output agreement with the TPIA laying out the relevant procurement, FM, and necessary environment and social risk management procedures. 54. Project planning will be participatory and based on principles of community planning. Priorities will be consolidated into project work plans. Technical assistance will be provided where needed, either through dedicated implementation structures or by technical specialists. 55. Physical infrastructure will be constructed by contractors, and services will be procured, in accordance with World Bank guidelines and privileging community contributions whenever possible. Figure A1.2 summarizes implementation arrangements. Page 90 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Figure A1.2. B. Results Monitoring and Evaluation Arrangements 56. As described under Subcomponent 4.3, the project will develop and implement a robust MEL system to track and assess the project’s progress toward the PDO, generate information that can be used in project management, and document and share project learnings. The M&E system will disaggregate all relevant project data by gender, further distinguishing female participants by age (women and girls) and household type (female- headed and male-headed), and it will analyze how gender gaps evolve over time. The project results MEL system will build on the MoA’s existing M&E system and will be guided by a project-level knowledge management strategy and MEL manual. A project-specific MIS will be established within the MoA’s existing, online M&E MIS platform. The GEMS/KOBO toolbox will be used to supplement the project MIS. Some aspects of M&E will be participatory, engaging the community in tracking project milestones and results and in identifying implementation problems and best practices. 57. Project M&E efforts would be supplemented by World Bank-procured TPM to ensure oversight in areas under the TPIA. The World Bank would contract a qualified TPM entity, subject to trust fund availability, to monitor project progress in Tigray to inform and adapt interventions and implementation modalities. TPM frequency 53In conflict areas, the Government of Ethiopia will contract, at least temporarily, the FAO as Third Party Implementation Agency. These activities will operate under a more centralized approach under the FAO.
