These TPM arrangements may cover the following aspects (among others): implementation progress or completion status (including beneficiary selection, timeliness of implementation, physical verification of infrastructure prepared as part of small-scale climate-resilient infrastructure development, and quality assurance); compliance with the ESF, and the effectiveness of the project’s GRM (including reporting on, and responding to, SEA/SH complaints); and fiduciary compliance, including through monitoring of recurrent costs, O&M, and compliance with relevant expenditure and procurement rules. The contracted agency will be a private or public firm, a civil society organization (CSO), an international NGO, or a UN organization, required to have strong knowledge of the country’s context, a country footprint, experience, ability to establish and enforce effective security systems, ability to develop effective working relationships with government and other implementing entities, relevant technical and sectoral knowledge, ability to integrate technology into monitoring procedures (where relevant), and ability to mobilize rapidly. Contracts will include provisions that require the contracted TPM entity to strengthen the government’s capacity to conduct such tasks at a later stage. IV. APPRAISAL SUMMARY A. Fiduciary Financial Management 58. An FM assessment was carried out for the Ethiopia FSRP. The FM arrangements will be based on the existing FM systems and arrangements established under AGP II. The federal PCU based at the MoA will retain the overall fiduciary responsibility overseeing regions and woredas. The project will prepare consolidated project AWPB for the World Bank’s ‘no-objection’. The project fund flow follows the Government’s channel 2 mechanism whereby the funds will be transferred to the MoA through a segregated DA to be opened at the National Bank of Ethiopia managed by the MoA. The project will also maintain separate local currency bank accounts at all levels. All disbursement methods are available to the project. For the Advance to Designated Account and Reimbursement methods, the project will use report-based disbursements, with submission of quarterly IFRs that include forecasts for advances/replenishment of the DA. The project will submit these quarterly IFRs within 60 days of end of the quarter. The project will have its accounts audited on an annual basis and its financial statement will be audited by an independent external auditor acceptable to the World Bank. The project will submit the annual external auditor’s report within six months of the fiscal year end. Additional details on FM arrangements can be found in annex 7. 59. Though the project will leverage the various strengths of the country’s public financial management system and World Bank-financed projects, there are noted gaps. 54 These include staff turnover and capacity, weak internal audit oversight, internal controls over advances, asset management weaknesses, and low quality of financial reporting particularly from lower levels. Furthermore, AGP II and FSRP implementation will overlap for about one year, affecting the federal and regional levels and 56 woredas, which might result in double dipping of resources. Added risks are also the impact of the conflict in the country and COVID-19 pandemic on the project. The Tigray region is not accessible. As a result, the FM residual risk rating for the implementation of the FSRP is considered Substantial. 54 The former and ongoing World Bank projects, AGP I and II, and other ongoing World Bank operations including Livestock and Fishery Sector Development Project (LFSDP, P159382), Lowlands Livelihood Resilience Project (LLRP, P164336), Resilient Landscapes and Livelihoods Project (RLLP, 163383), Climate Action Through Landscape Management (CALM, P170384), Development Response to Displacement Impacts Project (DRDIP, P161067), and Regional Pastoral Livelihood Resilience Project (RPLRP, P150006). Page 92 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 60. Mitigating measures have been agreed with the government. They include the retaining of dedicated accounting staff and increasing the supervisory capacity of the finance units; additional staffing to internal audit unit and availing of budget needed for reviews; adoption of the FM manual of AGP II; the use of project resources by overlapping woredas only after utilization and reporting on AGP II resources; and the adoption of checklists for monitoring quality of financial reports from lower level. To adequately mitigate the double dipping risk, the project will have (a) a detailed AWPB that will be carefully scrutinized by the task team before approval; (b) the IFRs will have a section to show the detail transfers and expenditure and balances of AGP III accounts; (c) project progress reports will have FM section that reports on progress of AGP II at overlapping woredas and woredas readiness to implement the FSRP; and (d) FM monitoring will target review of transaction to check expenditure double dipping. In addition, bank accounts will not be opened for the project at the 56 overlapping woredas until AGP II bank accounts are closed. At federal and regional levels only activities which are not covered under AGP II will be financed under FSRP until AGP II is fully closed. 61. As noted above, selected activities in conflict areas with difficult access will be done through third-party implementation arrangement with the FAO over an initial period of 18 months. Before the end of that initial period, assessments will be carried out to determine whether implementation can be transferred to government structures. A standard output agreement will be used for this contract. An FM assessment of the government’s capacity to supervise the activities to be carried out by the FAO will be conducted, as necessary, with the objective of determining the risk and assurance provided by the proposed FM arrangements. The assessment should take into account the design of the operation, the country context as it relates to the UN agencies’ arrangements in the field, and any relevant additional information. Progress in these areas will also be monitored during implementation through the third-party monitoring mechanism (TPMM), including for the project fiduciary management arrangement. The third-party monitoring agency (TPMA) will be recruited by the World Bank, subject to availability of trust fund resources. The project will use all modalities of supervision available for the project including GEMS, updated audit TORs with focus on risky areas, and third-party monitoring that is to be used under the project. 62. The conclusion of the FM assessment is that the project’s FM arrangements meet the World Bank's minimum requirements under the World Bank Policy and Bank Directive on IPF and FM manual. A detailed action plan has been developed and agreed to mitigate the risks identified . Procurement 63. The project’s procurement risk is assessed as Substantial. The applicable procedures, agreed procurement arrangements, procurement risks, mitigation measures, procurement arrangements for high or substantial risk contracts, and oversight mechanisms, are described in Annex 8. B. Environmental and Social 64. Certain project activities will give rise to environmental, health, and safety risks and impacts. For example, risks could arise from the construction of SSI schemes and storage facilities and the diffusion of certain productivity-enhancing technologies. Specifically, environmental and social risks could be associated with (a) the inappropriate use and disposal of agrochemicals, including pesticides and chemicals used by agricultural research laboratories; (b) laboratory construction and research activities (health and safety risks and impacts); (c) the inappropriate use of water resources, including their overuse and their contamination with agrochemicals, both of which could affect water quantity and quality in neighboring communities and downstream; (d) unsuitable land management techniques, which could result in the physical and chemical degradation of soils; (e) agricultural water usage and pollution and the introduction of invasive species, all of which can have an impact on biodiversity Page 93 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) and ecosystems; and (f) the construction of small-scale infrastructure such as storage facilities and SSI schemes, which can contribute to air pollution, construction waste pollution, noise pollution, and water pollution—although the construction activities envisaged by the Ethiopia FSRP will be site specific and small in scale. Agricultural activities also generate GHG emissions including methane, nitrous oxide, and carbon dioxide. These emissions arise at different stages in the food production cycle. Furthermore, the project will be implemented in existing agricultural lands and hence will not lead to the conversion of natural habitat. Considering the nature of the activities to be financed by the project and the client’s capacity to manage the risks, the environmental risk of the project is rated as Substantial at this stage. 65. The project triggers the World Bank Policy on “Projects on International Waterways” (OP/BP 7.50) because some of the proposed investments will take place in the Tekeze, Abay, Baro-Akobo, Omo-Ghibe, Genale- Dawa, Wabi-Shebelle, Awash, and the Rift Valley Lakes basins, which are shared by Djibouti, Egypt, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia, South Sudan, and Sudan and hence are international waterways as defined by the policy. All riparian countries were notified on August 21, 2021, and no objections were received. The requirements of OP/BP 7.50 have therefore been met. The process was approved by the Regional Vice President on March 7, 2022. 66. The project is expected to result in social benefits, but its social risk level is high. In particular, it is expected to increase rural employment opportunities (including for youth and women) and skills, improve livelihoods, enhance the resilience of highland farmers, improve access to finance, increase agricultural yields, and increase access to diverse and nutritious foods. However, the social risk of the project is considered high due to the contextual risks and because project-supported activities could result in land acquisition—notably for the construction of agricultural infrastructure such as small-scale and household irrigation, market infrastructures, and rural feeder roads and bridges. The latter may trigger involuntary resettlement (physical and economic displacement), restrictions on access to land, and a loss of livelihoods within affected communities. In addition, although the exact location of the investments will be determined during the project implementation stage, project activities will be implemented in highland and lowland areas with complex socioeconomic and political contexts. 67. To manage potential environmental and social risks, the MoA will put different mitigation measures in place. The ministry has prepared an ESMF in compliance with the requirements of the World Bank’s ESF. As part of the ESMF, various assessments including capacity assessment, conflict analysis and security assessment, and GBV or SEA/SH risks assessment have been conducted and associated mitigation measures and management plans have been developed. The ESMF will be used as a basis for identifying and managing environmental and social risks. Since the exact location of activities is yet to be determined at this stage, the MoA has prepared an ReF in line with the requirements of ESS5 and applicable national legislation. A social assessment (SA) targeting the ESS7 communities as well as other marginalized and vulnerable groups has been conducted incorporating a social development or social risk management plan to address the respective risks and impacts during the preparation stage. Further, site-specific SDPs will also be prepared as part of the preparation of site-specific risks management plans such as ESMPs, RePs, Livelihood Restoration Plans, and so on before the commencement of physical works that likely affect those communities identified as historically underserved people as per the SA or in line with ESS7. In addition, an SEP and LMP have been developed in line with the relevant Environmental and Social Standards (ESSs). The MoA has also prepared an ESCP that outlines its commitment to (a) implementing the project activities in compliance with the requirements of the applicable ESSs of the World Bank’s ESF; (b) developing environmental and social risk management implementation arrangements including the deployment of qualified staff at different levels of implementation; and (c) monitoring and reporting on the implementation of environmental and social risk management tools and plans. All the required environmental and social risk management instruments such as ESMF with SEA/SH action plans, SMP, ReF, and SA including SDP, IPMP, ESCP, SEP, and LMP were disclosed on Page 94 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) April 24, 2022 by the World Bank and in country on April 26, 2022. Given the country security context, the MoA (FSRP - PMU) will conduct/develop and disclose a more detailed stand-alone security risks assessment and security management (SMP) prior to program effectiveness. 68. The MoA will maintain and use the existing environmental and social risk management arrangement (established for AGP II) to manage the anticipated environmental and social risks of the Ethiopia FSRP. The MoA will recruit one environmental specialist and one social risk management specialist to join the federal PIU or retain the existing environmental and social specialists. Similarly, one environmental specialist and one social specialist will be recruited by each regional PCU. For the newly established regions (Sidama and southwest regions), regional PMUs shall be established with qualified staff and resources to support management of ESHS risks and impacts of the Program including qualified social and environment specialists. In addition, focal persons for environmental and social risk management will be assigned to all project implementing woredas and at kebele levels. Also, gender specialist/focal points shall be recruited/assigned at the federal PMU as well as in each of regions having more than four program target woredas. The MoA shall allocate the budget for recruiting all project-level environmental and social risk management staff. 69. Compliance with ESS will be monitored in several ways. Quarterly and annual environmental monitoring reports will be prepared by the regional PMU’s environmental and social experts and reviewed at the federal level, with copy sent to the World Bank. There will be independent annual, midterm, and end-line environmental and social audits. The regional- or woreda-level environmental protection authority (EPA) will review and endorse site- specific environmental and social risk management instruments and monitor compliance with the regulatory requirements. Detailed capacity assessment and capacity-building action plans have been prepared as part of the ESMF preparation. The minimum implementation arrangement will be established before implementing any project-financed activities that may have adverse environmental and social impacts. Relevant EPAs will review and clear environmental and social instruments, which will be prepared based on the updated ESMF, and monitor compliance with the regulatory requirements. ESMPs for subprojects of high and substantial risks such as full and partial ESIAs, RePs, and so on shall be submitted to the World Bank for review and clearance. C. Key Risks 70. The overall risk to achieving the PDO of the proposed operation is Substantial. Possible individual risks are identified, and their ratings are listed in the following paragraphs, along with proposed measures to mitigate their impacts. The experiences gained, challenges encountered, and lessons learned during the preparation and subsequent implementation of other projects have been instrumental in identifying these risks and mitigation measures. 71. Political and governance risks are rated High. The violent conflicts in different parts of the country, and specifically the military escalation in the northern conflict, have exacerbated security risks in the country and have led to increased instability in all regions of Ethiopia. Security challenges and risks (which include access restrictions imposed by the government as well as the operation of non-state armed groups) could play a significant role in delaying project implementation, increasing its costs, and causing damage to investments. Implementation in parts of the country, particularly in the north, will be conditioned by security considerations. To manage this risk, the operation will be guided by the World Bank’s regularly updated overall approach to work in conflict-affected areas to provide for adequacy of security and adherence to fiduciary and social and environmental standards. 72. Macroeconomic risk is rated High. Under a state-led development model, Ethiopia has enjoyed rapid economic growth and reasonable macroeconomic stability for more than 15 years. However, economic imbalances have widened, with merchandise exports as a percentage of GDP in decline since 2011, the crowding Page 95 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) out of the private sector from credit, and a growing parallel market premium on the exchange rate signaling scarcity of hard currency. The ongoing armed conflict is likely to affect agricultural production (and growth) and Ethiopia’s fiscal position. Disruptions to supply chains and overall uncertainty seem to be among the factors contributing to a surge in inflation and a temporary spike in the parallel market rate in early August 2021. The lapse of the Extended Credit Facility component of the IMF program in September 2021 and delays in debt reprofiling arrangements under the G-20 Common Framework exacerbate the macroeconomic risks. In this context, there is also the risk of a potential reversal in agreed macroeconomic reforms. These risks could affect activities that require imports of goods and services as well as where domestic supply chain disruptions increase investment costs and reduce gains resulting from investments targeting economic productivity. Focusing on small- scale investments, relying on a large part on local resources, can partly address these risks. 73. Fiduciary risk is Substantial. The capacity of the MoA in fiduciary management has improved over the years through the implementation of multiple World Bank-financed projects. However, with decentralized implementation, risks of staff turnover, capacity limitations, inadequate internal audit oversight, low-quality reports, and gaps in internal control processes become challenging. The added risk is the impact of the conflict in the country which may affect fiduciary compliance. Mitigating measures proposed in the fiduciary annexes aim to utilize existing systems and strengthen them where needed to minimize the risk. Based on the procurement capacity assessment carried out so far, the procurement risks are related to a lack of adequate procurement capacity (structure and staffing) to accommodate the workload associated with the new project and a lack experience in the contracting and management of the TPIA (specifically within the MoA). Absence of adequate skills in preparing bidding documents, evaluating bids, and contract management and the inaccessibility of the complaint management process and associated delays affect the integrity of the procurement system. The shortage of basic construction inputs and price increases are affecting market responsiveness and the successful completion of contracts. In addition, restrictions associated with COVID-19 and security and fragility situations may affect the interest of potential contractors and consultants (local and international) in responding to bidding opportunities. 74. Climate change-related risk is Substantial. Risks related to climate change, particularly drought and flooding, can critically affect project implementation and the achievement of project outcomes. Recurrent drought, flooding, erratic rainfall, pest, and diseases can weaken the resilience of agricultural communities, causing households to lose some or even all of their assets and ultimately forcing them to migrate or seek alternative livelihood opportunities. Similarly, some structures built by the project—such as for the provision of water and social services or natural resources management—may be destroyed totally or partially by floods, and cropland may be flooded. The mitigation measures to reduce the impacts of climate risk are embedded in the technical design of the project itself, which explicitly aims to reduce the adverse effects of climate change (specifically drought and flooding) by building the resilience of agricultural livelihoods. 75. The project-level GRM will be central to risk mitigation efforts and will help manage grievances from communities or by parties who feel that are or will be adversely affected by the project. The project-level GRM will serve as an avenue for communities to channel their concerns. The client will be supported to establish an accessible, effective, and efficient GRM with the capacity to receive and respond to grievances in local languages and on time. Grievances related to SEA/SH as well as other forms of GBV will get special attention and protocols to enable survivor-centered responses will be put in place. The team will ensure that project-related grievances are shared with the World Bank’s GRS. The team will work with clients to ensure that communities are aware of the multiple forms used to submit grievances to the GRM and the GRS in case they think they are or could be adversely affected by the project. Page 96 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) ANNEX 2: Madagascar Food Systems Resilience Project I. STRATEGIC CONTEXT A. Country Context 1. Poverty in Madagascar remains pervasive and deeply entrenched. Following hard-fought gains in recent decades, poverty is again increasing. The average Malagasy was more than 40 percent poorer in 2020 than they were in 1961. Due to downside pressures from the global pandemic, the share of Malagasies living on less than US$1.9 per day was expected to reach 81.1 percent in 2021, up from 80.7 percent in 2020. With more than four in five people living in poverty, Madagascar has one of the highest poverty rates in the world. Its poverty rate also far exceeds the global average of 10 percent and the regional average of 40 percent. At this pace, Madagascar is far off track in regard to achieving SDG 1 and all but a handful of the other goals by 2030. 2. Before the COVID-19 pandemic, Madagascar was on a modest growth trajectory. Growth peaked at 4.4 percent in 2019 and averaged 3.5 percent over 2013–2019, barely exceeding population growth and remaining insufficient to ensure rapid poverty reduction. The pandemic led to the deepest recession since 2002 and reversed more than a decade of gains. The initial impact of the COVID-19 crisis was severe, with the collapse of export earnings and private investment leading to a contraction of GDP of 7.1 percent in 2020, the strongest in two decades. A second wave of the pandemic in 2021 and continued border closures have delayed recovery, with growth estimated by the World Bank at 4.4 percent in 2021. Overall, per capita income fell by about 8 percent between 2019 and 2021, making the COVID-19 crisis the most intense economic shock since the crises of 1991 and 2002. The economic effects of the pandemic have been compounded by several consecutive seasons of below-average rainfall, which have fueled a severe food security crisis in the south of the country. B. Sector and Institutional Context 3. Farming, fishing, and forestry remain the backbone of Madagascar’s rural economy, but sustained growth remains elusive. Roughly four in five Malagasies, most of them small subsistence farmers, rely on agriculture for their livelihoods. Rice is the main staple food, accounting for 70 percent of the country’s total agricultural production and more than half of per capita daily calorie intake, but not enough is produced to feed a growing population. Alongside per capita production of rice, the sector’s value added has steadily declined by roughly one-third since its 1986 peak, amid sluggish and volatile growth rates. For a sector that accounts for roughly 64 percent of total employment, its share of total exports (37 percent) and GDP (23 percent) are markedly limited. 4. Underinvestment and deep-seated structural deficiencies impede the performance of agriculture and food systems and the sector’s growth prospects. Low labor, land, and soil productivity persist, for example, from low uptake of improved technology such as high-yielding seed, fertilizer, and agricultural machinery; limited storage and logistical capacities; poorly maintained water management and irrigation infrastructure; and a lack of public investment in agricultural research and extension and veterinary and animal health services. Add to this underdeveloped and poorly maintained road networks that constrain farmers’ connectivity and access to markets. Taken together, these constraints lead to low farmgate prices that sap farmers’ incentives to reinvest in their farming activities and produce a marketable surplus. Opaque and distortive public support policies stifle private investments that could otherwise fuel value chain development, agricultural diversification, and job growth. Moreover, gender inequality impedes women’s and girls’ access to productive assets and services (such as land, equipment, finance, and extension) and, in turn, their contributions to sector growth. Page 97 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 5. The unsustainable exploitation and degradation of Madagascar’s forests, soils, and other natural resources are also accelerating biodiversity loss and threatening the viability of food systems and rural livelihoods. For decades, agricultural production has increased mainly through the expansion of cultivated areas rather than the use of improved technologies or inputs. Traditional practices such as “slash-and-burn“, which convert forests into cropland, pasture, and other agriculture-related uses, continue to fuel rapid deforestation, land and soil erosion, and the loss of ecosystem services critical to sustaining food production and rural livelihoods. Many of Madagascar’s dry forests have already been exploited for agriculture, pasture, firewood, and construction materials. With deforestation affecting an average of 90,000 ha per year, biodiversity is under critical threat. Deforestation and forest degradation debilitate biological activity that plays an essential role in protecting the soil surface from erosion, conserving soil nutrients, and maintaining healthy conditions for plant growth. The degradation of forest cover and rangelands decreases water infiltration and recharge, increases runoff and erosion, reduces pollination, and reduces the fertility and water retention capacity of soils. The result is declining productivity, pushing hungry populations to abandon their fields in search of more fertile cropland elsewhere. 6. The declining performance of agricultural systems is undermining Madagascar’s food supply and food and nutrition security. In 2021, Madagascar joined Haiti, Nigeria, South Sudan, Yemen, and 23 other food security “hot-spot” countries around the globe that are struggling to feed themselves. Among these countries, Madagascar is the only one where conflict is not among the key drivers of food insecurity. While over one-third of Malagasy households lack adequate food at any given time of the year, the country’s southern regions are particularly prone to food insecurity. As of February 2022, a multiyear drought has resulted in one of the worst food crises in decades, threatening more than 1.64 million people in the country’s Grand Sud (IPC January 2022). 7. Unsustainable and increasingly fragile food systems are failing to feed and nourish Madagascar’s growing population. Madagascar faces chronic food insecurity and the highest malnutrition rates in Africa. About one-quarter of Madagascar’s population is undernourished (up from about 20 percent in the 1990s), meaning that its food intake fails to meet dietary requirements.55 On a national scale, 53 percent of rural households do not consume the amount or variety of nutritious foods they need to maintain an active and healthy life. Consequently, Madagascar has the fourth-highest rate of chronic malnutrition. Almost 40 percent children under five are stunted56 and nearly 8 percent are wasted (Global Nutrition Report 2020), meaning that 1.6 million children never reach their full physical and mental potential. 8. Malnutrition is high across the country. Poverty and acute malnutrition are most widespread in the south of Madagascar, but the highest rates of chronic malnutrition are found in the northern rice-producing regions where dietary diversity is low. Consumption of nutrient- and protein-rich vegetable products and meat, dairy, and other animal products is limited in most parts of the country. More than one in three women of reproductive age have anemia. Child malnutrition is primarily a result of maternal malnutrition and many children are born stunted, with severe implications for human capital development. Malnutrition is estimated to cost the country 7–12 percent of its GDP. 9. Climate change is expected to fuel more extreme and more volatile weather, amplifying pressure on Madagascar’s fragile food systems. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change’s Sixth Assessment Report suggests a near-term and persistent El Niño-like shift in the climate characterized by warmer eastern Pacific Ocean conditions, warmer air temperatures, and more frequent droughts over southern Madagascar. Increasing Indo- Pacific volatility is expected to bring more frequent and stronger El Niño, La Niña, and Indian Ocean Dipole events. 55 See Fill the Nutrient Gap Madagascar, World Food Programme, October 2016. 56 According to the Demographic and Health Survey, USAID, 2021. Page 98 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Warmer air temperatures will both increase desiccation during droughts and contribute to more extreme rainfall and flooding during storms and cyclones. 10. Fragile governance, high poverty rates, and geographic isolation are among factors that undermine Madagascar’s capacity, and the capacity of the country’s smallholder producers, to adapt to a variable and changing climate. Between 1980 and 2010, 53 natural hazards including droughts, earthquakes, epidemics, floods, cyclones, and extreme temperatures affected Madagascar, causing over US$1 billion in economic damage. In early 2022, four powerful tropical storms hit Madagascar in as many weeks, affecting the food and nutrition security of millions of the country’s most vulnerable households across nine regions along the country’s exposed Indian Ocean coastline.57 An impact assessment carried out in their wake estimated damages and losses amounting to US$657.5 million or 4.8 percent of Madagascar’s 2020 GDP.58 Agricultural activities and assets absorbed nearly 42 percent of the impact. High poverty rates and a lack of functional institutions along with decades of underinvestment in roads, agricultural research and innovation, extension, and other basic services have increased the vulnerability of farming communities, especially women and other marginalized groups, undermining their capacity to mitigate, cope with, and recover from climatic and other shocks. 11. Moving toward a more efficient and sustainable management of the country’s natural resources (land, soil, rangelands, forests, and fisheries) remains paramount to safeguarding and improving the performance of its food systems. There is a need to restore the functions of landscape ecosystems affected by agricultural practices and address related drivers of land degradation. Land restoration efforts and integrated land management approaches are needed to simultaneously improve vegetation and water availability and quality while raising the productivity and resilience of agrifood systems and rural livelihoods. Other priority actions include (a) improving the performance and maintenance of irrigation and road infrastructure; (b) enhancing farmer access to quality inputs such as certified seed and fertilizers and information services (for example, weather, agro-advisory, and price); (c) promoting the wide adoption of CSA technologies and practices that can at once increase yields, improve resilience, and lower the carbon footprint of agriculture and food systems; and (d) diversifying the incomes of rural households beyond subsistence agriculture, such as support to animal husbandry. 12. Three dimensions of regional integration in which Madagascar can improve are trade, infrastructural, and productive integration. As measured by the Africa Regional Integration Index,59 Madagascar is stronger than average in two of the five dimensions of integration: macroeconomic integration and the free movement of people. CAADP is the region’s key policy framework, and under it, participating countries including Madagascar have committed to pursuing food security and food resilience at the regional level through a coordinated approach. Its pillars are food supply and hunger, sustainable land and water management, market access, and agricultural research. 13. Building food systems resilience at the regional level will strongly benefit from regional coordination and cooperation—notably on trade policy and agroclimatic research—but the building blocks of regional food systems resilience are also national. A collapse in food security in just one or a subset of MPA countries can—by leading, for example, to regional supply shortages, price hikes, and border closings—reverberate throughout the region. Conversely, relatively food-abundant countries in the region can supply to others experiencing deficits of 57 In January 2022, the cyclone season in the southwest Indian Ocean intensified, causing loss of life, widespread flooding, wind damage, landslides, and massive destruction of infrastructure and property. Madagascar was particularly affected, with four storms occurring in four weeks. TD Invest 93S was followed by TC Batsirai, Moderate TS Dumako, and TC Emnati (which passed through areas that were also affected by TC Batsirai). 58 Global Rapid Damage Estimation (GRADE) Report - Madagascar, March 2022. 59 The Regional Integration Index is a product of the AUC, United Nations Economic Commission for Africa, and the African Development Bank. https://www.integrate-africa.org/ Page 99 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) volume or variety, making not only trade but also national production, agroprocessing, and marketing capacity important potential contributors to regional food systems resilience, especially in the context of a changing climate. However, for food markets to function efficiently and arbitrate supply and demand between countries and within countries, adequate road networks and other marketing infrastructure is critical. 14. Meanwhile, if trade is a manifestation of market-based resilience opportunities that lie beyond national borders, Madagascar has not fully exploited that potential—to sell and buy from other African countries. While Madagascar trades with 45 countries on the African continent, trade levels are relatively low. With growing middle classes and changing consumption patterns, several countries in the region might be prospective export markets for some of the specialty products the island is famous for—for example, vanilla, lychees, honey, groundnuts, cocoa paste, green coffee, spices, horticulture, and fiber crops. Madagascar also has substantial fishery and aquaculture potential, and its shrimp and crab exports are significant. While it trades with 45 countries across the African continent, the range of products and volumes are relatively limited. In 2019, Africa represented roughly 11 percent of Madagascar’s food exports, destined for primarily Kenya (57.5 percent), Morocco (18.3 percent), and Mauritius (10.8 percent). In 2020, Africa was the third-largest origin (39 percent) for imports into Madagascar, behind Asia and Europe; imports are dominated by South Africa (34 percent, primarily wheat), Mauritius (26.6 percent), and Egypt (17.4 percent). 15. The package of interventions under the Madagascar Food Systems Resilience Project (Madagascar FSRP) aims to at once strengthen the productivity and resilience of Madagascar’s food systems and support their stronger connection to regional markets. The project mobilizes an integrated set of interventions, which together will not only help producers better access domestic markets but also strengthen value chains in ways that will allow them to link to regional markets. This set of interventions ranges from improving crop and livestock production, land, and water management and promoting irrigation to upgrading rural road networks,60 revitalizing agricultural research and innovation systems, and harmonizing food quality, safety, and other standards with other countries in AFE. The different activities planned under the project complement one another and are all necessary to help Madagascar better integrate with the African region and accelerate socioeconomic development. C. Relevance to Higher-Level Objectives 16. The Madagascar FSRP is timely in light of the country’s increasingly fragile food systems and food security situation. The multiyear drought has brought renewed attention to the need for identifying long-term solutions to recurrent food crisis in the country’s Grand Sud and increasing food insecurity. The AU has pushed to make 2022 the “Year of Nutrition and Food Security” amid increasing reports linking climate change to food insecurity and coming off the momentum of COP27, which is being referred to as the “Africa COP.” 17. The Madagascar FSRP is well aligned with Madagascar’s and the World Bank’s existing strategies. In particular, the project will contribute to the main objectives of the WBG’s Madagascar CPF for the period FY17-21 (Report No. 114744-MG) discussed by the Board on June 5, 2017) and with the Government of Madagascar’s development plan for 2019–2023, the “Plan Emergence Madagascar (PEM).” Overall, the CPF seeks to increase the resilience of the most vulnerable people and promote inclusive growth while strengthening national and local institutions to reduce fragility. As envisioned, the Madagascar FSRP will also help further the PEM’s Commitment 60 Madagascar has one of the least developed road networks in the world. Road density is only 5.4 km per 100 km 2 of land, and most of its national and local roads are earth roads and in poor condition. Its Rural Accessibility Index (measured by the share of the rural population who live within 2 km of an all-season road) is 11.4 percent—among the lowest globally—which means that 17 million Malagasy rural residents are left unconnected. Page 100 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 12, which focuses on actions to sustainably achieve productivity gains, strengthen food and nutrition security, and improve access to domestic markets. The project also aligns with the World Bank’s new Climate Change Action Plan (2021–2025), which aims to increase support for adaptation and resilience. Activities to be financed under the Madagascar FSRP will also seek to facilitate the adaptation of Madagascar’s food systems so that they can make a positive and lasting contribution to delivering on the World Bank’s 2030 vision of healthy people, a healthy planet, and a healthy economy characterized by inclusive incomes, jobs, and livelihoods. 18. Consistent with the MPA’s focus on gender, a special emphasis will be placed on empowering women and youth. The Madagascar FSRP will specifically seek to address inequities that persist between women and men in terms of access to resources and economic opportunities.61 Although women are actively engaged in the agricultural economy, several factors continue to undermine their full participation. They include insufficient access to productive resources (including land, credit, training, and advisory services), low human capital, limited access to markets, and a legal framework that renders women financially dependent on their spouses. To address inequities, applications from women and youth for postgraduate (master’s and doctoral) studies will be encouraged and prioritized under the project through a robust outreach campaign to identify candidates. About 60 percent of available scholarships and professional training courses will be reserved for qualified women applicants and priority placements will be reserved for women and youth. To help close the gender gap in access to agricultural inputs, credit, and services, female beneficiaries and women’s groups will also be prioritized to receive subsidized inputs (fertilizers and seeds) and business development grants. Household nutrition and savings will be improved by helping women, women’s groups, and youth establish homestead and community gardens and supporting village savings and loans programs. II. PROJECT DESCRIPTION A. Project Development Objective 19. The PDO of the Madagascar FSRP is to increase the resilience of food systems and preparedness for food insecurity in project areas. 20. Enhancing the performance of Madagascar’s agricultural sector and food systems is vital not only to reducing malnutrition and chronic food insecurity but also to creating jobs, raising rural incomes, and reducing poverty. The ambition is not only to achieve higher agricultural productivity but also to sustain higher levels of productivity while reducing the sector’s environmental footprint and unsustainable use of natural resources. Instead of remaining a primary driver of deforestation, biodiversity loss, and GHG emissions, agriculture can become part of the solution to poverty reduction and resilient and sustainable economic growth. By promoting the adoption of climate-smart technologies and practices and empowering communities to directly and more sustainably manage natural ecosystems and resources (land, pastures, forests, soil, and water), more productive and resilient agricultural systems will not only meet the food, nutritional, and livelihood needs of Madagascar’s population but will also become a critical driver of economic diversification, transformation, and growth. 61 The Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development’s 2019 Social Institutions and Development Index (SIGI) 61 ranks Madagascar as “high” in institutional gender-based discrimination (with a 48 percent rating, compared to South Africa at 22 percent or Yemen at 64 percent). According to SIGI, the legal framework for land tenure is relatively gender equal, but customary law and traditional practices often supersede it, and enforcement is weak. As a result, 85 percent of land is held in men’s names, which perpetuates gender inequalities that affect women’s access to productive resources, credit, and other services. Page 101 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) B. Project Results Indicators 21. Progress toward the PDO will be measured by five indicators. They are: (a) reduction in food insecure people in project-targeted areas (percentage); (b) farmers adopting supported climate-smart innovations and practices (number, and percentage of which female and climate resilient); (c) increase in land area under sustainable landscape management practices (hectares); (d) increase in volume of agricultural production sold on domestic and regional markets (percentage); and (e) policy products related to agriculture, natural resources management, and food systems resilience adopted with the project’s support (number). Project Components 22. The Madagascar FSRP prioritizes activities designed to address the underlying drivers of food security crises and related climate vulnerabilities and to strengthen the productivity and resilience of national and regional food systems. The project builds on the World Bank’s broad experience supporting agriculture and land management investments in Madagascar. Proposed interventions are informed by, and seek to build upon, past and current investments such as the Irrigation Development and Watershed Management Project (BVPI, P074086), the PADAP (P154698), and CASEF (P151469). BVPI aimed to increase productivity, improve water resources management capacity, and restore associated watersheds in central and northern regions of Madagascar. The Madagascar FSRP will also build on the World Bank’s broader experience in the Africa region, as elsewhere, in helping governments revitalize national agricultural research and innovation systems, strengthen the collection and management of information and data, develop and deploy robust early warning and decision- support tools, and mobilize evidence-based public support programs. The selection of technologies will also be guided in part by activities from the approved list in Annex A.C.1 of the 2020 Joint Report on Multilateral Development Banks’ Climate Finance.62 Mitigation technologies promoted in the project conform to the list in Table 5 of Common Principles for Climate Mitigation Finance Tracking for Multilateral Development Banks. 63 Priority value chains—selected with consideration for their export and import substitution potential, their potential contributions to nutrition, and other criteria—include those for rice, maize, sorghum, cassava, cowpea, sweet potato, livestock, and milk. Component 1: (Re-)Building Resilient Agricultural Production Capacity (US$27.1 million IDA equivalent) 23. Aligned with Pillar 2 of the MPA, Component 1 of the Madagascar FSRP aims to strengthen the productivity and resilience of food production to shocks and stressors. This will be achieved by facilitating improved research and innovations development and strengthening service, information, and technology delivery systems. It will finance agricultural R&D and the deployment of CSA innovations as well as the development of pluralistic and farmer-responsive delivery mechanisms. This component will finance infrastructure, goods and equipment, capacity building, and technical assistance for public or semi-public goods and services such as 62 ERBD et al. (2021), 2020 Joint Report on Multilateral Development Banks’ Climate Finance. 63 https://www.eib.org/attachments/documents/mdb_idfc_mitigation_common_principles_en.pdf. Page 103 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) agricultural research and information systems, extension and advisory services, and risk management tools. The fundamental investment in the research system is critical to developing climate-smart agricultural technologies to help generate productivity gains while minimizing emission intensity; increasing carbon sequestration through testing and deploying new technologies; and reducing overall carbon emissions from the agricultural sector. To enhance service delivery, the project will pilot or scale up the use of public-private approaches including aggregation and input voucher schemes (Dokany Mora ho an’ny Mpamokatra) and agribusiness centers (for example, CABIZ). Component 1 will also provide matching and innovation grants through the Agriculture Development Fund (FDA) and leverage other financing mechanisms (microcredit and credit guarantees) to incentivize the use and delivery of resilience-boosting goods and services at the farm level. Examples include the adoption of climate-adapted varieties and breeds, water- and energy-efficient crop production and storage technologies, SSI kits, equipment and machinery leasing services, and certified seed multiplication. Subcomponent 1.1: Supporting Climate-Informed and Pluralistic Seed Systems 24. Subcomponent 1.1 aims to strengthen the development and delivery of improved varieties of food and fodder crops: ones that are high yielding, climate resilient, and nutrient rich. Activities will seek to upgrade Madagascar’s informal seed systems. The project will support the development of a national seed roadmap for selected crops and stress-tolerant and climate-adapted varieties (for example, rice, maize, sorghum, cassava, sweet potato, and cowpeas), building up the capacity of seed regulators in quality assurance and identifying and addressing other binding constraints impeding development of the seed value chain. The subcomponent will also increase farmers’ access, through matching grants and other credit support mechanisms such as loan guarantee facilities, to source technologies (including early generation seeds and disease- and insect-free tissue cultures), helping them both mitigate their emissions and adapt to the changing climate. It will provide training and capacity building, including on climate-resilient seeds and crop and livestock management practices, and marketing support to seed producers including private companies and farmer-based groups. It will reinforce the technical and operational capacity of national agricultural research centers such as Foibe FIkarohana ampiharina Fampandrosoana ny eny Ambanivohitra (FOFIFA) and Fiompiana Fambolena Malagasy Norvéziana (FIFAMANOR) for safeguarding and ensuring the provision of pre-basic and basic seed. It will rehabilitate and upgrade public and community-based centers for seed multiplication, processing, storage, and marketing. It will strengthen seed research by facilitating and funding collaborative research alliances with One CGIAR (for example, AfricaRice, the International Institute of Tropical Agriculture, the International Potato Center, the International Crops Research Institute for the Semi-Arid Tropics; and the International Livestock Research Institute) and other partners. These, in turn, will identify and promote innovations including higher-nutrient (such as orange-fleshed sweet potato), higher-yielding, and climate-resilient seed varieties, and storage and agroprocessing technologies, using various participatory demonstration models involving, for example, farmer field schools (FFS), lead farmers, seed multiplication centers, and cooperatives of seed producers. Subcomponent 1.2: Supporting Enhanced Access to Climate-Smart Agricultural Mechanization 25. Subcomponent 1.2 will enhance smallholders’ access to mechanization. Farm mechanization is crucial to boosting input use efficiency (seeds, fertilizers, and fuel) along with increased production and enhanced productivity.64 This subcomponent will support the development and delivery of equipment that can be used in land preparation, seeding (direct sowing), fertilization, weeding, harvest, the postharvest management (threshing and processing), and the overall production of target crops. These technologies will help maintain healthy soils 64A Government of India (1996) study revealed that the adoption of suitable mechanizing technologies can lead to (a) increased farm production and productivity by 10–15 percent, (b) higher cropping intensity by 5–20 percent, (c) savings in seeds up to 15–20 percent, (d) savings in fertilizer and chemicals up to 15–20 percent, and (e) reduction in time and labor up to 20–30 percent. Page 104 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) and manage water and inputs efficiently, thereby improving resilience and carbon sequestration. The subcomponent will focus its support on the development and commercialization of enhanced input use and energy-efficient and sustainable mechanization concepts that can help reduce labor and overall emissions. It will provide matching grants to agricultural equipment vendors to expand their offerings, their market reach, and after-sale services. Criteria for matching grants will include the adoption of energy-efficient farm implements that reduce carbon emissions. The subcomponent will also support the development of a digital platform linking smallholder farmers, producer groups, and agribusinesses to mechanization services. And it will strengthen the capacity of the Center of Agriculture Mechanization (Centre de Fabrication et de Formation et d’Application du Machinisme et de la Mécanisation Agricole [CFFAMMA]). In collaboration with One CGIAR, the project will support the development of innovative agricultural equipment and machinery (for example, rice transplanting machinery, threshers, and parboiling and peeling equipment) that can help increase efficiency, reduce emissions, and overcome impediments to productivity and resilience—including by reducing post-harvest losses. Subcomponent 1.3: Strengthening Agricultural Production, Price, and Weather Data Management and Information Systems 26. Subcomponent 1.3 will support the development of data and digital information systems to help manage weather and market risks and better inform decision-making among agricultural value chain actors. It will help develop systems for collecting, assessing, managing, and disseminating information and advice among actors ranging from smallholder producers to livestock herders, agroprocessors, and policymakers. The data will pertain to real-time weather or forecasted weather; crop production and food supply; animal and plant health (such as pest or animal disease outbreaks); the prices and availability of seeds, fertilizers, and food stocks and other market information; soil health; and other relevant information that would facilitate better climate risk management and adaptation planning. The subcomponent will also build capacity at the national level for agrometeorological and hydrometeorological services (public, private, and academic ones) to (a) gather data on hydrometeorological phenomena to complement regional and global data and infrastructure for climate risk management and (b) provide impact-based forecasting, warning, and advisory services to farming communities to increase the climate resilience of farming communities and help them better anticipate and respond to threats. Investments in digital agricultural services will support more efficient and climate-resilient farming systems by, for example, enabling farmers to make timely and informed decisions in response to agrometeorological and climate forecasts or fostering the more judicious use of fertilizer, improving soil fertility management and potentially contributing to carbon sequestration. By helping to enhance efficiency across agrifood supply chains, they are also expected to help reduce levels of food loss and waste. The digital delivery of agricultural services will also help reduce the need for face-to-face interaction with extension agents, thereby reducing field travel and related fossil fuel emissions. Component 2: Supporting the Sustainable Development of Natural Resources for Resilient Agricultural Landscapes (US$44.8 million IDA equivalent) 27. Aligned with Pillar 3 of the MPA, the objective of this component is to promote participatory planning and more sustainable, community-led natural resources and irrigation infrastructure management, to reduce or reverse the adverse impacts of climate change. Better management of the natural resource base (water, land, soil, and vegetation) at both the landscape and farm levels is critical to improving food security, building climate resilience, and enhancing carbon sequestration. Component activities will facilitate the improved use of water, soils, and forests and the restoration of degraded resources to enhance the natural functions and natural capital base that underpin more productive and resilient food systems. Page 105 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 28. Activities financed under this component will promote the sustainable management of critical watersheds. Climate-induced increase in surface temperatures can affect hydrologic processes of a watershed system, making the protection and sustainable management of watersheds critical for the provision of ecosystem services like food and fiber production, carbon storage, erosion control, nutrient cycling, and biodiversity conservation. This component will focus on watersheds that play an important role in irrigated and rainfed agriculture and the protection of biodiversity and on other opportunities to improve water management, irrigation services, and the productivity of natural resources. Privileging integrated, participatory, and decentralized approaches, Component 2 will contribute to (a) protecting watersheds by reducing erosion and sedimentation and increasing water infiltration; (b) increasing the productivity and sustainability of agricultural production based on the adoption of agroecological and improved agroforestry reforestation practices; and (c) building the capacity of communities to implement landscape or watershed upgrades and the capacity of WUAs to sustainably manage irrigation infrastructure. The project will finance the study and implementation of an innovative approach that would involve not only WUAs but also cooperatives and CIGs in compliance with Madagascar’s law (2014-042) on the rehabilitation and O&M of irrigation infrastructure. The component will focus on investments expected to benefit community groups and have positive long-term environmental impacts, including GHG sequestration and emission reductions. Component 2 has three subcomponents. Subcomponent 2.1: Promoting Participatory Planning and Strengthening the Knowledge Base 29. Subcomponent 2.1 will promote participatory planning and strengthen the knowledge base for more sustainable and resilient natural resources management. It will support planning and capacity building relating to the sustainable management of watersheds. It will specifically support the preparation of watershed, sub- watershed, and local management plans with climate adaptation and mitigation as central objectives. Integrating potential changes in hydrology and temperatures due to climate change in these planning exercise will ensure that the recommended investments will be climate resilient and more sustainable in the long term. It will also support (a) institutional and community-based capacity for sustainable NRM, including among WUAs; (b) forest management and landscape restoration, including the rehabilitation of degraded lands, reforestation, fire management, terracing, agroforestry, and productive forestry for energy and construction wood; and (c) planning, mapping, data collection, and knowledge generation for enhancing climate resilience and increasing carbon sequestration. The subcomponent will generate baseline data on soil carbon, forest cover, land use, water use and demand, water and sediment flow, the status and diversification of livelihoods, and GHG emissions from land use and land use change. It will also raise awareness among project beneficiaries of the negative impacts of bushfires, slash-and-burn agriculture, and excessive burning or charcoal production in risky zones. Particular emphasis will be placed on addressing the needs of women and other vulnerable groups with regard to forest management and landscape restoration. Subcomponent 2.2: Investing in Sustainable Land and Water Management for Enhanced Climate Resilience 30. Subcomponent 2.2 will focus on investments in productive assets with a focus on water resources management, water services, and watershed management for climate adaptation and mitigation. It will support (a) practices that promote soil and water conservation including agronomic measures and CSA practices; (b) stream and groundwater control including small-scale storage; (c) rainwater harvesting, natural catchment protection, riverbank restoration, agroforestry, silvopastoral systems, and sustainable rural energy to restore forest cover and groundwater recharge;65 and (d) natural resources-based livelihoods such as nontimber forest products, beekeeping, green charcoal, and nature-based tourism (working with community groups). The 65 Biomass or alternative energy to protect woodlands. Page 106 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) subcomponent will generally seek to increase the use of trees and woodlots in agricultural landscapes through the promotion of reforestation, improved grazing, and agroforestry and agroecological approaches. These interventions also aim to promote positive behavioral change among beneficiary communities to protect the natural resource base upstream and downstream. This will contribute to sustainable adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices and carbon sequestration and emission reduction benefits. Subcomponent 2.3. Improving Water Management and Irrigation Services 31. Subcomponent 2.3 will rehabilitate and improve existing gravity-fed irrigation sites and support the adoption of energy-efficient (for example, solar-powered) SSI systems. Irrigation can be a powerful tool for adapting to climate change and facing drought and flooding conditions, to the extent that it can help ensure a more regular and reliable supply of water for agriculture. In that respect, investments financed under this subcomponent will enhance the ability of farmers to manage the adverse effects of marked seasonal variations in water availability attributable to climate change, thus enhancing the resilience of agricultural production. Using proven and innovative approaches, the subcomponent will prioritize the rehabilitation and sustainable management of public infrastructure such as dams and dykes that are highly vulnerable to climate shocks and susceptible to seeing their performance deteriorate due to a lack of regular upkeep. The subcomponent will facilitate the operationalization of the Hydro-Agricultural Network Maintenance Fund ( Fonds de Remise en État et d’Entretien des Réseaux Hydro-Agricole) so that major infrastructure can benefit from regular maintenance (every three to four years) and be repaired when damaged. The subcomponent will also contribute to improving water governance by building the capacity of WUAs. Component 3: Improving Market Connectivity and Access for Smallholders (US$64.2 million IDA equivalent) 32. Aligned with Pillar 4 of the MPA, Component 3 of the Madagascar FSRP will promote the inclusion of smallholder farmers and rural communities in crop and livestock value chains and more efficient food markets. Focusing on the project’s priority value chains, the subcomponent will enhance the business skills and entrepreneurial capacity of CIGs, including among youth and women members, and support their participation in agricultural value chains. Priority value chains—selected with consideration for their export and import substitution potential, their potential contributions to nutrition, and other criteria—include those for rice, maize, sorghum, cassava, cowpea, sweet potato, livestock, and milk. Support will be provided in the form of training, matching grants, and loan guarantees. Project support will be offered with systematic consideration for climate adaptation and mitigation, environmental, social responsibility, and nutritional considerations. The subcomponent will help strengthen households’ resilience to changing climate. Subcomponent 3.1: Promoting Diversification and Value Addition for Better Income, Nutritional, and Health Outcomes 33. Subcomponent 3.1 aims to develop, adapt, and deliver agroprocessing technologies that enhance the availability and quality of healthy food products and related income opportunities, thereby contributing to household climate resilience. Improved commercialization is a core adaptation strategy, and it can be pursued in ways that enhance nutritional outcomes. While stimulating the development and consumption of higher-quality, nutrient-rich, and healthy foods, this subcomponent will support new income generation and employment opportunities, including among women and youth. The subcomponent will finance, directly and through matching grants and loan guarantee programs, activities, materials, and equipment that (a) enhance the nutritional value of rice through improved varieties and improved processing methods (such as the fortification of locally popular varieties with zinc, iron, and vitamins, and mini-parboiling) and (b) facilitate the development of innovative food products using tubers, cereals, and legumes (for example, cassava and sweet potato flour or fortified infant food) Page 107 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) and feed products (using cassava peel for example) to be promoted in villages, towns, and urban markets, including through nutritional centers—and potentially to be scaled up at the regional level. Subcomponent 3.2: Rehabilitation and Decentralized Maintenance of Rural Feeder Roads for Improved Market Access and Resilient Value Chains 34. Subcomponent 3.2 will finance the rehabilitation of infrastructure to improve the transportation of agricultural products to markets and rural producers’ access to market opportunities. Functioning roads are an important determinant of farmers’ resilience and ability to withstand shocks. A lack of them is a key impediment to the development and resilience of priority value chains. By giving farmers timely access to more markets and helping reduce food losses along the way, better roads are one of the keys to enhancing famers’ incomes and resilience. The subcomponent will rehabilitate feeder roads that small-scale producers including rural women use to access inputs and output markets. It will do so using weather-resistant paving material that can withstand extreme weather events and more generally follow climate-smart design principles and building codes. Where required, wider drains and culverts will be built, and slopes will be stabilized, in anticipation of more frequent and intense precipitation and flooding (under the effects of climate change). Road selection will be based on an assessment of potential economic returns, with consideration for the production potential of the catchment area, the number of producers who will benefit, and existing or potential market linkages, among other criteria. The selection process will also take into account regions’ and communes’ priorities, to align investments with local development plans in coordination with the heads of decentralized offices of the MoA’s Regional Directorate of Agriculture and Livestock (Direction Régionale de l’Agriculture et de l’Elevage [DRAE]) and the Ministry of Public Works (Direction Régionale des Travaux Publics). Subcomponent 3.3: Construction and Rehabilitation of Public Laboratories and Equipment 35. Subcomponent 3.3 will finance the rehabilitation and construction of public laboratories as well as their procurement of equipment. It aims to enable public laboratories to take part in quality and SPS standard controls, epidemio-surveillance, seed certification, fertilizer and pesticide quality control, and other agrifood value chain support functions. The subcomponent will integrate design standards and building codes that account for climate resilience. Other climate adaptation and mitigation measures that will be used in building laboratories will include designs and materials with lower embedded GHG emissions, integrating, for example, water recycling infrastructure, thermal protection through green roofs and shading, the use of energy-efficient mechanical and electrical equipment, and the installation of renewable energy sources. Construction and rehabilitation activities will be guided by performance or gap assessments pertaining to the implementation of specific standards or based on a call for proposals to laboratories. Subcomponent 3.4: Strengthening Priority Value Chains and Linkages to Regional Markets 36. Subcomponent 3.4 will strengthen priority value chains and linkages to regional markets. Its purpose is to enable subsistence farmers to not only generate a surplus and diversify their production into higher value crops but also to get their surplus to markets and receive better prices for their products, enhancing the resilience of food access among both buyers and sellers. This subcomponent will support these outcomes by investing in cold chain facilities and post-harvest storage infrastructure and by addressing infrastructure bottlenecks that affect priority value chains. It will also establish a platform for the aggregation, marketing, and promotion of export- ready products such as rice, beans, and essential oils, making it easier for regional entrepreneurs to import agricultural products from Madagascar. Various standards and best practice guidelines will be updated to align with regional and international ones (for example, good agricultural practices, good manufacturing practices, Page 108 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) hazard analysis and critical control points [HACCP], and international standards and guidelines relating to food safety, gender equity, and child labor). At the same time, the subcomponent will build capacity of farmers, farmer organizations, government officials, and agribusiness entrepreneurs to adopt or disseminate new or more demanding practices and standards. In parallel, the project will promote the digitization of government services and information and communication systems by, for example, supporting the development of integrated agricultural data platforms and piloting traceability systems. These developments will facilitate regional entrepreneurs’ access to Malagasy markets and Malagasy entrepreneurs’ access to regional ones. By investing in post-harvest infrastructure, market aggregation and integration, and more generally in supply chain efficiency, this subcomponent is expected to reduce food losses, potentially mitigating climate risk by reducing food-related GHG emissions, enhancing agricultural incomes, and improving food availability and access and household resilience. Component 4: Project Coordination, Knowledge Management, and Dissemination (US$22.0 million IDA equivalent) 37. Aligned with Pillar 5 of the MPA, Component 4 of the Madagascar FSRP will handle cross-cutting policy efforts as well as knowledge management and project coordination and management. Subcomponent 4.1: Project Coordination and Management 38. Subcomponent 4.1 will support all aspects of project management and M&E. It will fund activities relating to project start-up, M&E, knowledge management, communications, and compliance with fiduciary, procurement, environmental, and social requirements including corporate commitments (such as citizens’ engagement activities). It will also cover staff costs. Subcomponent 4.2: Enabling Climate-Informed Policies, Institutions, and Regional Coordination 39. Subcomponent 4.2 will facilitate climate-informed and cross-cutting policy efforts relating to food systems resilience, including regional-level efforts. It aims to (a) enhance the regulatory and policy framework for digital agriculture; (b) strengthen public agricultural support programs and create incentives for the adoption of CSA; (c) support efforts toward the regional harmonization of national food security policies, regional knowledge sharing, and intraregional food trade; (d) strengthen institutional, professional, and technical capacities of government agencies; and (e) support the capacity to monitor and track deforestation, land use change, and GHG emissions. The subcomponent will finance the development and deployment of digital technologies to create a more inclusive, efficient, productive, healthy, climate-resilient, and connected agricultural sector that supports the long-term health, well-being, and livelihoods of rural communities. It will support performance assessments of public policies and programs and identify ways in which public spending and policies can be reoriented to better align with sustainability, resilience, gender equity, and other core objectives of the Program. It will strengthen the knowledge and skills of key research and technical staff across public institutions through the provision of technical short- and long-term training (PhDs and master’s degree in science) for the staff of the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MinAE) and key research and technical institutions such as FOFIFA, FIFAMANOR, CFFAMMA, the Ministry of Research, the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development, and the Ministry of Higher Education and Scientific Research. Training will cover, among others, climate-smart concepts, climate change risk modeling, agrometeorological forecasting, and big data analytics (for example, methodologies for estimating changes in agricultural productivity, net carbon sequestration, net GHG emissions, soil erosion, vegetation cover, meteorological and hydrological modeling, area-based weather forecasting, cloud-based data management, and soil and vegetation cover mapping). The capacity development Page 109 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) focus on mainstreaming CSA into public policy and programming will help improve the capabilities of IAs to promote and accelerate climate change adaptation and mitigation (through carbon sequestration, reducing emission intensity, and reducing post-harvest losses). Component 5: Contingency Emergency Response Component (CERC) 40. This component will provide immediate response to an eligible crisis or emergency, as needed. Following an eligible crisis or emergency, the borrower may request the World Bank to reallocate project funds to support emergency response and reconstruction. This component would draw on the uncommitted financial resources from other project components to cover emergency response. D. Beneficiaries and Areas of Intervention of the Project 41. The ultimate beneficiaries of the project are households and communities that will benefit from improved food production and higher output, improved access to productive inputs, and the sustainable management of the natural resources. The project will also benefit institutions responsible for seed production, research institutions, and technical services responsible for delivering specific services and inputs to rural households that will benefit from capacity building and equipment. Staff from sector ministries at the national and local levels, totaling approximately 1,000, will also benefit from capacity building, as will service providers (for studies and civil work), another 200 people. At the global level, the population will benefit from reductions in GHG emissions, improvements in hydrological services, and the protection of habitats and biodiversity. Institutions that will benefit from technical assistance, capacity building, and grant-funded activities include FOFIFA, FIFAMANOR, the National Center for Environmental Research, the General Directorate for Meteorology, and agriculture universities, in addition to MinAE departments (such as the Service Officiel de Contrôle des Semences, Direction d'Appui à la Production Végétale,Direction d'Appui à la Production Animale,Direction de la Protection des Végétaux, Direction du Génie Rural, Direction des Services Vétérinaires , and Service Environnement Climat et Réponses aux Urgences), MSMEs, and community groups (such as the Fikambanana Fampivoarana ny Tantsaha, WUAs, Organisations Paysannes Faîtières au niveau Régional, and Tranoben'ny Tantsaha Mpamokatra). 42. The project aims to enhance the productivity and resilience of Madagascar’s food systems and strengthen linkages to regional markets. It will notably support research centers such as FOFIFA, FIFAMANOR, and CFFAMMA, by facilitating collaboration with regional and global research centers of excellence such as ASARECA, IPC, IITA, IIRI, and ILRI, and financial agencies such as FDA and FERHA. The Madagascar FSRP will also finance landscape and infrastructure rehabilitation activities in target areas that (a) are government priorities; (b) demonstrate strong potential for increased food supply and demand; (c) make it possible to complement other investments by the World Bank and other development partners; and (d) are vulnerable to food or nutritional insecurity. The list of target regions, to be refined in consultation with the government, includes Analamanga, Vakinakaratra, Itasy, Antsinanana, Analanjirofo, Atsimo Atsinanana, Vatovavy, Fitovinany, Anosy, DIANA, and Betsiboaka. The southern regions of Madagascar, which are among the most vulnerable and food insecure and the focus of the ongoing Support for Resilient Livelihoods in the South of Madagascar (Mionjo) Project (P171056, US$200 million), will also benefit from FSRP interventions including through better access to improved seeds, weather, and market information.
