Institutional and Implementation Arrangements 43. The institutional and implementation arrangements will be developed at the national and regional levels using the principles of building institutional capacity by supporting existing government systems and mechanisms that have proven to be effective for approaches of similar projects. 44. The Madagascar FSRP will be managed and implemented by the MinAE. The majority of the proposed activities will be implemented by a national PIU. The national PIU of the ongoing PADAP (P154698) has ensured the preparation of the project, while an independent national PIU will be established for the Madagascar FSRP’s implementation. At the regional level, the DRAEs will implement the project. For certain activities, the MinAE will associate with other ministries, such as the ministry responsible for environment. 45. The project will be guided by a Project Steering Committee (PSC). The PSC will provide strategic oversight of the project and include representatives of key stakeholders including the MinAE (its Secretary General), the Ministry in charge of Environment (MEDD), and Ministry in charge of Finance, regional governors, producers, and local communities and organizations (for example, WUAs). The PSC will be chaired by the Secretary General of the MinAE. This committee will meet in ordinary sessions twice a year, or once every six months, at the invitation of its chairperson. Page 111 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 46. Project coordination will be conducted by a national PIU. A national project coordinator will oversee the execution of the Madagascar FSRP and monitor its implementation. The FSRP will recruit a technical manager to strengthen the technical coordination of project activities, reporting to the project coordinator. Additional experts and support staff will also be recruited or appointed within three months of project effectiveness. The project team will include experts on grants, the environment and natural resources management, and agriculture and food security. 47. Regional Implementation Units (RIUs) will be set up in regions where the Madagascar FSRP operates. Each RIU will be led by and housed in the relevant DRAE. It will be strengthened with additional agricultural and environmental staff. These staff will be appointed or assigned on a competitive basis. More specifically, Component 1 will be implemented in collaboration with FOFIFA, with the support of CGIAR institutions and the well-established FDA. Component 2 will be led by the MinAE in close collaboration with other ministries such as the MEDD—also co-implementers of PADAP at the regional level—for activities such as reforestation, sustainable forest management, and community forest management. Component 3, which includes investments in physical infrastructure, will be implemented by the national and regional PIUs through contractors and services providers procured in accordance with World Bank guidelines. Component 4 will be implemented by the national PIU. 48. Reporting and performance arrangements will be provided in the PIM that will be prepared to guide project implementation. Those arrangements will be subject to review if performance is poor. B. Monitoring and Evaluation 49. The project will develop a system for monitoring, evaluating, disseminating, and communicating project achievements. The project will have a robust M&E system to track and evaluate project progress toward the PDO, provide useful information for project management, and document and share project learnings. The system will build on the MinAE’s existing M&E and knowledge management systems and will be guided by a project knowledge management strategy and M&E manual—both to be developed at project start-up. The review committee, PIU, and program M&E specialist will oversee project M&E activities. The GEMS/KOBO toolbox will be used to supplement the project MIS. The latter will collect real-time and geotagged data, including data that can be used in carbon accounting and understanding changes in carbon sequestration in soils and on land. The information generated can contribute to assessing the potential to mobilize climate finance to sustain funding beyond the life of the project. 50. The project will develop a manual of procedures for M&E and knowledge management covering M&E arrangements. Internal monitoring will be carried out by the M&E manager using the dashboard developed for this purpose. A survey will be conducted in the first year of the project to collect baseline data and verify the targets presented in the results framework. In addition, an M&E mechanism will be put in place to monitor emergency response activities. Periodic activity reports will be produced as required (quarterly and annually). Joint supervision and monitoring missions, an MTR, a final review, a completion report, and specific or thematic studies will be organized during the project implementation period. 51. With the support of implementing partners, the PIU will ensure that insights are synthesized and shared at the project and Program levels and beyond. Project evaluation will pay particular attention to evaluating project impacts on farmers’ food security, resilience to various (economic, health, and climate) shocks, access to market, upgrading to facilitate regional trade integration, household nutritional status, and women and youth empowerment. Page 112 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) IV. APPRAISAL SUMMARY A. Fiduciary Financial Management 52. An FM assessment has been completed for the Madagascar FSRP. Details on FM arrangements are provided in Annex 7. Procurement 53. The project procurement risk is Substantial. The applicable procedures, agreed procurement arrangements, procurement risks, mitigation measures, procurement arrangements for high or substantial risk contracts, and oversight mechanisms, are described in Annex 8. B. Environmental and Social 54. For Madagascar, the environmental and social risk classification (ESRC) is rated as Substantial. The project will have significant positive environment and social impacts as activities to be financed under Component 2 will support managing sustainable natural resources management and promote participatory approaches. However, there are also various environmental and social risks that need to be properly managed. The relevant standards that have been identified through the environmental and social risk screening at the concept stage of the project are as follows: ESS1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts; ESS2: Labor and Working Conditions; ESS3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention and Management; ESS4: Community Health and Safety; ESS5: Land Acquisition, Restrictions on Land Use and Involuntary Resettlement; ESS6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources; ESS8 Cultural Heritage; and ESS10: Stakeholder Engagement and Information Disclosure. 55. Activities to be financed under Component 1 associated with strengthening agriculture research and providing grants) could lead to increased demand for agrochemicals and will require measures to manage wastes that may be generated by animal health care services. The project will also finance the rehabilitation and construction of SSI schemes (Component 2) and the construction and rehabilitation of market infrastructure (Component 3) investments for value addition, food safety, and reduced food loss and waste (storage, cold chain, processing, and marketing). These activities could result in various environment, health, and safety risks and impacts including (a) inappropriate use and disposal of agrochemicals and agricultural research laboratory chemicals; (b) health and safety risks and impacts during construction works; (c) inappropriate use of water resources and agrochemical contamination, affecting water quantity and quality in neighboring communities and downstream; (d) physical and chemical degradation of soils from unsuitable land management techniques; (e) adverse impacts on biodiversity and ecosystems through an introduction of invasive species; and (f) though site specific and small in scale in the context of this project, construction of small-scale infrastructure such as storage facilities and SSI schemes and associated adverse externalities (air pollution, construction waste pollution, noise pollution, and water pollution). 56. Agricultural activities produce GHG emissions including methane, nitrous oxide, and carbon dioxide. However, the activities to be financed by this project are CDD types of activities and not expected to generate significant emissions. The small irrigation schemes to be financed by the project will meet the World Bank’s requirements for small dams. The project will be implemented on existing agricultural land and hence will not lead Page 113 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) to the conversion of natural habitat. Considering the nature of the activities to be financed by the project and the client’s capacity to manage the risks, the environmental risk of the project is rated as Substantial at this stage. 57. The project is expected to result in social benefits, but its social risk level is Substantial. In particular, it is expected to increase rural employment opportunities (including for youth and women), improve livelihoods, enhance the resilience of farmers, improve access to finance, increase agricultural yields, and increase access to diverse and nutritious foods. However, the social risk of the project is considered Substantial because of the project’s extensive scope and country context. In addition, the project-supported activities could result in land acquisition—notably for the construction of agricultural infrastructure such as small-scale and household irrigation, market infrastructure, and rural feeder roads and bridges. The latter may trigger involuntary resettlement (physical and economic displacement), restrictions on access to land, and a loss of livelihoods within affected communities. In addition, although the exact location of the investments will be determined during the project implementation stage, project activities will be implemented in highland and lowland areas with complex socioeconomic and political contexts. 58. Other forms of social risk may exist. It may, for example, arise from (a) insufficient community and stakeholder engagement and elite capture; (b) the exclusion of vulnerable groups and individuals from project benefits due to poorly designed and/or disseminated or nontransparent beneficiary selection process or eligibility; (c) social tensions and conflict induced by competition over agricultural resources including irrigation water resources and contextual security risks in conflict-affected areas; (d) labor influx and associated community health and safety risks, SEA/SH, and other forms of GBV; (e) the failure to comply with labor standards, especially within activities financed by matching grant, and the potential use of forced and child labor; (f) operational concerns due to remoteness and insecurity, including challenges in monitoring social risks and handling grievance management; and (g) weak implementation capacity, especially at the grassroots level due to limited functional structures and trained manpower. The propagation of COVID-19 during the implementation of project activities has also been identified as a cross-cutting risk. 59. To identify and manage the potential ESHS risks, the respective national MinAE will prepare the required environmental and social instruments. They will include (a) an ESMF to screen project activities and guide the development of site-specific instruments, (b) an IPMP to mitigate potential risks and impacts associated with the application of pesticides, (c) a Resettlement Policy Framework (RPF), and (d) an SEP. An ESCP will also be developed to outline measures to be implemented including implementation arrangements and monitor and report on the implementation of environmental and social risk management tools and plans in line with the ESF. 60. The project will take measures to mitigate and manage these risks. The ESCP, the SEP, including a GRM, have been disclosed both on the World Bank website on April 24, 2022, and in country on April 25, 2022. Draft LMP including a workers’ GRM, draft ESMF including IPMP; and RPF have been developed and disclosed on Country, on April 25, 2022, for public consultation. The final version of these documents will be a condition for disbursement of activities under Components 1, 2, and 3; the adoption of a grant manual will be a condition of disbursement for certain activities under Components 1 and 3; and the completion of the World Bank’s standard CERC-related conditions will be a condition of disbursement for CERC activities. 61. The project’s M&E system will be responsible for monitoring environmental and social impacts and measures. As the project’s IA, the PIU—with the guidance of its environmental and social specialists and the World Bank environmental and social team—will be responsible for the preparation of the relevant environmental safeguards assessment documents and other appropriate safeguards tools. Monitoring checklists will be prepared based on mitigation plans. Page 114 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 62. The project will take measures to ensure social inclusion, gender equity, and CE . In accordance with corporate directives on CE in IPF projects, the project will emphasize approaches that maximize outreach and participation of communities and broader public awareness of project activities. The project will have two CE indicators: one related to beneficiaries’ feedback and the other linked to the project GRM. In accordance with ESS10 and the guidelines for citizen involvement in projects throughout the preparation and implementation processes, the project will further promote CE and the establishment of a process for processing community feedback. These aspects will be included in the SEP, which will also cover the establishment and operationalization of a project GRM. Grants under Component 2 supporting infrastructure development and income diversification opportunities will be identified through demand-driven processes. Participatory assessment and planning approaches will ensure that all groups, including those that are historically marginalized such as women and youth, are engaged in planning and decision-making processes. Local authorities will present final plans and advise on the selection of grant proposals that will be financed under the project. Finally, the project’s GRM will ensure that any feedback will generate a timely and comprehensive response and, where needed, an acceptable resolution by the project. 63. Measures to mitigate environmental and social risks and impacts are listed in table A2.3. Key Risks 64. The overall risk associated with the implementation of the Madagascar FSRP is rated as Substantial. The overall project implementation risk is rated as Substantial given Madagascar’s volatile political and economic situation, substantial procurement-related risks, the disruptive nature of reforms at the national and local levels, the risk of political interference and elite capture, the technical challenges faced by the project due to weak fiduciary and human resources capacity, the elevated levels of insecurity and fragility, and the current uncertainty on economic recovery. 65. The overall project risk rating is Substantial. Key risks as well as mitigation measures have been identified to minimize the potential impact of these risks. Further mitigation measures and residual risk will be assessed during preparation. This may delay preparation and implementation. For this reason, the project is being specifically designed to be relatively simple to implement and the update of the rice strategy and the design studies for a part of the hydraulic infrastructure are to be carried out during preparation. Furthermore, corruption and vested interests are not uncommon and may hamper the implementation of delivery of services. Therefore, the task team will include experienced governance and FM experts to design appropriate and transparent measures limiting the overall governance risk. Other risks relate to the technical nature of the rehabilitation. 66. Politics and governance risk rating is Substantial. Between 2009 and 2014, Madagascar experienced several political crises that severely weakened its institutions and governance. With the democratic transition of power in 2019, Madagascar further reinforced a stabilized political environment conducive to continued development efforts, though the country still suffers from poor governance. It is nonetheless expected that potential volatility following the COVID-19 crisis and potential changes in government counterparts at both political and technical levels may induce some delays in project preparation and implementation. 67. Technical design and institutional capacity for implementation and sustainability risk rating is Substantial. The project will apply the principles of an IPF. Because the project is comprehensive, with activities as diverse as the rehabilitation of irrigation infrastructure, technical assistance to WUAs, promotion of climate and nutrition-sensitive agriculture, and the improvement of the enabling environment in the rice sector, the project will require spatial coordination and careful sequencing. The project will be anchored in the Ministry in charge of Agriculture, which has experience with the World Bank through the implementation of agricultural investment projects. The latter include the Rural Development Support Project (P051922, P092848), Irrigation and Watershed Management Project (P074086, P123532), Irrigation and Watershed Management Project (PHRD P128831), CASEF (P151469), and the PADAP (P154698). The MinAE will be responsible for coordinating project implementation by working closely with local governments (regions and districts) and other project stakeholders. It is also proposed that the oversight committee (Comité de pilotage) includes the Ministry in charge of Trade and Page 116 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Industry, the Ministry in charge of Transport and Meteorology, the Ministry of Economy and Finance, and the Ministry in charge of Decentralization, with the MinAE acting as committee chair. 68. Fiduciary risk is Substantial. The MinAE will be responsible for project implementation. The MinAE is currently executing CASEF (P151469) and the PADAP (P154698/P157909). With respect to CASEF, there is no overdue financial audit and the external auditor opinion is unqualified for these projects. IFRs were submitted on time and were acceptable. Regarding the PADAP, the project is compliant with auditing and reporting requirements. Lack of control is noticed over activities in regions resulting in loss of assets. Corrective actions are implemented to address these weaknesses. The project FM performance is deemed Moderately Satisfactory. The FM assessment was conducted at the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MinAE) under which the designated project implementation unit will be established (PIU). The substantial risk was mainly attributed to the PFM weaknesses particularly in budgeting and disbursement mechanism, the nature of activities and the level of decentralization of the project. 69. The ESRC risk is Substantial. The relevant standards that have been identified through the environmental and social risk screening are as follows: ESS1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts; ESS2: Labor and Working Conditions; ESS3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention and Management; ESS4: Community Health and Safety; ESS5: Land Acquisition, Restrictions on Land Use and Involuntary Resettlement; ESS6: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources; ESS8 Cultural Heritage; and ESS10: Stakeholder Engagement and Information Disclosure. 70. Environmental risks and impacts. The proposed project will bring significant benefits to the communities in the target regions through the provision of enhanced services, technical assistance and sectoral coordination, as well improvements to agriculture infrastructure, the rehabilitation or construction of feeder roads and bridges, access to matching grants to increase access to markets and other investments to unlock agribusiness potential. Although the impacts of the project are likely to be positive, these activities carry several risks that are mainly generated by the activities under Components 1 and 2. The rehabilitation and expansion of existing irrigation infrastructure, rehabilitation of feeder roads and bridges, and rehabilitation and upgrading of market and post- harvest infrastructures, which all include small- to medium-scale civil works, will more than likely generate (a) adverse site-specific risks and impacts to the occupational health and safety of workers during construction and operational phases; (b) increased levels of dust and noise and community health and safety risks from, particularly the risk of pollution to surface and groundwater sources during dredged of channels; and (c) solid and liquid waste. In addition, Subcomponent 2.2, through the promotion of farmers' increased use of fertilizers, could cause adverse health effects linked to improper use, storage, and disposal and contribute to the degradation of surface and groundwater quality. However, based on an initial review of project activities, the potential adverse risks and impacts on human populations and/or the environment are not likely to be significant and will be site specific. 71. It is expected that project activities will have positive social impacts by financing rural infrastructure investments, irrigation infrastructure, community markets, and feeder roads. However, the proposed project activities to be financed through Components 1 and 3 on improving agricultural infrastructure may include small- to medium-scale civil works. These activities are likely to induce some social risks and impacts that are however mostly temporary, predictable, and/or reversible. Labor influx and associated risk and impacts can be a point of concern, especially on community health (including the risk of transmission of diseases such as sexually transmitted diseases as well as the transmission and propagation of COVID-19, GBV, and SEA/SH). No irreversible social risks related to land access or cultural heritage are anticipated. No physical displacement is expected under the project, but there will be a risk of land acquisition and temporary displacement during the works. The matching grant program risk might be the inadequacy of criteria of eligibility which could lead to exclusion of eligible Page 117 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) persons. In line with the recommendations of the GBV Good Practice Note, specific measures will be applied during project preparation and implementation. Measures to be taken will include, but are not limited to, (a) the development and implementation of a GBV action plan; (b) the systematic inclusion of (code of conduct) clauses on workers’ conditions and management, child protection, and GBV prevention in all contracts; and (c) the reinforcement of the GRM to ensure that any incident related to GBV will be addressed effectively with sufficient social sensitivity. 72. The project-level GRM will be central to risk mitigation efforts and will help manage grievances from communities or by parties who feel that they are or will be adversely affected by the project. The project-level GRM will serve as an avenue for communities to channel their concerns. Clients will be supported to establish an accessible, effective, and efficient GRM with the capacity to receive and respond to grievances in the local languages and on time. Grievances related to SEA/SH as well as other forms of GBV will be given special attention and protocols to enable survivor-centered responses will be put in place. The team will ensure that project-related grievances are shared with the World Bank’s GRS. The team will work with clients to ensure that communities are aware of the multiple forms used to submit grievances to the GRM and the GRS in case they think they are or could be adversely affected by the project. Page 118 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) ANNEX 3: Activities Implemented at the Regional Level by IGAD I. STRATEGIC CONTEXT Sector and Institutional Context 1. There is a long history of institutional efforts across the region to reduce vulnerability and build resilience.66 IGAD was established in 1986 and amended its establishment agreement in 1996 to coordinate the efforts of member states67 in achieving regional food security and encourage and assist efforts of member states to collectively combat drought and other natural and man-made disasters and their consequences. The IGAD region covers an area of 5.2 million km2 with a current population of just over 270 million, projected to reach half a billion by 2050.68 Among the eight IGAD member states, three are land-locked: Ethiopia, South Sudan, and Uganda. Strong dependence by the populations of the IGAD region on natural resources such as rainfed agriculture and livestock, which are highly vulnerable and most exposed to climatic hazards, implies that a large part of the population is vulnerable to food insecurity. The region is projected to have strong annual demographic growth ranging between 2.5 percent and 3.5 percent, and at present over 60 percent of the estimated 270 million people in the region are youth (30 years old or less). 69 The youth population brought with them both opportunity and challenge depending on how well it is managed. 2. IGAD has a longstanding partnership on resilience building with development partners such as the European Union (EU), African Development Bank, Word Bank, UN agencies, IFAD, and many others. The engagement cover strengthens resilience against droughts; food security information and analysis; early warning and DRM; implementation of cross border actions; conflict prevention; NRM; market access and trade; and capacity building; there is also institutional strengthening and coordination through the IGAD Drought Disaster Resilience and Sustainability Initiative (IDDRSI). Today, IGAD is leading the coordination of the region’s response to key challenges at the intersection of climate resilience, water resources and fragility. 70 IGAD has been developing capacities within its institutional framework, for regional and country-scale coordination and implementation of programs on several important themes such as programs of resilience, food security, climate prediction and hydromet services, livestock/pastoral support, platforms for groundwater management and transboundary pest management, and an observatory of regional conflict. IGAD, therefore, is well suited to foster collaboration and achieve economies of scale in the region, as well as to improve the regional knowledge on aquifers as the basis for future transboundary groundwater management and collaboration. 3. Under the MPA, the role of IGAD as a facilitator of regional dialogue and knowledge sharing is key to building resilience in AFE through sustainable groundwater development. Promoting information sharing in the region, creating value added by developing functioning data analysis platforms and aquifer studies, building a groundwater knowledge base that serves as a foundation to robust decision-making on infrastructure development and sustainable groundwater exploitation, helping build institutional capacity on sustainable 66 Resilience is defined as the capacity of people, assets, or institutions to absorb, adapt and potentially transform in the face of the impacts of (short term) shocks and (long term) stressors. WB (2017) “Operational Guidance for Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) in Climate and Disaster Resilience-Building Operations”, Resilience M&E (ReM&E) initiative. 67 IGAD current has eight MS: Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Kenya, Somalia, South Sudan, Sudan, and Uganda. 68 The Horn Economic and Social Policy Institute (2017): Macroeconomic performance of IGAD and the implications of China’s economic slowdown 69 IGAD (2019). The IDDRSI Strategy (2019 – 2024). 70 Regional responses include IGAD’s Drought Disaster Resilience and Sustainability Initiative (IDDRSI), the IGAD Support Platform for refugees, IGAD’S Livestock Policy Initiative, increased regional collaboration in response to the desert locust crisis, and IGAD’s multi- country projects directed at building resilience of pastoral and agro-pastoral communities in cross-border areas Page 119 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) groundwater management, and creating an inclusive space for dialogue around groundwater issues, are among the most essential functions that IGAD is planning to undertake as part of the Program, thus building trust among participating countries. 4. At the HoA scale, IGAD developed the Regional Resilience Framework (RRF), which serves as a common framework for drought resilience programming and contributed to reducing the number of people in need of humanitarian assistance in the HoA. To implement the RRF, a five-year (2021-2025) IGAD Strategy as its Regional Strategic Plan (RSP) is prepared that provides vision, mission, and priority areas, anchored in the HoA’s Vision 2050. The 2021-2025 RSP of IGAD is aligned with the AU Agenda 2063 and in line with the commitment of IGAD member states to the SDGs 2030. IGAD is partnering and implementing several projects and programs that have informed the AFE FSRP design building on IGAD’s experience and results. Key programs and projects include: DRDIP (P161067, Kenya and IGAD) and (P152822, Djibouti, Ethiopia, Uganda and IGAD), the Pastoralism and Stability in the Sahel and Horn of Africa Project (P153713), the Regional Pastoral Livelihoods Resilience Project (P129408), the North and North Eastern Development Initiative in Kenya, the Nile Cooperation for Results Project (P130694), the Emergency Locust Response Program (ELRP, P173702) and the pipeline operations of ELRP MPA Additional Financing (P177843), and Phase 1 Additional Financing for Ethiopia (P178434). With IGAD included in the FSRP, the regional-scale integration and coordination, will strengthen information and data sharing as well as contribute to institutional onboarding of other regional entities that are expected to join Phase 2 and subsequent phases of FSRP (for example, SADC, COMESA, EAC, CCARDESA, and GMI) to enable and facilitate transboundary groundwater collaboration and governance, setting the foundations for long-term integration. 5. At the regional level, the activities undertaken by IGAD will complement national and local efforts by strengthening institutional capacity as well as the enabling environment for food security at national and subnational scales. As new countries join the subsequent program phases, based on their readiness, IGAD will extend its regional coordination and facilitation support framework. Key country readiness criteria will include financing availability and progress on the technical preparation of project proposals, including the necessary ESF instruments. 6. Over time, and in response to the member states, IGAD has taken measures to strengthen its institutional capacity to deliver services to the countries and better leverage regional coordination and learning. IGAD has established several programs and centers of excellences to enhance integration in different thematic areas. These include the Conflict Early Warning and Response Mechanism (CEWARN), the Climate Prediction and Applications Centre (ICPAC), the Centre for Pastoral Areas and Livestock Development (ICPALD), the Centre of Excellence for Preventing and Countering Violent Extremism, the Sheikh Technical Veterinary School, the Institute for Diplomatic Studies (IGAD Foreign Service Institute), and IGAD Centre of Excellence on Nutrition (ICEN). While several units and technical teams of IGAD will be involved in the implementation of the program, ICPAC through its Food Security, Nutrition and Resilience Analysis Hub, will provide country and regional services at the AFE scale. The Resilience Analysis Unit and the IPC are target programs of the ICPAC that will provide an up-to-date FSN situation analysis (early warning) and offer a forum to build consensus on critical issues facing policy and interventions. 7. To support the MPA, IGAD will define guidelines and tools for food security measures and sustainable land and water resources management for countries in the HoA, and facilitate cross-country as well as cross- HoA and AFE dialogue on food systems resilience topics. The latter will include food security monitoring, earlier warning systems, water and land use management strategies, and environmental and climate change indicators. IGAD has strong programs of resilience, food security, climate prediction and hydromet services, livestock/pastoral support, platforms for groundwater management and transboundary pest management, and Page 120 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) an observatory of regional conflict. This regional platform can provide early warning information on the FSN situation and provides a forum to build consensus on how to address FSN issues. Moreover, IGAD plays an important role in the political economy of the HoA region and can reach beyond its member state to foster dialogue as needed. Through ICPAC, a regional food security platform is already operational as the FSNWG, which is co-chaired by IGAD and the FAO.71 In addition to the IGAD members states, over 80 organizations are members of the FSNWG including the UN agencies, NGOs, donors, and research institutions, who contribute to the operation of the working group, and its nutrition, markets, food security information, livestock, and pastoralist sub-groups. The platform provides information and analysis services through regular and periodic updates and alert-bulletins such as quarterly Food Security Alert;72 East Africa Cross Border Trade Bulletin,73 Special Report on Drought,74 and East Africa Regional Desert Locust Impact Monitoring Report.75 The ICPAC also provides regular climate monitoring and alert services including the 10-day, monthly, and seasonal monitoring products. 76 II. PROJECT DESCRIPTION Project Development Objective 8. The PDO of phase 1 of the Program is to increase the resilience of food systems and preparedness for food insecurity in project areas. Page 121 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Component 1: (Re-)Building Resilient Agricultural Production Capacity 11. IGAD will contribute to building the resilience of regional food supply to shocks and stressors through multiple activities. It will provide technical assistance, support knowledge management and communications relating to innovations and technologies, and organize capacity building and training programs. Subcomponent 1.1: Developing National or Regional Agricultural Information Systems 12. IGAD will promote regional information systems and knowledge . Specifically, IGAD will establish a regional architecture of the DCAS that includes climate-data monitoring and interpretation services on demand from countries. The DCAS will provide timely and site-specific recommendations on crop cultivars, soil preparation, optimal sowing, fertilization, harvest time, and other information that will enable them to better manage agricultural risks and reduce uncertainties that often constrain decision-making. IGAD will also strengthen capacity for agriculture and environment data and statistics; coordinate regional-scale input quality-control through strengthened standards and certification of seeds, fertilizer, pesticides, and other goods and services; support regional agricultural risk management mechanisms that include index-based agricultural insurance (for crops and livestock) and credit solutions; and establish a food systems monitoring mechanism for the HoA that includes national technical working groups (involving public and private stakeholders including CSOs, academia, and research institutes), data collection and analysis protocols, and a report on the state-of-food systems for the HoA. Subcomponent 1.2: Developing and Delivering Agricultural Technologies and Services 13. Specifically, IGAD will (a) strengthen existing regional research centers and establish new national and regional research centers. For this IGAD will: (a) support the upgrading of existing national centers through institutional linkages with regional and international networks and academic and research institutions; (b) coordinate specific knowledge and expert exchange programs among national and regional centers; (c) coordinate specific climate-smart knowledge and expert exchange programs among national and regional centers; (d) facilitate the establishment of RCoLs in participating countries in partnership with CCARDESA;77 (e) facilitate the establishment of a regional monitoring control and surveillance center to combat illegal, unregulated, and unprotected fishing in coordination or consultation with existing efforts such as the Second South West Indian Ocean Fisheries Governance and Shared Growth (SWIOFish) Project (P153370), which involves Madagascar; (f) support the implementation of the recommendations made in the CAADP’s third biannual report with a special focus on climate resilience, and update the IGAD Regional Agriculture Investment Plan, linking with NAIPs; (g) facilitate the adoption of a One Health approach to climate adaptation in HoA by establishing a regional platform that will convene and coordinate animal, human, and environmental health services, through regional interagency coordination and knowledge products; and (h) support IGAD Sheikh Technical Veterinary School and Reference Centre (ISTVS) to provide better veterinary, animal production, crop production and dryland livelihoods in the ASAL region. 14. IGAD will also promote regional climate-smart agricultural R&D. It will specifically (a) facilitate regional agricultural information systems that connect national systems to provide coordinated research and data on 77 Five RCoLs currently operational in Southern Africa provide regional-scale agricultural innovation and R&D institutional mechanism for joint research and dissemination and cross-country exchange of knowledge, expertise, and climate-adapted varieties and technologies, thereby allowing regional specialization around priority farming systems and more strategic investment in agricultural research capacity. Under the APPSA (P164486), five RCoLs are established: Angola Cassava RCoL, Lesotho Horticulture RCoL, Malawi Maize RCoL, Mozambique Rice RCoL, and Zambia Legumes RCoL. Page 122 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) climate smart and resilient agriculture; (b) establish regional FFS, pastoralist field school, and fish field school curricula that follow an experiential learning model based on the demonstration, practice, and application of learning material on farmers’ own plots; and (c) facilitate regional research on a broad range of topics such as cross-border livestock disease and pest-prevention, pest and pathology surveillance and response, cross-border knowledge exchange mechanisms for agricultural mechanization, seed production and quality monitoring, livestock breed improvement, bio-risk management in the HoA, nutrition, food chemistry, and food microbiology. 15. IGAD will support the scaling up of CSA initiatives in the HoA. For this, it will (a) formulate regional policy on climate-smart and resilient agriculture, (b) develop and support community adaptation investment programs and strengthen national and subnational capacity for delivering extension and field schools, and (c) facilitate evidenced-based agroclimatic information collection and dissemination to support regional and national policy dialogue. Component 2: Supporting the Sustainable Development of Natural Resources for Resilient Agricultural Landscapes 16. IGAD will support and coordinate transnational and regional technical assistance, analytical and advisory work, and training efforts, especially on topics related to sustainable management of transboundary natural resources. Subcomponent 2.1: Identification and Validation of Interventions at the Local or Watershed Level 17. IGAD will identify and validate interventions at the local and watershed levels using criteria that ensure climate adaptation and mitigation co-benefits. For this, it will: (a) undertake a regional assessment of hydromet monitoring, weather advisory services, and spatial analysis tools to improve the timely availability and quality of data for decision-making; (b) build a regional knowledge base for coordinated action, the prioritization of intervention areas and themes, and the development of specific territorial investment plans; (c) support the national adoption and implementation of regional environmental policies and frameworks on pollution, invasive species management, and similar topics; (d) promote pilots for the transboundary management of biodiversity in selected cross-border national parks; and (e) strengthen the HoA Wildlife Network (HAWEN) secretariat in its efforts to fight illegal cross-border wildlife trade by establishing centers near borders to care for confiscated animals. Subcomponent 2.2: Investments in Sustainable Natural Resources Management 18. IGAD will coordinate country and regional progress on CCBs. It will do this by (a) establishing a regional mechanism that consists of a simple and effective georeferenced monitoring system designed and implemented to assess the impacts of decisions taken and actions carried out at the landscape level; (b) supporting implementation of IGAD rangeland management strategy; (c) facilitating scale-up of proven fodder interventions in some pilot areas along the wet and dry season grazing areas; (d) facilitating integrated climatic risk and impact indicators to model scenarios on how key agricultural sectors will be affected by climate change in IGAD region (how productivity will change under different emission scenarios); (e) conducting crop suitability mapping based on different future climatic scenarios to inform long term planning and policy; and (f) establishing multistakeholder and multisectoral coordination mechanisms on climate security that promote knowledge among pastoral and agropastoral communities on rangelands drought early warning. Page 123 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Component 3: Getting to Market 19. To support the development of competitive agrifood value chains and well-functioning marketing infrastructure, IGAD will support HoA member states in taking full advantage of the AfCFTA and other intraregional trade and value addition opportunities. Subcomponent 3.1: Developing Competitive Value Chains 20. IGAD will support this subcomponent through studies on risk assessments and value chain competitiveness. IGAD will: (a) conduct value chain analyses; (b) support assessment of market intelligence systems, including their responsiveness to the needs of smallholder farmers and other value chain actors; (c) coordinate regional studies on the costs of doing business; and (d) support assessment of market compliance and quality controls for enhanced resilience. 21. IGAD will also coordinate regional knowledge development and dissemination on value chain and market competitiveness. It will do this by: (a) conducting a regional assessment of epidemio-surveillance, trade border controls, seed certification, pesticide quality controls, and so forth; (b) developing an HoA regional agricultural trade and market scorecard fashioned after the EATM-Scorecard78 in partnership with other regional organizations such as SADC and COMESA, as well as representatives from member states in particular producers’ groups, famers’ unions, and private sector actors; (c) supporting young agro-entrepreneurs in the HoA region through training-of-trainers on topics like value addition for selected products and business incubation through business start-up planning or business upgrading. Subcomponent 3.2.: Upgrading Marketing Infrastructure 22. IGAD will conduct a cross-country assessment of the market and trade connectivity infrastructure in the HoA. It will include: (a) an assessment of border-crossing posts and economic priority zones for their ease-of- trade, (b) new cross-country trade and economic corridors connected through road and rail infrastructure, (c) the strength of current border procedures as well as ‘single-window’ and ‘multi-tier’ operations that facilitate or hinder the cross-border movement of goods and services, (d) the extent of digital connectivity in trade facilitation services and areas for improvement to inform country policy reforms, and (e) assessment of cross-border livestock and fish markets and diagnostic capacity. IGAD will also foster increased awareness of the role of international trade as an important adaptation mechanism to deal with geographically dispersed climate-related harvest failures. Component 4: Promoting Food Systems Resilience in National and Regional Policymaking 23. IGAD will support national government agencies and regional organizations in ways that pertain to high- level policies, initiatives, institutional arrangements, and even budgeting decisions that have cross-cutting relevance to food systems resilience. IGAD will closely work with participating countries and other regional institutions to coordinate, collaborate, and, to the extent feasible, jointly undertake the preparation and implementation of regional climate adaptation and mitigation policies and regulations to increase regional flows of agricultural goods and inputs and consolidate the regional food reserve system. 78The ECOWAS Agriculture Trade and Market Accountability Mechanism is being developed under the West Africa Food Systems Resilience Program MPA (P172769). The Scorecard aims to assess implementation of policy commitments on agricultural trade and market access in the region by identifying country-level policy gaps and areas for improvement related to intra-regional agricultural and food trade. Page 124 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Subcomponent 4.1: Making Food Systems Resilience a Priority in Public Policies and Spending 24. IGAD will support country-level efforts to make food systems resilience a higher priority of public policies and spending in multiple ways. IGAD will support countries in integrating resilience-focus into existing and new policies by: (a) conducting policy review to assess resilience-focus within country policy frameworks and technical assistance to strengthen it within existing and upcoming policies; (b) developing a new-generation framework of agricultural PER that supports alignment of public resources with resilience-informed policy priorities; (c) advising on how to better align price and policy incentives in agriculture, natural resources management, trade, and other domains with food systems resilience objectives; (d) reviewing and advising on the harmonization of key agricultural adaptation policies such as ones relating to seed production and the cross- border movement of germplasm and other genetic material, and agricultural expenditure tracking systems; and (e) facilitating the development of a regional climate resilient livelihoods policy and action plan. 25. IGAD will increase regional collaboration to build compliance on SPS, quality, and traceability systems. It will do this by (a) promoting innovative and digital mechanisms (potentially blockchain approaches) within country and regional value chains; (b) providing technical assistance on harmonizing legislation and policy frameworks to support implementation of national SPS systems by countries; (c) facilitating knowledge and experience exchanges among country stakeholders and experts from international networks and institutes; and (d) strengthening the East Africa Parliamentary Alliance on Food Security and Nutrition. 26. IGAD will review and modernize the architecture of existing food stock or food reserve systems. Its actions will include: (a) mapping existing food reserves in the HoA and identifying areas of support needed to strengthen these; (b) promoting and coordinate commodity exchanges between reserve systems across member states where this is mutually beneficial; (c) developing a digitized information and decision-making support component for food reserves; (d) integrating advanced technologies for procuring, importing, storing, and monitoring food stocks and grain reserves; (e) facilitating interagency coordination; and (f) updating and modernizing inventory and warehousing capacities at the country level with attention to harmonizing systems across the region to facilitate the movement of food during crisis situations. Subcomponent 4.2: Building the Capacity to Implement Resilience-Focused Policies 27. IGAD will strengthen its own technical and program delivery capacities. The capacities targeted are those which allow it to: (a) conduct policy analysis and harmonization through analytical work, needs assessments, and policy dialogue or policy harmonization activities in key areas that affect R&D at the national and regional levels; (b) conduct and disseminate thematic analytical work such as seed production and regulatory systems, intellectual property rights, biosafety regulations, and similar topics; (c) deliver training and knowledge on resilience-themed technical subjects through existing and emerging multisectoral stakeholder platforms; (d) facilitate regional consultations on trade protocols, biotechnology safety guidelines, regulatory and inspection mechanisms, and other topics. Subcomponent 4.3: Supporting Regional Organizations to Build Food Systems Resilience Transnationally 28. Under this subcomponent IGAD’s capacity to support implementation of the MPA will be strengthened . Specifically, IGAD will strengthen its various technical and institutional capacities including: (a) technical capacities relating to digital technologies, competitiveness, and marketing compliance strategies; (b) institutional capacity to design and implement regional MISs and operationalize the regional M&E system; (c) institutional capacity for knowledge generation and dissemination and use of advanced information and communication technologies (ICTs); (d) technical capacity for conducting regional agricultural R&D coordination and integrated NRM analysis; Page 125 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) (e) capacity to implement cross-border dissemination of improved varieties and technologies in partnership with other regional organizations; and (f) capacity to promote regional cooperation for improved data exchange on agriculture, food security, vulnerability, One Health, agricultural risk financing and insurance, and related topics. 29. IGAD will build its capacity to organize inclusive multistakeholder dialogue and consultation mechanisms, and negotiations related to continental free trade for climate adaptation. It will do this by (a) strengthening IGAD’s organizational and communication capacity to organize high-level forum, meetings, and other events to deliberate, negotiate and collaborate among countries, international agencies, and development partners on import food resilience topics; (b) strengthening its ability to organize multi-country and regional trade dialogues in food and other agricultural goods and services to facilitate intraregional trade in raw and processed foods, agricultural inputs, and technologies, and more; and (c) enabling IGAD to promote multi-country and regional memorandums of understanding (MOUs) and other bilateral and multilateral agreements on resilience- relevant trade and knowledge systems, while reducing the movement of pests, pathogens, and invasive species. III. IMPLEMENTATION ARRANGEMENTS 30. The overall responsibility for the implementation and oversight of the IGAD Project will lie with IGAD’s AED. The IGAD Secretariat will set up a Djibouti-based PCU within the existing Agriculture and Food Security Unit (AFSU) under the AED. The functions of the AFSU include regional programming, coordination of resource mobilization, M&E services, regional knowledge management, and regional capacity development. The MPA PCU will have the following staff: (a) a project coordinator, (b) policy officer, (c) gender officer, (d) research/innovation officer, (e) procurement officer, (f) project accountant, (g) safeguards expert, (h) monitoring, evaluation and learning officer, and (i) project assistant. 31. These staff will be supported by the different executive units of IGAD. The latter include the Economic Cooperation and Regional Integration Division, Health and Social Development and IGAD’s specialized institutions (ICPAC, CEWARN, ICPALD, and ICEN), which will support the implementation of the technical components of the Project. 32. Operationally, IGAD will liaise with the countries through designated political and technical focal points. IGAD’s Directorate of Administration and Finance will receive the funds and facilitate the operations of the FSRP- PCU as well as the implementation of project activities. The relevant planning and M&E units of IGAD—including the Planning Coordination and Partnerships Division — will monitor and supervise implementation. All aspects of project management including the procurement of equipment and materials, compliance with fiduciary and environmental and social safeguards requirements, M&E and impact assessment, and knowledge management will be supported by the relevant divisions and offices. 33. The FSRP-PCU will be overseen by a Regional Coordination Committee (RCC) whose primary function is to provide the project policy and technical guidance during implementation. The RCC will be co-chaired by IGAD’s Director for the AED and SADC’s Director for FANR or their designates. The main objectives of a co-chaired RCC will be to (a) ensure close coordination among subregional organizations involved in MPA implementation, including ones covering the HoA, Eastern Africa, and Southern Africa; (b) act as the main mechanism for interregional knowledge exchange and planning; and (c) establish joint mechanisms for monitoring, knowledge generation and knowledge sharing, and reporting and record keeping for all countries and partners involved in the MPA from Phase 1 onward. The RCC will invite other regional and development partners as observers or prospective collaborators and partners of the MPA. Regional partners under consideration include the AUC, SADC, COMESA, the EAC, ASARECA, ILRI, farmers’ organizations, and the private sector. Detailed TORs will be developed as part of the PIM. STRATEGIC CONTEXT Sector and Institutional Context 1. CCARDESA was established by SADC in 2010 to coordinate agriculture-related R&D programs across SADC’s 16 member states. Its specific mission is to set the regional R&D agenda, mobilize resources, support capacity development, foster collaboration, and disseminate agricultural information and knowledge among SADC’s 16 member countries. In 2019, the combined population of the SADC member states stood at 362 million. 79 2. Since its establishment, CCARDESA has actively coordinated and supported the implementation of programs aligned with member states’ regional integration aspirations. Its interventions in the agricultural sector have been guided by the priorities and strategies of SADC countries. As a subsidiary of SADC, CCARDESA has also made it a priority to operationalize various agreements that SADC countries have entered into at the regional, continent, and global levels. The strategies, policies, protocols, and multilateral agreements in question include the Regional Indicative Strategic Development Plan, the SADC Common Agricultural Policy, the CAADP, the Malabo Declaration on Accelerated Agricultural Growth and Transformation, the UN SDGs, and the SADC Protocol on Fisheries and the SADC Protocol on Forestry. 3. Thematically speaking, CCARDESA’s focus is laid out in its Long-Term Strategic Plan for 2020–2029. Themes of distinct importance in that that document include climate change, commercialization and access to markets, agricultural production and productivity, capacity building of national AR4D institutions, and information and knowledge management. 4. CCARDESA has long been at the forefront of efforts to improve the resilience of Southern Africa’s food systems. Past efforts to enhance the resilience of agricultural production systems in the region include its support for the Adaptation to Climate Change in Rural Areas project in the SADC region as well as the Global Climate Change Alliance Plus (GCCA+) project. Currently, CCARDESA is implementing the European Union-supported CAADP-XP4 project which aims to enable agricultural research and innovation, including extension services, with the potential to mitigate the effects of climate change, and enhance food and nutritional security. CCARDESA is also coordinating the APPSA, which has carried out a number of field experiments to generate technologies that can improve the resilience of agricultural production systems. In addition, a regional forestry R&D program promoting good forest governance and sustainable forest management is being initiated in close consultation with SADC member states. In parallel, an integrated aquaculture-agriculture program is being initiated at the regional level. 5. CCARDESA also has a track record in successfully supporting the implementation of phased, multicountry programs, and it will continue to play this role in the context of the MPA. Since Phase 1 of the MPA will include just one country in Southern Africa, Madagascar, one early role of CCARDESA will be to bring visibility to the Program, its merits, and learnings among SADC’s other member countries. In that way, CCARDESA will enable the Program to grow organically and in a way that is responsive to SADC country priorities and needs. CCARDESA has played this role before, one recent example being in the context of the APPSA, a program that 79 SADC Demographic and Social Statistics, 2019 Page 128 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) started out in three countries—Malawi, Mozambique, and Zambia—and later moved on to include two more, Lesotho, and Angola. 6. In supporting the MPA, CCARDESA will leverage its existing networks and outreach tools for regional coordination mechanisms throughout Southern Africa. One of CCARDESA’s most significant tools in that respect is its information, communication, and knowledge management (ICKM) system, through which information and knowledge products are disseminated to stakeholders. Over the years, CCARDESA has also established strong networks and partnerships involving agricultural research, agricultural advisory services providers, government institutions, civil society, NGOs, academia, and private sector entities. All these will facilitate flows of information and access to key decision-makers. 7. CCARDESA also has experience working with a range of development partners, including but not limited to the World Bank. It has worked with the EU, IFAD, USAID, Swiss Development Cooperation, the German Government through the German Agency for International Cooperation ( Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit), and others. These have provided support to regional projects before and CCARDESA is familiar with their requirements. For example, CCARDESA is currently implementing the APPSA supported by the World Bank, the CAADP-XP4 project and the GCCA+ project both supported by the EU, and the C-NRM project supported by German Government. CCARDESA has received and managed grants from these partners and successfully provided sub-grants to partners for similar regional projects. Thus, CCARDESA is accustomed to sustainable FM. II. PROJECT DESCRIPTION Project Development Objective 8. The PDO of phase 1 of the Program is to increase the resilience of food systems and preparedness for food insecurity in project areas. Project Results Indicators 9. Progress toward the MPA PDO will be measured by three indicators. Table A4.1 presents these indicators and their targets. Table A4.1. Results Framework for CCARDESA Indicator Baseline End target Regionally harmonized policy frameworks and legislation facilitated by RECs through the 0 3 program (number) Strengthening or establishing regional centers of leadership (RCoL) in participating countries 0 1 (number) Technical areas for which a regional knowledge sharing mechanism is put in place (number) 0 3 Project Components 10. In line with the overall MPA, CCARDESA’s interventions will include short-term response activities designed to respond to the unfolding food security crisis, as well as activities with a medium- and longer-term orientation. To support the MPA, CCARDESA will regionally coordinate and facilitate activities planned under the MPA’s five pillars. Page 129 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Component 1: (Re-)Building Resilient Agricultural Production Capacity 11. CCARDESA will contribute to building resilient agricultural production capacity by technically backstopping and coordinating the development of climate and other information systems and developing joint research initiatives at the regional level. CCARDESA will provide technical assistance, organize knowledge management and communication on innovations and technologies, and organize capacity-building and training programs. Subcomponent 1.1: Developing National or Regional Agricultural Information Systems 12. CCARDESA will promote regional information and knowledge systems for adaptation planning and resilience building. It will do so by: (a) strengthening capacities to implement early warning systems that provide timely climate data and support the preparation and dissemination of information to farmers and (b) establishing SADC-level DCAS. The DCAS will provide information and recommendations on crop cultivars, soil preparation, sowing, fertilization, the timing of harvest, product storage, ways of reducing post-harvest losses, and other information that farmers can use in decision-making. Agricultural information and advisory systems will enhance producers’ and other decision-makers’ preparedness by equipping them to adapt not only to seasonal variability in weather, but also to longer-term trends in climate change. Subcomponent 1.2: Developing and Delivering Agricultural Technologies and Services 13. Specifically, CCARDESA will help strengthen existing regional research centers and establish new national and regional research centers. For this, it will: (a) support the upgrading of existing national centers through institutional linkages with regional and international networks, and academic and research institutions; (b) coordinate specific knowledge and expert exchange programs working with national and regional centers; (c) facilitate the establishment of RCoLs in participating countries, in partnership with IGAD; 80 (d) facilitate the adoption of a One Health approach to climate adaptation in Southern Africa by establishing a regional platform that convenes and coordinates animal, human, and environmental health services, enabling interagency coordination at the regional level, and disseminating knowledge products; and (e) facilitate capacity building on the effective management of transboundary pests and diseases, phytosanitary measures, and other resilience- enhancing technologies and management practices. 14. CCARDESA will also promote regional agricultural R&D among SADC member countries and collaborate with IGAD and other regional organizations. CCARDESA will support regional agricultural R&D by: (a) establishing a regional FFS curriculum that follows an experiential learning model for CSA involving the demonstration, practice, and application of learning materials on farmers’ own plots; (b) coordinating and facilitating regional research collaborations on topics relevant to food security and food systems resilience; and (c) facilitating the use of innovative approaches to extension and advisory service delivery in major value chains. 80Five RCoLs are already operating in Southern Africa. They act as a regional-scale agricultural innovation and R&D mechanism, facilitating joint research and its dissemination, and the cross-country exchange of knowledge, expertise, and climate-adapted varieties and technologies, thereby allowing regional specialization around priority farming systems and more strategic investment in agricultural research capacity. The five RCoLs were put in place under the APPSA (P164486) and include the Angola Cassava RCoL, Lesotho Horticulture RCoL, Malawi Maize RCoL, Mozambique Rice RCoL, and Zambia Legumes RCoL. Page 130 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Component 2: Supporting the Sustainable Development of Natural Resources for Resilient Agricultural Landscapes 15. CCARDESA will support and coordinate transnational and regional technical assistance, analytical and advisory work, and training efforts, especially on topics related to sustainable management of transboundary natural resources for climate resilience. Subcomponent 2.1: Identification and Validation of Interventions at the Local or Watershed Level 16. CCARDESA will: (a) support the capacity of AR4D institutions to conduct regional research on NRM and risk assessments; (b) facilitating studies on water and water resources management policies (through partners such as GMI), and the regional assessment of hydromet and weather advisory services, and the development of spatial analysis tools to improve the timely availability and quality of data for decision-making; and (c) supporting regional-scale multi-sectoral coordination and steering structures at different levels of governance and involving private and public sector stakeholders. The identification and validation of watershed interventions will be guided by climate considerations that provide adaptation and mitigation benefits. Subcomponent 2.2: Investments in Sustainable Natural Resources Management 17. CCARDESA will coordinate country and regional progress on CCBs by: (a) establishing a regional monitoring mechanism that provides harmonized, georeferenced data that can be used to monitor and assess the impacts of landscape-level decisions and actions and (b) organizing capacity building and climate change-related knowledge exchange programs among national AR4D institutions and other technical and development partners. Component 3: Getting to Market 18. To support the development of competitive agrifood value chains and well-functioning marketing infrastructure, CCARDESA will support SADC member countries in taking full advantage of the AfCFTA and other intraregional trade and value addition opportunities. Subcomponent 3.1: Developing Competitive Value Chains 19. CCARDESA will support this subcomponent through technical studies on value chain competitiveness and risk assessments. CCARDESA will: (a) support regionally significant agricultural value chain climate risk assessment and analyses; (b) support the assessment of market intelligence systems, including their responsiveness to the needs of smallholder farmers and other value chain actors; (c) coordinate regional studies on the costs of doing business; and (d) assess the status of market compliance and quality controls. 20. CCARDESA will also support the regional dissemination of value chain analyses and other knowledge products, while drawing attention to private investment opportunities. CCARDESA will implement activities jointly with FANR’s Industrial Development and Trade Directorate and the Infrastructure and Water Directorate (both within SADC). They will specifically (a) carry out an assessment, for Southern Africa, of epidemio- surveillance, trade border controls, seed certification, and pesticide quality controls and (b) establish the SADC Regional Agricultural Trade and Market Scorecard, to be fashioned after the EATM-Scorecard 81 in partnership with other regional organizations, as well as producer groups, famers’ unions, and other private sector entities. 81 TheECOWAS Agriculture Trade and Market Accountability Mechanism is being developed under the West Africa Food Systems Resilience Program MPA (P172769). The scorecard aims to assess implementation of policy commitments on agricultural trade and market access in the region by identifying country-level policy gaps and areas for improvement related to intra-regional agricultural and food trade. Page 131 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) Subcomponent 3.2.: Upgrading Marketing Infrastructure 21. This subcomponent will establish and upgrade agrifood marketing infrastructure, and at the regional level, CCARDESA will contribute through a study on regional market and trade connectivity infrastructure in Southern Africa. The study will examine (a) border-crossing posts and economic priority zones and their ease-of- trade, (b) new cross-country trade and economic corridors connected through climate-resilient road and rail infrastructure, (c) the strength of current border procedures, as well as ‘single-window’ and ‘multi-tier’ operations that facilitate or hinder the cross-border movement of goods and services, and (d) the extent of digital connectivity in trade facilitation services and areas for improvement to inform country policy reforms. Component 4: Promoting Food Systems Resilience in National and Regional Policymaking 22. Under this component, CCARDESA will support national government agencies and regional organizations in ways that pertain to high-level policies, initiatives, institutional arrangements, and even budgeting decisions that have cross-cutting relevance to food systems resilience. CCARDESA will support participating countries and other SADC member states in harmonizing country policies with international and regional cooperation frameworks, agricultural quality standards, and food safety protocols. CCARDSEA will also support the preparation of regional policies and regulations to increase regional flows of agricultural goods and inputs, and consolidate the regional food reserve system. In this way, the activity will increase awareness of the role of international trade in climate change adaptation. Subcomponent 4.1: Making Food Systems Resilience a Priority in Public Policies and Spending 23. CCARDESA will support country-level efforts to make food systems resilience a higher priority of public policies and spending in multiple ways. It will specifically (a) facilitate and coordinate policy reviews to assess entry points for building a resilience-focus into national planning mechanisms and policies; (b) develop, in collaboration with IGAD and other regional partners, a new-generation framework for carrying out agricultural PERs that offer insight into opportunities to better align public resources with resilience-informed policy priorities; (c) coordinate the harmonization of agricultural policies relating to seed production and the cross-border movement of germplasm and other genetic material, as well as other policies and agricultural expenditure tracking systems; and (d) contribute, in collaboration with SADC, IGAD, and other regional economic communities, to the review and modernization of regional food stock or reserve architecture. Subcomponent 4.2: Building the Capacity to Implement Resilience-Focused Policies 24. Under this subcomponent, CCARDESA will strengthen its own technical and program delivery capacities. CCARDESA will specifically build capacity to carry out policy and value chain analyses, needs assessments, and other analytical work on agricultural climate change adaptation and mitigation, notably with a focus on certain thematic areas such as seed production and regulatory systems, intellectual property rights, biosafety regulations, and similar topics. It will also acquire capacity to facilitate regional policy dialogue. Subcomponent 4.3: Supporting Regional Organizations to Build Food Systems Resilience Transnationally 25. Under this subcomponent, CCARDESA’s general capacity to implement the MPA will be strengthened. It will gain this capacity by (a) developing expertise on technical areas such as DATs, competitiveness, and marketing compliance strategies; (b) designing and implementing regional MISs and operationalizing the regional M&E system; (c) building institutional capacity for knowledge generation and dissemination and the use of advanced ICTs; and (d) facilitating high-level multi-country and regional trade dialogue. Page 132 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) III. IMPLEMENTATION ARRANGEMENTS 26. The overall implementation and oversight responsibility for the Program will be placed in CCARDESA’s management team. A dedicated PCU will be established under the direct reporting line of the CCARDESA Executive Director. The PCU will be based in Gaborone, Botswana within the CCARDESA Secretariat. The PCU will have the following staff: (a) project technical manager, (b) assistant procurement officer, (c) assistant finance and administrative officer, (d) assistant M&E officer, and (e) environment and social safeguards officer. 27. The PCU will be supported by existing units of CCARDESA including the finance, M&E, ICKM and procurement. These units will support the PCU as part of the institution’s ownership and hosting of the project. 28. The FSRP-PCU will be overseen by an RCC whose primary function is to provide policy and technical guidance to the implementation of the project. The RCC will be co-chaired by the Director of FANR (within SADC) and IGAD’s Director for the AED or their designates. The main objectives of a co-chaired RCC will be to: (a) ensure close coordination among subregional organizations involved in MPA implementation, including ones covering the HoA, Eastern Africa, and Southern Africa; (b) act as the main mechanism for interregional knowledge exchange and planning; and (c) establish joint mechanisms for monitoring, knowledge generation and knowledge sharing, and reporting and record keeping for all countries and partners involved in the MPA from Phase 1 onward. 29. The RCC will invite other regional and development partners as observers or prospective collaborators and partners of the MPA. Regional partners under consideration include the AUC, SADC, COMESA, the EAC, ASARECA, ILRI, farmers’ organizations, and the private sector. Detailed TORs will be developed as part of the PIM. 30. To avoid delays in implementation, the project technical manager will be recruited within three months of the grant becoming effective. Their technical manager’s recruitment will be followed by that of the assistant procurement officer, the assistant finance and administrative officer, the assistant M&E officer, and the environmental and social safeguards officer. CCARDESA will support participating countries in the management of the project, including by facilitating linkages among participating countries for joint learning and partnership building. It will also promote the MPA’s expansion across Southern Africa by raising awareness of the Program among its members, and helping to disseminate Program achievements. 31. CCARDESA has experience managing World Bank-funded projects in Southern Africa and has the capacity to manage fiduciary arrangements, including procurement and FM for the MPA. FM will be handled by the existing FM team, which comprises three qualified chartered accountants: a financial manager, financial officer, and assistant financial officer. Procurement activities will be managed by the existing procurement specialist at the CCARDESA Secretariat. CCARDESA will work closely with country PIUs to coordinate and implement regional activities of the Program. Regular consultative meetings between country PIUs and CCARDESA will be organized.
