Page 134 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) ANNEX 5: Food Systems Resilience: A Regional Challenge and Opportunity 1. The depth and breadth of food insecurity affecting the region calls for a regional approach. Many of the root causes—and consequences—of food insecurity defy national borders, suggesting that its drivers can and often need to be addressed cooperatively. Climate systems and extreme weather, watersheds and landscapes, foodsheds and food markets, pest and disease outbreaks, and conflict often spill over national boundaries. Also, in a region prone to hunger and acute food security crises precipitated by FCV, persistent malnutrition, and extreme weather events, a regional approach is the key to moving rapid response into a less reactive mode. 2. At the same time, the transboundary nature of many food system risks suggests that national investments can benefit more than one country. In that regard, data, knowledge, food surpluses, and institutional strengths generated nationally can also feed into regional research, learning, trade, and cooperation, and ultimately, food systems resilience. 3. Resilience will grow stronger when the full wealth of regional resources can readily flow to where they are needed. This is the case whether they are channeled through free and open markets or shepherded by coordinated efforts involving public and intergovernmental stakeholders. In a region characterized by strong disparities of public infrastructure, capacities, and resources, investments in shared data, research and innovation capacity, risk surveillance, marketing infrastructure, and other public goods can expand both access to them and their quality. 4. Food systems resilience will not be built in isolation but collectively, and in that respect, a regional program with activities at both the regional and national levels is fit for purpose. To some extent, the regionalization of efforts supporting food systems resilience is among the MPA’s aspirations, making the regional nature of the Program an essential element of its theory of change and design. Figure A5.1. Regional Outputs and Impacts of the MPA, Illustrated Page 135 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) ANNEX 6: Implementation Support Plan 1. The objective of implementation support is to ensure that the relevant regional and government agencies implement the Program properly. It is also to ensure that the resources and staff allocated by the World Bank are sufficient to supervise and support Program implementation. The plan basically aims at making the implementation support to the Borrower more flexible and efficient, and therefore focuses on the principal risks identified and the agreed risk mitigation measures to be undertaken as described in the risk section of the PAD (Section VI). It will consist of: (a) semi-annual implementation support missions carried out jointly by the World Bank, the participating countries, IGAD and CCARDESA as well as technical partners (such as CGIAR centers and the FAO) when technical needs arise, and (b) technical assistance in areas of weaknesses and where new approaches/procedures have been introduced. 2. Objective of implementation support mission. The implementation support and oversight missions will have the combined aim of reviewing the quality of implementation, providing solutions to implementation problems, and assessing the likelihood of achieving the PDO. More specifically, they will: (a) review implementation progress by pillar or component (including the level of implementation of recommendations made by former review missions), including institutional development aspects; (b) provide solutions to implementation problems as they arise; (c) review the action plan and disbursement programs with the national and regional PIU for the next six months; (d) review the Program’s fiduciary aspects, including disbursement and procurement; (e) verify compliance of Program activities with the fiduciary agreement and the World Bank’s environmental and social safeguard policies; (f) review case studies and survey results to ascertain results indicators and determine progress toward the PDO with regard to the targets set within the Results Framework, and assess the quality of implementation; and (g) review the quality of capacity-building activities, which are crucial for an effective implementation of the Program. The missions will combine some field visits whenever feasible; field-based focus group discussions and interactive workshops with stakeholders for feedback; they will also include regional workshops, as well as national workshops to highlight implementation issues, pick up emerging implementation lessons, and share mission recommendations, including agreements on actions moving forward. Reviews of quarterly/annual reports and various studies will also be undertaken. The supervision strategy will use a number of instruments to review progress and respond to implementation issues, including the following: (a) Implementation support missions. The World Bank task team will conduct joint semi-annual review and implementation support missions with country teams, IGAD, and CCARDESA to review overall MPA implementation performance and progress toward the achievement of the PDO. Support from technical partners, such as the CGIAR Centers, FAO, and others, will be sought when needed. The semi-annual implementation support missions will be followed by regional ‘wrap-up meetings’ that will bring together national teams, regional technical and research institutions, regional value chains stakeholders and key regional partners to discuss progress made and serve as a platform for sharing knowledge and building partnerships. The first implementation support mission will be fielded as soon as possible after Program effectiveness to provide start-up support through direct and timely feedback on the quality of implementation plans. (b) MTR. An MTR will be carried out midway in the implementation phase. It will include a comprehensive assessment of the progress in achieving Program objectives as laid out in the Results Framework. The MTR will also serve as a platform for revisiting design issues that may require adjustments to ensure satisfactory achievement of the Program’s objective. Page 136 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) (c) Other reviews. Each year, the World Bank and the line ministry in each country will consider the need for additional analytical, advisory, and knowledge sharing activities and/or third-party reviews. Such reviews will be planned for over and above the semi-annual implementation support missions. (d) Implementation completion. At the close of the Program, each Government, IGAD, CCARDESA, and the World Bank will carry out separate implementation completion reviews to assess the success of the Program and draw lessons from its implementation. (e) MPA task team set up. Arrangements made for the preparation phase will be maintained during implementation support, involving a regional TTL, as well as country based co-TTLs in MPA countries, and co-TTL from participating Global Practices (DRM), Water and Environment) to the extent possible. The regional TTL will be supported by one operations officer/analyst. This arrangement will enhance interaction with MPA countries and improve monitoring of progress. The World Bank is also seeking a possible trust fund budget to strengthen supervision activities on top of the World Bank budget. (f) Technical assistance. Implementation support will include specialized technical support from the World Bank, IGAD, CCARDESA and possibly other bilateral/multilateral agencies for critical aspects of the Program, including proper FM/procurement and the monitoring of social and environmental safeguards. The objective of the technical assistance will be to help the Program teams internalize good practices and resolve implementation bottlenecks, as they are identified during missions. Technical assistance will include training workshops to develop core resource skills within implementing units and program teams, helping finalize manuals, and reviewing and advising on TOR for required studies and technical support missions. Available FAO-World Bank Collaborative Program budget could be considered for FAO participation to Program implementation support. Implementation Support Plan 3. Focus of support. The first two years of implementation will need technical support to put in place the specific tools required for activity planning and implementation; the focus will later change to more routine monitoring of progress, troubleshooting, and assessments based on the Results Framework. Country implementation support missions will be every six months, followed by regional wrap-up workshops to discuss and exchange views on progress, experiences, best practices and challenges for each country. A common rating process will be done at the end of each wrap-up mission. 4. Technical support. The implementation support missions will be complemented with regular short visits by individual specialists to follow up on specific thematic issues as needed. The team will also hire consultants to provide technical support to PIUs and IAs. Regional trainings will be provided by the World Bank on key thematic areas such as safeguards, procurement, M&E, gender, and MFD. In addition, the FAO Investment Center, IGAD, CCARDESA, and a number of consultants may be mobilized periodically to provide technical assistance to IAs in the form of hands-on training and mentoring. 5. M&E support. The World Bank M&E specialist and relevant consultants will provide technical support and organize regional training for the M&E team, composed of the M&E officers from the concerned regional organizations and PIUs. 6. Fiduciary support. Fiduciary teams based in each involved World Bank country office (procurement and FM specialists) will closely supervise the Program's fiduciary management. They will participate in the twice-yearly Page 137 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) country implementation support missions and facilitate capacity building for the Program’s fiduciary staff. At least once a year, the procurement staff will organize a post review of procurement activities. 7. During implementation support missions, the Program FM specialist, based in the country office, will: (a) review the FM systems, including capacity for continued adequacy; (b) evaluate the quality of the budgets and IAs’ adherence thereto; (c) review the cycle of transaction recording until the end of report generation; (d) evaluate the internal control environment, including the internal audit function; (e) review IFRs and/or annual financial statements; (f) follow-up on ageing of the advance to the designated account; (g) follow-up on both internal and external audit reports; and (h) periodically assess the program’s compliance with the FM manual as well as the Financing Agreement. 8. On procurement, the World Bank will provide implementation support to the Borrower through a combination of prior and post reviews, procurement training to Program staff and relevant IAs, and periodic assessment of the Program’s compliance with the procurement manual. Implementation support missions will be geared toward: (a) reviewing and updating procurement documents; (b) providing detailed guidance on the World Bank’s Procurement Regulation; and (c) monitoring procurement progress against the detailed Procurement Plan and agreed actions to mitigate procurement risks. Following the recommendations of the fiduciary assessments of the IAs, and in addition to the prior review supervision to be carried out from World Bank offices, the semiannual supervision missions will include field visits, of which at least one mission will involve post review of procurement actions. 9. Safeguards. The World Bank specialists in social and environmental safeguards will have responsibility for supervising safeguard activities. Each year, they will conduct supervision of the Program’s safeguard activities, participate in regional meetings to discuss findings, and draft action plans to improve implementation. 10. FM assessments have been carried out by the World Bank for Ethiopia, Madagascar, IGAD and CCARDESA to evaluate the adequacy of FM arrangements to support project implementation. The assessments focus on implementing entities at the regional and country levels. The objective of the assessments is to determine whether the proposed FM arrangements (a) are capable of correctly and completely recording all transactions and balances relating to the project; (b) would facilitate the preparation of regular, accurate, reliable, and timely financial statements; (c) would safeguard the project’s assets; and (d) would be subject to acceptable auditing arrangements. The assessments build as much as possible on the World Bank’s knowledge of country FM systems and requirements, and experience and performance of the implementing entities through its involvement in other World Bank-financed operations. This annex summarizes the key FM arrangements, risks and mitigating measures. Detailed assessments for each country were prepared, which are the basis for this summary. 2. Organization and staffing. The implementing entities for the project all have experience in managing World Bank-financed operations. The overall FM responsibility will lie with the MoA for Ethiopia, the MinAE for Madagascar, IGAD, and CCARDESA finance units for the regional integration activities. Each institute will use PCUs for existing projects which are staffed with qualified accountants. The finance manager at CCARDESA will be responsible for the project FM. Given the decentralized implementation arrangements, additional accountants are required for Ethiopia at regional agriculture bureaus and new woredas which are not covered under the existing AGP II. IGAD will deploy an accountant or finance officer to support FM responsibilities at the PIU. One financial officer and one accountant based at the central PIU in the MinAE and one financial assistant at each directory in the MinAE per RIU will be recruited for Madagascar. Required positions may be financed as part of the project costs as needed. 3. Planning and budgeting. Country and institution budgetary regulations in terms of budget formulation, execution and monitoring will be followed. The AWPB for the project will be prepared annually by the MOA, MinAE, IGAD and CCARDESA for their respective activities and will be reviewed by the World Bank for ‘no objection’. Once it is approved, the budget will form the basis for disbursements and expenditure under the project. All entities are required to maintain up-to-date budget tracking records, and budget versus actual expenditure variance analysis will be prepared quarterly as part of the IFRs and used as a management tool for decision-making.  Ethiopia. Budget preparation will be led by the MoA which will prepare consolidated annual budget using the Government’s budget preparation process for the project which will be approved by the SC, Ministry of Finance and the World Bank.  Madagascar. Budget will be prepared by the PIU at the MinAE and approved by the PSC and the World Bank.  IGAD. The budget will be prepared by the PCU and approved by the IGAD AED before submission to the World Bank for ‘no objection’.  CCARDESA. The budget will be prepared by the PIU and approved by the entity management and the World Bank. 4. Accounting systems, policies, and procedures. The main implementing entities have systems, policies and procedures in place which are used by existing projects. Existing projects’/institutions’ finance manual will be used for the project with some modification to account for project specifics. Page 140 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566)  Ethiopia. The Government’s accounting policies and procedures (modified cash basis of accounting) will be used for accounting of the project. The accounting centers for the project are the MoA, EIAR/RARIs, the Regional Bureaus of Agriculture and Natural Resource Development (RBoANRs), the Regional Bureaus of Water, Irrigation and Electricity, the Agricultural Transformation Institute, the Federal Cooperative Agency, and 177 woredas/towns in nine regional states. The Project will enter data in the Integrated Financial Management Information System at federal level where component and subcomponent specific data will be entered using other software such as the Integrated Budget and Expenditure (IBEX) or Peachtree at all levels.  Madagascar. The Government’s accounting policies and procedures (modified accrual basis of accounting) will be used for accounting of the project. The PIU will purchase and use an adequate accounting software to record project financial transactions, monitor the budget execution and prepare the financial reporting. The PIU at the MinAE and RIUs at each regional directory of the MinAE to support the project will form the project accounting centers. Component 1 of the project will be implemented through FOFIFA and FDA while Components 2 and 3 will be implemented by line ministries based on the MoU to be signed between the MinAE and these implementing entities.  IGAD. IGAD uses the SUN system which will facilitate recording of budgetary allocations and commitments and tracking of payments against the approved budget and enabling comparison of actual performance against the budget.  CCADESA. The project uses Sage Evolution accounting software for project FM and the production of accounts. The accounting software is capable of transaction processing, production of project annual financial statements, IFRs, and other reports as required for the effective management and monitoring of the project 5. Internal controls. Internal control comprises the whole system of control, financial or otherwise, established by management to (a) carry out project activities in an orderly and efficient manner, (b) ensure adherence to policies and procedures, (c) ensure maintenance of complete and accurate accounting records, and (d) safeguard the project’s assets.  Ethiopia. The Government internal control procedures are incorporated in the financial administration proclamation, regulation, directives, and various manuals ensuring that budget institutions have the responsibility to maintain and uphold sound internal control systems of the entities. However, internal control weaknesses are noted in terms of budget monitoring, cash management, accounting and payment controls, advances/payables monitoring, assets managements, and follow-up and implementation of previous audit recommendations. The entity wide issues are monitored by other World Bank-financed PforR operations. The magnitude of such findings in projects is limited and there is reasonable follow-up to rectify the irregularities. Through the FM manual, to be updated from AGP II FM manual, provision of training and close supervision of woredas by regions, these risks will be mitigated.  Madagascar. The MinAE has an internal control procedures manual through the existing projects it is implementing. A specific procedures manual will be developed for the FSRP implementation. The PIM will be based on the existing procedures including budgeting process, inventories and assets management, and payment authorization process and will consider specificities in the project as well as the World Bank requirements including the authorization process for payments as well as the subsequent controls to be undertaken, accounting, assets, and inventories management systems. Page 141 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566)  IGAD. Project payments will be processed in line with the established internal control framework in IGAD. This includes procedures for approval and authorization of payments, adequate segregation of functions in payment processing, and a requirement for all payments to be supported by adequate supporting documentation. The project accountant will prepare monthly bank reconciliation reports to confirm accuracy of cash and bank balances. Challenges in coordinating soft activities (trainings and workshops) across various countries and the risk of unsupported payments which could result in ineligible expenditure and damages or wastes to project assets are perceived as internal control risks at IGAD. These are mitigated by conducting risk-based internal audit ensuring payments are properly supported by relevant documentary evidence and putting in place measures to safeguard project assets including annual physical count.  CCARDESA. The project will use the entity’s financial policies and procedures which are assessed adequate to manage the project. 6. Internal audit. The project will be subject to internal audit reviews for each country and institution.  Ethiopia. The assessed entities have internal audit function responsible for the review and internal control oversight of the entity’s resources. However, their oversight over projects is limited due to limited staff and capacity gap. The project will provide training to internal auditors on the FM aspects of the project and, allocates budget for this and supervision costs that may be required by the unit to carry out the task. The unit will also work with BoANRDs audit units to share the burden and share findings on the Project. Moreover, the project will have dedicated internal auditor, with adequate experience and competence recruited from the project to be based at the FPCU and support the project internal audit work liaising with the regular internal auditors at all levels.  Madagascar. The Internal Audit unit will periodically review the adequacy of the internal control and the compliance. An Internal auditor (consultant) will be recruited within the MinAE PIU, based on TORs agreed with the World Bank, to continuously review the governance, risk management and control over the project’s activities.  IGAD. The institution internal audit will provide a risk-based internal audit oversight support to the project.  CCARDESA. Due to the size of the project, it is not envisaged to have internal audit arrangements for the project. The project will rely on the external auditors and the World Bank’s supervision for assurance that funds are being used for the intended purposes 7. Financial reporting. Each IA will prepare quarterly unaudited IFRs and annual project audited financial statements. IFRs will be submitted to the World Bank within deadlines set out in each project participating recipient’s DFIL. The financial reports will be designed to provide quality and timely information to the project management. The contents of the IFR will include narration report, cash forecast statement, statement of sources and uses of funds, statement of uses of funds by project activity/component, expenditure documentation sheet, DA activity statements, trial balances, and other related schedules. The format and content of the IFR for each country were prepared and agreed during negotiation. The project will also prepare the project annual accounts/financial statements within three months after the end of the accounting year in accordance with accounting standards acceptable to the World Bank and submit to the project’s external auditors. 8. External audit. In accordance with World Bank policy, implementing entities are required to submit annual project financial statements audited in accordance with International Standards of Auditing (ISAs), by an Page 142 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) acceptable external auditor and following TORs approved by the World Bank. The audit reports for all implementers will be submitted to the World Bank within six months of the year-end along with management letters. In accordance with the World Bank’s policies, the audited financial statements will be disclosed to the public. Fund Flow and Disbursement Arrangements 9. The detailed fund flow arrangement for each of the countries and the regional implementers will be documented in each of the implementers DFIL. The proposed arrangement is described below. 10. The MoA, the MinAE, IGAD, and CCARDESA will each open a DA at banks acceptable to IDA. The project may follow one or a combination of the following disbursement methods: DA, direct payment, reimbursement and special commitment. Detailed arrangements are summarized as follows:  Ethiopia. The DA account will be opened in the name of the MoA at the National Bank of Ethiopia. Given the decentralized nature of the Project, local currency accounts will also be opened at the national and sub national levels. The authorized ceiling of the DA for Ethiopia will be two quarters of forecasted expenditure based on the approved AWPB. Fund from the US dollar designated account will be further transferred into local currency (birr) account to be held by the MoA. From the birr account, the MoA will transfer the funds to separate local currency bank accounts to be opened by other participating federal entities and RBoANRs, and then to local currency bank accounts.  Madagascar. The PIU will use the DA denominated in US dollars at the BFM, to be opened for the PPA, to receive the project funds from the World Bank. Transaction-based disbursements will be used for this project. An initial advance of up to the ceiling of the DA and representing four months of forecasted project expenditures payable through the DA will be transferred after credit effectiveness and subsequent disbursements will be made monthly against submission of the Statement of Expenditures (SOE). The secondary accounts, denominated in Malagasy ariary or US dollars, to be opened during the preparation by the PIU at the commercial bank will enable payment of eligible expenditures.  IGAD. The project disbursement will follow the SOE method. A DA will be opened in the name of IGAD where the proceeds of the IDA grant will be deposited. Upon effectiveness, the project will submit a withdrawal application accompanied by a six-month cash forecast to withdraw the initial advance. Subsequent withdrawal of funds will be based on submission of withdrawal application to the World Bank together with an SOE incurred.  CCARDESA. The entity will be required to open a DA designated in US dollars at the Central Bank to receive the funds from the World Bank. Upon effectiveness of the financing agreement and submission of a withdrawal application, the World Bank will disburse an initial amount equivalent to six months expenditure into the DA. Subsequent disbursements will be made on the basis of withdrawal applications and unaudited IFRs. Specific Considerations 11. The following specific issues are noted for Ethiopia which require follow up. 12. Implementation overlap period. For Ethiopia, the existing AGP II will overlap with the FSRP for about one year. Given the similar activities to be financed under the two operations, it is important to manage the fund flow and reporting to avoid any double dipping. In this regard, until AGP II is closed, FSRP resources will only flow to Page 143 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) new woredas/towns which are about 121. To ensure this and mitigate the double dipping risk, (a) the project will have a detailed AWPB that will be carefully scrutinized by the task team before approval, (b) the IFRs will have a section to show the detail of transfers and expenditure and balances of AGP II accounts, (c) project progress reports will have an FM section that reports on progress of AGP II at overlapping woredas’ and the woredas readiness to implement the FSRP, and (d) FM monitoring will target review of transactions to check expenditure double dipping. In addition, bank accounts will not be opened for the project (FSRP) at the 56 overlapping woredas until AGP II bank accounts are closed. At federal and regional levels only, activities which are not covered under AGP II will be financed under FSRP until AGP II is fully closed. 13. Conflict-affected areas. Selected activities in conflict areas with difficult access will be done through third- party implementation over an initial period of 12-18 months. Before the end of that initial period, assessments will be carried out to determine whether implementation can be transferred to government structures. The GoE has selected the FAO for this effort and the contracting of the UN agency will be a condition for the project’s effectiveness for which the MOA will sign a standard output agreement. An FM assessment of the Government’s capacity to supervise the activities to be carried out by the UN agency, on its behalf, will be conducted, as necessary, with the objective of determining the risk and assurance provided by the proposed FM arrangements. The assessment should take into account the design of the operation, the country context as it relates to the UN agencies’ arrangements in the field, and any relevant additional information. Progress in these areas will also be monitored during implementation through the TPMA, including for the project fiduciary management arrangement. The TPMA will be recruited by the World Bank, subject to availability of trust fund resources. The project will use all modalities of supervision available for the project including GEMS, updated audit TOR with focus on risky areas, and third party monitoring to be used under the project. Fraud and Corruption 14. All implementing entities are expected to adhere to the World Bank Anti-Corruption Guidelines as outlined under the World Bank’s policy and procedure for IPF operations. The possibility of circumventing the internal control system with colluding practices such as bribes, abuse of administrative positions, and misprocurement is a critical issue and may include (a) late submission of supporting documents, (b) poor filing and records, (c) lack of system integration, (d) lack of budget discipline, and (e) unauthorized commitment to suppliers, bypassing budget and expenses vetting procedure; among others. These are mitigated as follows: (a) inclusion of specific aspects on corruption ISA 240 in the audit TOR; (b) FM procedures (as part of the FM manual) approved and in operation for the Program (c) strong FM arrangements (including qualified project accountants in the implementing entities, (d) periodic IFRs including budget execution and monitoring, and (e) separate accounts, internal audit, and external audit reviews. 15. FM risk and mitigating measures. The overall FM risk of the Program is substantial. The Program will inherit the various strengths of countries’ public FM system and World Bank-financed projects. However, the gaps identified during the assessment must be mitigated. Table A7.1 discusses the risks and mitigating measures per country or regional institution. Page 150 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) ANNEX 8: Procurement Arrangements 1. Applicable procurement procedures. Program procurement will be guided by and in accordance with the World Bank’s Procurement Regulations and ’Guidelines on Preventing and Combatting Fraud and Corruption’ and provisions stipulated in the relevant Financing Agreements. During their implementation, FSRP projects will elaborate a procedural manual based on and in accordance with the above-mentioned World Bank references . 2. Procurement documents. The World Bank’s latest SPDs will be used for procurement of goods, works, and non-consulting services while approaching the international market. The SPDs are available on the World Bank external website: www.worldbank.org/procurement/standarddocuments. For procurements from the national market, the borrower country may use the respective national procurement procedure, as agreed in the Procurement Plan, subject to addressing the conditions stated in the Procurement Regulations paragraphs 5.3 and 5.4 (a to i). 3. Use of national procedure. The respective borrower country’s procedure is accepted for procurement from the national market provided that the requirements for use of the national procedure specified in the Procurement Regulation paragraph 5.4 are met. Other national procurement arrangements (other than national open competitive procedure) that may be applied by the borrower (such as limited/restricted competitive bidding, request for quotation (RFQ)/shopping, and direct contracting), shall be consistent with the World Bank’s core procurement principles and ensure that the World Bank’s Anticorruption Guidelines and Sanctions Framework and contractual remedies set out in its Legal Agreement apply. In addition to the requirements in the regulation, the ‘national’ and ’other national procurement arrangements’ shall be applied provided that the respective procurement risk mitigation measures, are implemented. The risk mitigation measures will be updated during the project implementation period. 4. PPSD. The Implementing Agencies in the respective countries/Institutions prepared PPSDs that were reviewed and cleared by the World Bank. The summary of the PPSDs is presented in table 8.3 establishing the procurement arrangement and market approach options for high-or substantial-risk contracts. The PPSDs will be updated at least annually, or as required, to reflect changes in the respective procurement arrangements necessitated by changes in requirements, market conditions, and the procurement environment. 5. STEP. The IAs in each of the countries will plan and process procurement using STEP which is an online procurement planning, implementation, monitoring and reporting system. The procurement specialist assigned in each of the IAs shall be provided with the necessary facilities and training on how to work in the STEP system. 6. The following section provides the procurement arrangement description and summary of the capacity assessment carried out in each of the countries implementing the Program. The procurement capacity of the IAs was assessed using the World Bank’s PRAMS to determine the project risks, risk mitigation measures, responsibilities, timing and monitoring process, and procurement performance monitoring during implementation. Ethiopia 7. Procurement implementation arrangements. Procurement activities will be implemented using the existing government structure at the federal, regional, and local levels. The MoA will be the focal IA responsible for the overall coordination and reporting of the procurement activities in the project. Besides, the MoA supported by a PIU will carry out strategic procurements centrally which includes high-value consolidated procurements (like Page 151 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) vehicles and IT facilities), major consultancy assignments, and technical assistance by approaching national or international markets. Likewise, in regional states82 implementing the project, the Bureau of Agriculture (BoA) and the PIUs established under the BoA will be responsible for coordinating and leading procurement implementation in each of the respective regions. At the woreda level, the Bureau of Finance (BoF) will be responsible for carrying out procurement activities in the respective woredas. In addition, the federal Ethiopian Agriculture Research Institute, the Regional Agriculture Research Institutes (RARIs), the Irrigation and Lowland Development Bureaus (ILLDBs), or equivalents will carry out their own procurement activities using existing structure as enhanced through capacity enhancement measures. Assessment is ongoing to verify the establishment/reestablishment of basic procurement capacity in the newly created Southwest regional state, in conflict-affected woredas in Amhara, Oromia and Benshangul Gumuz regional states, and in the newly established ILLDBs (or equivalents) in each of the regional states implementing the project. 8. Capacity assessment. The following paragraphs summarize the findings of the procurement capacity assessment in Ethiopia. (a) Procurement regulatory framework and management capability. At the federal level, public procurement is governed by the Public Procurement and Property Administration Proclamation (No. 649/2009) and the Procurement Directive issued in June 2010 and amended in December 2015. Except for the newly established Sidama and Southwest regional state that have adopted SNNPR’s documents, the regional states issued their own procurement proclamations and directives which are applicable in procurements under their respective regions. Among other provisions, the proclamations stipulate the organization responsible for procurement, the key procurement principles and operational procedures, and the complaint handling system. The assessment verified that basic procurement structures including staffing are established in the federal MoA, EARI,BoA and ILLDBs and in the finance office at the woreda level. However, to carry out the project procurement responsibilities successfully in compliance to the agreed procedures, the MoA and regional BoAs should be supported by PIUs staffed with qualified procurement experts. To accommodate the additional workload associated with this project, the existing procurement capacity in ILLDBs, each of the woreda finance offices and research institutes should also be enhanced by hiring at least one additional dedicated procurement expert. (b) Procurement integrity and oversight. Each of the IAs has established and maintained internal audit function that carries out regular audit and report to the management and Ministry of Finance or BoF (as appropriate). However, in general, the internal audit is not adequately staffed and steered to carry out audit in donor-financed projects. Likewise, the external audit mechanism is established at the federal and regional levels as an independent body accountable to the highest law-making authority in the federal and regional governments. In addition, the procurement regulatory authorities (at the federal and regional levels) monitor and regulate procurement implementation in the respective regional states and city administrations. However, the capacity and structure of the regulatory authorities are limited to provide enforceable and independent audit recommendations. In particular, some of the regional states like Oromia and Amhara have established the procurement regulatory bodies under the BoF which has further undermined their structure and capacity to deliver their responsibilities independently. Procurement complaints at the federal and regional levels are managed in three-tier hierarchical structures at the procuring entity level, that is, a complaint handling board (CHB) which is a quasi-judicial body established with representatives from Oromia, Amhara, Tigray, SNNPR, Sidama, Benishangul Gumuz, Gambella, Harari and the newly established Southwest regional state as 82 well as Dire Dawa City Administration and Addis Ababa. Page 152 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) the private sector and formal court. However, in some of the regional states like Oromia and Amhara, the CHB has not been established. Instead, the BoF reviews and decides on procurement complaints that have not been resolved at the procuring entity level undermining the independence and integrity of the complaint system. At the woreda level, the complaint system is not accessible to all bidders. Aggrieved bidders that are not satisfied with the response from the procuring entity are required to travel to the regional capital to submit appeal to the CHB/or BoF, as appropriate. (c) Procurement process. Open bidding is the default procurement method for which notices are published in the national newspaper (Ethiopian Herald). The associated bidding documents are prepared using the Standard Bidding Documents (SBDs) issued by the Public Procurement and Property Administration Agency and contains all the necessary sections including standard conditions of contracts that allow appropriate allocation of responsibilities, risks and liabilities among contracting parties. However, the SBDs and the resulting bidding documents lack provisions as prescribed in the Procurement Regulation paragraph 5.4 (c) and (d) related to the World Bank’s Anti-Corruption Guideline and ESHS risks and impacts. To address the inconsistency, the World Bank recently issued a modified SPD applicable to works procurement from the national market, which will be used in this project. Some of the regional states issued provisions that require IAs to award government contracts to jobless youth (fresh graduates) organized as ’micro and small enterprises’ through preferential treatments, that is, ’set aside’ and ’mandatory subcontracting’. The practice denies market access to contractors of similar capacity and exposes the client to the risk of awarding contracts to inexperienced contractors. Regarding evaluation of bids, the client staff require training in the use and application of appropriate post-qualification criteria and how to apply professional judgment to avoid losing competitive bids with minor deviations. The other area that needs improvement is contract management which is carried out without adequate structure and staffing in almost all agencies covered in the assessment. In terms of transparency, there are no practices of publishing procurement award information in accessible media. Besides, the performance in the use of STEP has not been satisfactory in preceding projects like AGP II, which should be improved through trainings and by establishing accountability mechanisms. (d) Procurement complexity. None of the envisaged procurement activities require unique arrangement, procurement documents method, or approach. The procurement activities in the MoA are primarily standard goods like IT equipment and vehicles that will be consolidated and procured centrally by approaching the international market using standard procedures. However, given that the MoA has no experience of contracting out project activities to third-party implementers, it should enhance its capacity to successfully manage the contract. (e) Market readiness. The majority of the procurement activities are standard items that are fairly available in the local market. However, the risk of shortage of construction basic inputs (cement and reinforcement bar) and the associated price hike endanger successful completion of procurement activities and contracts associated with the construction of SSI schemes and other works contracts. The long queue to access foreign currency, is one possible hurdle affecting the responsiveness of the market. The market in a fragile environment may suffer from limited interest from international contractors/consultants or high-price bids/proposals because of country security risk, which may lead to low response to published tenders. A flexible approach will be taken to address the restrictions due to the ongoing conflict, COVID-19, and impact of fragility in the procurement environment. Page 153 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) (f) Procurement risk. Based on the above assessments and findings, the overall procurement capacity and risk is assessed as Substantial. Table 8.1 presents mitigation measures. Madagascar 9. Implementation arrangement. In Madagascar, procurement activities will be implemented through the procurement unit of the PADAP. The unit will be supported by a dedicated PIU staffed with qualified procurement officers. The PADAP has limited experience with the World Bank’s new procurement framework but long experience in managing the old guidelines. The project will address any procurement capacity gaps by training the PIU’s procurement officers, who have already been involved in the implementation of the similar preceding project. The World Bank procurement team will continue to provide coaching and continuous hands-on support to the PIU. 10. Capacity assessment: The following paragraphs summarize the findings of the procurement capacity assessment in Madagascar. 11. Procurement regulatory framework and management capability: The project has just moved from the old guidelines to the Procurement Regulations. It has very strong experience in managing World Bank-financed projects for more than five years. The procurement team has a strong background in World Bank procedure, and it needs specific support for this transition. 12. Integrity and oversight. The agency has an effective complaints management system known to the bidding community to review and resolve complaints/disputes at appropriate stages of the procurement cycle. In that way, a toll-free number is available for the public. 13. Procurement process. The agency has a good track record of managing ESHS risks including preventing sexual exploitation, rape, and assault throughout the procurement process. When implementing activities, clear procurement documents proportionate to the need are used to encourage broad participation from potential competitors. Procedures for bid submission, receipt, and opening are clearly described in the procurement documents and complied with. Bid evaluation is based on the criteria stated in the procurement documents to award the contract. However, all these were based on old guidelines. 14. Market readiness. The target market has a good history of delivery. The target market views the purchaser as attractive. There are no restrictions to open competition in the market. However, the target market recently had some issue related to supply chain and/or contract management. 15. Procurement complexity. Procurement will be centralized within a few IAs. Procurement does not have complex arrangements requiring specialized skills, for example, dialogue-based arrangements that present probity risk (for example, competitive dialogue, negotiations, PPP, or Best and Final Offer). The delivery/contract implementation-related risks are largely within the supplier’s/contractor’s control. 16. Procurement risk rating. The PADAP PIU will need strengthening of its procurement and contract management capacity to handle the additional workload. The procurement committee needs to be established and operationalized, and the procurement complaints review process properly documented in the procurement manual. Based on these findings, the overall procurement capacity and risk is assessed as Substantial . Page 154 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) IGAD 17. Procurement implementation arrangements . Procurement activities at IGAD will be implemented through the procurement unit established and staffed with procurement professionals who have the responsibility for coordinating the procurement function. The unit will be supported by a dedicated PIU staffed with qualified procurement experts. 18. Capacity assessment. The following paragraphs summarize the findings of the procurement capacity assessment at IGAD carried out in accordance with the format provided in the PRAMS. 19. Procurement regulatory framework and management capability . Procurement at IGAD is governed by the Procurement Manual revised edition of April 2021 which is issued under the authority of IGAD and supersedes the IGAD Procurement and Grants Manual (First Edition) which was in use from July 2012 and revised on March 31, 2014. The Procurement Manual establishes the procurement policies and provides further guidance to effectively undertake procurement activities in compliance with the Financial Rules and Regulations and other applicable legal instruments and documents and is issued according to Articles 3, 5, 10, and 13 of the General Conditions of the Agreement Establishing IGAD. The assessment verified that IGAD has established procurement structures including staffing with experienced and qualified staff who have experience in management of donor- funded projects including World Bank projects. However, it is essential that the procurement activities in the project are implemented by a dedicated unit staffed with qualified procurement expert. In addition, IGAD should enhance its contract management capacity to effectively manage the various contracts that may be generated related to procurement of goods and consultancy services. 20. Procurement integrity and oversight. IGAD has an established internal audit function unit which is well staffed and annually submits its audit findings and management responses are adequately provided. Other than the internal audit unit, external audit firms are engaged on an annual basis to provide independent opinion besides the audits carried out by the donors such as the post procurement reviews (PPRs). The Procurement Manual has instructions on the code of ethics expected from the parties involved in the procurement processes. In addition, the manual has provisions on handling procurement complaints through the Tender Committee and Procurement Appeals Review Committee. IGAD also has a debarment system to bidders engaged in fraudulent or corrupt practices, but there is no agreed cross-debarment with the World Bank. 21. Procurement process. Procurement opportunities are widely advertised on the IGAD website and appears to attract a reasonable number of bidders. IGAD has not yet developed SPDs and hence, bidding documents are normally prepared using the SPDs provided by donors which includes all standard sections. However, most of the procurements were for goods of small value processed through the RFQ method, which requires little experience in preparing bidding documents, conducting evaluation, and managing contracts relevant to high-value complex procurements. Regarding transparency, the IGAD Procurement Manual requires that all contract awards for procurement US$50,000 and above are published on its website (http://www.igad) in addition to posting all the procurement processes through STEP. However, training has to be provided in the use of the STEP system for procurement monitoring. 22. Procurement complexity. The expected procurements under the project are less complex as they will mainly be a few goods for office use and operations and assorted consultancies whose selection would be through Quality and Costs-Based Selection (QCBS) and Selection Based on the Consultants’ Qualifications (CQS) to be undertaken at the PIU level which the IA will be able to handle. Page 155 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 23. Market readiness. There is no anecdotal evidence to suggest that market access is restricted as the market mechanisms do not appear to be a constraint to active private sector participation. There are sufficient suppliers and consultants actively bidding and working on development projects to ensure prices are competitive based on the data on the previous projects. 24. Procurement risk: Based on the above assessments and findings, the overall procurement capacity and risk is assessed as Moderate. Mitigation measures are in the table 8.1. CCARDESA 25. At the regional level, regional activities will be implemented by the regional technical organizations CCARDESA and SADC’s FANR with each participating regional organization having its own Financing Agreement. CCARDESA, being a subsidiary of SADC and neither a national government department nor a state-owned entity, follows its own procurement policies and procedures. The policy generally meets the WBG core procurement policy objectives of value for money, economy, efficiency, effectiveness, integrity, transparency, fairness, and accountability. 26. Procurement regulatory framework and management capability. CCRADESA has documented procurement policy and procedures, but they are not publicly available. The entities produce Procurement Plans while preparing the annual budget. CCARDESA has a procurement support unit manned by one procurement expert hired under the APPSA, who has experience in World Bank funded projects. Although hired under the APPSA, the expert is responsible for all procurement activities of CCARDESA including other donor-funded projects and internal organizational procurements. The TOR of the expert would need to be expanded to cover the new project. There is limited capacity and availability of technical leads internally to manage the contracts of the various sector studies which places additional workload on the procurement unit. To coordinate the procurement activities taking place under the various projects and support contract management, CCARDESA would need to hire additional qualified procurement staff to augment the capacity of the procurement unit. 27. Integrity and oversight. CCRADESA does have access to the services of internal auditors and external auditors. CCARDESA has been requested to share copies of the latest internal audit reports and any governance- related reports to enable review of findings, if any, related to procurement and commitment of management to resolve these. There is no established and documented procurement-related complaints review mechanism in place to enable interested parties to seek redress on procurement decisions. The lack of a complaints review procedure, known to the market, undermines the transparency and integrity of the procurement system. The CCARDESA procurement manual provides for the establishment of a Procurement Committee, comprising the Executive Director as chairperson, and Procurement Officer as secretary, with four other members appointed by the Board of Directors which shall be the highest approval authority for CCARDESA procurement and shall be the approval authority responsible for providing prior authorization and guidance for any steps in the procurement process above US$50,000. This committee has not been established. There is a risk of staff authorizing procurements above their authority, that is, above US$50,000. 28. Procurement process. The procurement process is documented in the procurement manual and CCARDESA uses the procurement documents from donor partners to guide the procurement process. The default method of procurement is open competition. Procurement opportunities, evaluation outcomes, and award of contracts are published on the CCRADESA website and World Bank external website. All procurements will require compliance to the WBG Anti-Corruption Guidelines and include the World Bank’s right to audit and inspection. Page 156 of 161 The World Bank Food Systems Resilience Program for Eastern and Southern Africa (P178566) 29. Market readiness. Generally, consultants and vendors are available in the regional and international markets for the sector studies envisaged under this project. Flexibilities in procurement approaches, such as allowing submission of bids/proposals by electronic means, may need to be applied to address the restrictions due to COVID-19 pandemic. 30. Procurement complexity. Procurement will comprise selection of consultant firms and individuals as well as procurement of low-value goods. Experience from the APPSA shows capability to manage similar procurements and acceptable documentation in place. However, there is a need to augment contract management capacity. 31. Procurement risk rating. Based on the above assessments and findings, the overall procurement risk for CCARDESA is assessed as Substantial. 32. Overall procurement risk and mitigation measures. Except for IGAD where procurement residual risk is Moderate, the procurement risk in the other IAs is Substantial. Thus, the overall procurement residual risk of the project is considered Substantial. Table A8.1 provides risk and risk mitigation measures by IAs. Table A8.1. Procurement oversight and monitoring arrangements. The World Bank exercises its procurement oversight through a risk-based approach comprising prior and post reviews as appropriate. The World Bank sets mandatory thresholds for prior review based on the procurement activity risk rating, as determined in the Procurement Plan that will be agreed with the World Bank throughout project implementation. The prior review threshold applicable in the project for the different risk levels is provided in the table A8.2. Table A8.2. Procurement Prior Review Thresholds (US$ millions) Type of Procurement High Risk Substantial Risk Moderate Risk Low Risk Works 5.0 10.0 15.0 20.0 Goods, Its, and non-consulting services 1.5 2.0 4.0 6.0 Consultants (firms) 0.5 1.0 2.0 4.0 Individual consultants 0.2 0.3 0.4 0.5 34. The World Bank shall carry out post-reviews of procurement processes undertaken by the borrower to determine whether procurement implementation complies with the requirements of the Legal Agreement. The World Bank may use a third party such as a supreme audit institution, acceptable to the World Bank, to carry out post reviews. Any such third party shall carry out the reviews in accordance with the TOR provided to it by the World Bank. The procurement reviews will be carried out using the online PPR system established by the World Bank, and the clients in the respective countries are urged to ensure that procurement activities are processed through the STEP system and procurement information and documents are uploaded timely. 35. All contracts at or above the mandatory procurement prior review thresholds are subject to international advertising and the use of the World Bank’s SBDs (or other documents agreed with the World Bank). 36.
